Wavefront sets of unipotent representations of reductive p p -adic groups II
Dan Ciubotaru, Lucas Mason-Brown, Emile Okada

Abstract
. The wavefront set is a fundamental invariant of an admissible representation arising from the Harish-Chandra-Howe local character expansion. In this paper, we give a precise formula for the wavefront set of an irreducible representation of real infinitesimal character in Lusztig’s category of unipotent representations in terms of the Deligne-Langlands-Lusztig correspondence. Our formula generalizes the main result of [ CMO21 ] , where this formula was obtained in the Iwahori-spherical case. We deduce that for any irreducible unipotent representation with real infinitesimal character, the algebraic wavefront set is a singleton, verifying a conjecture of Mœglin and Waldspurger. In the process, we establish new properties of the generalized Springer correspondence in relation to Lusztig’s families of unipotent representations of finite reductive groups.



1.
Introduction

Let
\mathsf{k}
be a nonarchimedean local field of characteristic
0
and residue field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
of sufficiently large characteristic, and let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive algebraic group defined over
{\mathsf{k}}
, inner to split. Fix an algebraic closure
\bar{\mathsf{k}}
of
\mathsf{k}
and let
K\subset\bar{\mathsf{k}}
be the maximal unramified extension of
\mathsf{k}
in
\bar{\mathsf{k}}
. The main result in this paper is a formula for the wavefront set of an arbitrary irreducible unipotent representation of
\mathbf{G}({\mathsf{k}})
of real infinitesimal character. To formulate our result, we will need to review some preliminary ideas.

1.1.
Wavefront sets

Let
X
be an irreducible admissible representation of
\mathbf{G}({\mathsf{k}})
. The
wavefront set
of
X
is a fundamental invariant related to the Harish-Chandra-Howe local character expansion. In its classical form, the wavefront set of
X
is a collection of nilpotent
{\mathbf{G}}({\mathsf{k}})
-orbits in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}({\mathsf{k}})
, denoted
\mathrm{WF}(X)
. We will recall the definition in Section
2.4
. In this paper, we consider two coarser invariants. The first of these invariants is the
algebraic wavefront set
, denoted
\hphantom{}{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X)
. This is a collection of nilpotent orbits in
\mathfrak{g}({\bar{\mathsf{k}}})
, see for example
[
Wal18
, p. 1108]
. The second invariant is a natural refinement
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)
of
\hphantom{}{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X)
called the
canonical unramified wavefront set
, defined recently in
[
Oka21
]
. This is a collection of nilpotent orbits
\mathfrak{g}(K)
(modulo a certain equivalence relation
\sim_{A}
). The relationship between these three invariants is as follows: the algebraic wavefront set
\hphantom{}{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X)
is deducible from the usual wavefront set
\mathrm{WF}(X)
as well as the canonical unramified wavefront set
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)
. It is not known whether the canonical unramified wavefront set is deducible from the usual wavefront set, but we expect this to be the case.

1.2.
Representations with unipotent cuspidal support

In
[
Lus95
]
, Lusztig defines the notion of an irreducible
unipotent representation
of
\mathbf{G}({\mathsf{k}})
. These are the quotients of the representations which are compactly induced from Deligne-Lusztig unipotent cuspidal representations of the reductive quotients of parahoric subgroups. We denote the set of such
\mathbf{G}({\mathsf{k}})
-representations by
\Pi^{\mathsf{Lus}}(\mathbf{G}({\mathsf{k}}))
. We will now briefly recall the classification of
\Pi^{\mathsf{Lus}}(\mathbf{G}({\mathsf{k}}))
(see Section
4.1
for a more detailed statement). While our main results are independent of isogeny (see in particular Remark
4.1.2
), we will assume in the introduction that
{\mathbf{G}}
is of adjoint type. Let
G^{\vee}
denote the complex points of the Langlands dual group of
\mathbf{G}
. Then
\Pi^{\mathsf{Lus}}(\mathbf{G}({\mathsf{k}}))
is in one-to-one correspondence with
G^{\vee}
-conjugacy classe of triples
(s,n,\rho)
consisting of

•

a semisimple element
s\in G^{\vee}
,

•

a nilpotent element
n\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
such that
\Ad(s)n=qn
, and

•

an irreducible representation
\rho
of the component group of the centralizer of
s
and
n
in
G^{\vee}
.

We write
X(s,n,\rho)
for the unipotent representation associated to the triple
(s,n,\rho)
. Conjugating if necessary, we can assume that
s
belongs to a fixed maximal torus
T^{\vee}\subset G^{\vee}
. There is a canonical decomposition
T^{\vee}=T^{\vee}_{c}T^{\vee}_{r}
, where
T^{\vee}_{c}
is the maximal compact subgroup of
T^{\vee}
and
T^{\vee}_{r}
is a real vector group. The representation
X(s,n,\rho)
is said to have
real infinitesimal character
if
s
belongs to
T^{\vee}_{r}
.

1.3.
Aubert-Zelevinsky duality

In
[
Aub95
]
, Aubert defines an involution
X\mapsto\mathrm{AZ}(X)
on the Grothendieck group of finite-length smooth
\mathbf{G}({\mathsf{k}})
-representations (generalizing an involution defined by Zelevinsky in
[
Zel80
]
). For details, we refer the reader to
[
CMO21
, Section 2.7]
. After a sign normalization depending only on the Bernstein component, this involution takes irreducibles to irreducibles and preserves the set
\Pi^{\mathsf{Lus}}(\mathbf{G}({\mathsf{k}}))
of unipotent representations. Moreover, if
(s,n,\rho)
is the parameter of a unipotent representation, then
\mathrm{AZ}(X(s,n,\rho))=X(s,n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
.

1.4.
Main results

Let
G
be the complex connected reductive algebraic group associated to
\mathbf{G}
. Let
\mathcal{N}_{o}
be the set of nilpotent orbits in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and let
\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}}
be the set of pairs
(\mathbb{O},\bar{C})
consisting of a nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}\in\mathcal{N}_{o}
and a conjugacy class
\bar{C}
in Lusztig’s canonical quotient
\bar{A}(\mathbb{O})
of the
G
-equivariant fundamental group
A(\mathbb{O})
of
\mathbb{O}
. Write
\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{o}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{o,\bar{c}}
for the corresponding sets for
G^{\vee}
. In
[
Spa82
, Proposition 10.3]
, Spaltenstein defines a duality map

d:\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{o}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{o}

An orbit
\mathbb{O}\in\mathcal{N}_{o}
is in the image of
d
if and only if it is special in the sense of Lusztig. In
[
Ach03
]
, Achar defines a related map

d_{A}:\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{o,\bar{c}}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}}

This map generalizes
d
in the following sense: for every orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\in\mathcal{N}_{o}
, there is a conjugacy class
C^{\prime}
in
\bar{A}(d(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}))
such that

d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},1)=(d(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}),\bar{C}^{\prime})

Furthermore,
\bar{C}^{\prime}=1
if
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is special.

There is a natural bijection between
\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}}
and the set of
\sim_{A}
-equivalence classes of unramified nilpotent orbits
\mathcal{N}_{o}(K)
of
{\mathbf{G}}(K)
, see
[
Oka21
, Section 5.1]
. Thus, we can regard
d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},\bar{C})
as an element of
\mathcal{N}_{o}(K)/\sim_{A}
.

Theorem 1.4.1

(See Theorem
6.0.3
and Corollary
6.0.5
below)
.

Let
X=X(s,n,\rho)
be a unipotent representation of
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathsf{k})
with real infinitesimal character and let
\mathsf{AZ}(X)=X(s,n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
.

(1)

The canonical unramified wavefront set
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)
is a singleton, and

{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)=d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\mathsf{AZ}(X)},1),

where
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\mathsf{AZ}(X)}
is the
G^{\vee}
-orbit of
n^{\prime}
and
d_{A}
is the duality map defined by Achar. In particular,

\hphantom{}{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X)=d(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\mathsf{AZ}(X)}).

(2)

Suppose
X=X(q^{\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee}},n,\rho)
where
h^{\vee}
is the neutral element of a Lie triple attached to a nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
. Then

d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},1)\leq_{A}\hphantom{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X),

where
\leq_{A}
is the partial order defined by Achar. In particular,

d(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})\leq\hphantom{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X).

This result is a vast generalization of
[
CMO21
, Theorem 1.2.1]
where a similar result was proved in the case when
{\mathbf{G}}
is split and
X
is Iwahori-spherical. When
{\mathbf{G}}
is inner to
\mathsf{SO}(2n+1)
and
\mathsf{AZ}(X)
is tempered, a formula for
\hphantom{}{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X)
was previously obtained by Waldspurger in
[
Wal18
]
.

We note that the class of representations considered in Theorem
1.4.1
is probably the most general setting for a result of this form. If
X
is unipotent of
non-real
infinitesimal character, then both formulas in (1) may not hold.

Example 1.4.2
.

Let
G^{\vee}=E_{7}
, let
T^{\vee}
be a maximal torus, and le
s\in T^{\vee}
be a semisimple element such that the centralizer
C_{G^{\vee}}(s)
of
s
is a subgroup of type
A_{1}+D_{6}
(there are two such
s
up to
W
-conjugacy).
Let
u
be a unipotent element in the orbit
(2)\times(5,3^{2},1)
of
C_{G^{\vee}}(s)
.
Then
A(s,u)
is a cyclic group of order 2 and
u
belongs to the orbit
A_{3}+A_{2}+A_{1}
of
G^{\vee}
.
Let
\rho
be the sign representation of
A(s,u)
.
We have that
d(A_{3}+A_{2}+A_{1})=A_{4}+A_{2}
.
However if
\mathsf{AZ}(X)
has parameters
(sq^{\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee}},u,\rho)
then
X
has wavefront set
A_{4}+A_{1}
which is the special orbit directly below
d(A_{3}+A_{2}+A_{1})
.
This is an example where both equalities in (1) fail (it suffices to show that second fails since the first implies the second).

We note that there is a strictly weaker result which appears to hold for all unipotent representations (including the example above). Let
d_{S}
be the duality map (
2.3.3
) defined by Sommers
[
Som01
]
. The following conjecture is due to the third named author.

Conjecture 1.4.3
.

Let
X
be a unipotent representation of
{\mathbf{G}}({\mathsf{k}})
.
Then
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)
is a singleton and

d_{S}(\hphantom{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X))=\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\mathsf{AZ}(X)}.

This is a weakening of our main theorem in the following sense: for a representation
X

{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)=d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\mathsf{AZ}(X)},1)\Leftrightarrow d_{S}(\hphantom{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X))=\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\mathsf{AZ}(X)}\text{ and }\hphantom{}^{{\bar{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X)=d(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{\mathsf{AZ}(X)}).

If we leave the world of unipotent representations, further difficulties arise. For one, there is no complete classification of irreducible representations in this more general setting.
Moreover, it was recently announced in
[
Tsa22
]
that
\hphantom{}{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X)
may not always be a singleton for positive depth representations.

We conclude with the following remark.
The study of wavefront sets has traditionally been guided by the question ‘How can we determine the wavefront set from the L-parameter?’. However since
d_{S}
is a many to one map, Conjecture
1.4.3
suggests it is more natural to ask ‘How can we determine the nilpotent part of the L-parameter from the wavefront set of the AZ dual?’.
Moreover, since
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)
is a coarsening of
\mathrm{WF}(X)
one might further wonder how much arithmetic information exactly is encoded in
\mathrm{WF}(X)
?

1.5.
Structure of paper

In Section
2
we collect some preliminaries regarding the structure theory of
p
-adic groups, nilpotent orbits, and wavefront sets. In Section
3
, we recall some facts regarding (graded) affine Hecke algebras, including Lusztig’s reduction theorems, the classification of simple modules, analytic deformations, and branching to parahoric subalgebras. In Section
4
, we recall the details of Lusztig’s arithmetic-geometric correspondence, and use it to obtain a formula for the structure of the parahoric restrictions of a unipotent representation. Our main results, stated and proved in Section
6
, depend on verifying a certain technical property of nilpotent orbits related to the generalized Springer correspondence. We call this property
x
-faithfulness
; it can be viewed as a generalization of the notion of an
adapted pair
from
[
Lus20
]
. In Section
5
we check this property in all types. In the process, we establish a new combinatorial characterization of the generalized Springer correspondence for spin groups. In Section
6
, we use
x
-faithfulness to prove our main results.

We mention that the motivation of
[
Lus20
]
is to give a new, purely algebraic, description of the Springer correspondence, via families of unipotent representations for (maximal) parahoric subgroups. We expect that our results in Section
5
will play a similar role for the generalized Springer correspondence.

1.6.
Acknowledgments

This research was partially supported by EP/V046713/1. The authors thank Ruben La for helpful discussions about the generalized Springer correspondence.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notation

Let
\mathsf{k}
be a nonarchimedean local field of characteristic
0
with residue field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
of sufficiently large characteristic, ring of integers
\mathfrak{o}\subset\mathsf{k}
, and valuation
\mathsf{val}_{\mathsf{k}}
. Fix an algebraic closure
\bar{\mathsf{k}}
of
\mathsf{k}
with Galois group
\Gamma
, and let
K\subset\bar{\mathsf{k}}
be the maximal unramified extension of
\mathsf{k}
in
\bar{\mathsf{k}}
.
Let
\mathfrak{O}
be the ring of integers of
K
.
Let
\mathrm{Frob}
be the geometric Frobenius element of
\mathrm{Gal}(K/\mathsf{k})
, the topological generator which induces the inverse of the automorphism
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}x^{q}
of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

Let
{\mathbf{G}}
be a connected reductive algebraic group defined over
{\mathsf{k}}
, inner to split, and let
{\mathbf{T}}\subset\mathbf{G}
be a maximal torus. For any field
F
containing
{\mathsf{k}}
, we write
\mathbf{G}(F)
,
\mathbf{T}(F)
, etc. for the groups of
F
-rational points. Let
{\mathbf{G}}_{\ad}={\mathbf{G}}/Z({\mathbf{G}})
denote the adjoint group of
{\mathbf{G}}
.

Write
X^{*}(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}})
(resp.
X_{*}(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}})
) for the lattice of algebraic characters (resp. co-characters) of
\mathbf{T}({\bar{\mathsf{k}}})
, and write
\Phi(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}})
(resp.
\Phi^{\vee}(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}})
) for the set of roots (resp. co-roots). Let

\mathcal{R}=(X^{*}(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}}),\ \Phi(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}}),X_{*}(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}}),\ \Phi^{\vee}(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}}),\ \langle\ ,\ \rangle)

be the root datum corresponding to
\mathbf{G}
, and let
W
the associated (finite) Weyl group.
Let
G
be the complex reductive group with the same absolute root datum as
{\mathbf{G}}
and let
\mathbf{G}^{\vee}
be the Langlands dual group of
{\mathbf{G}}
, i.e. the connected reductive algebraic group defined and split over
\mathbb{Z}
corresponding to the dual root datum

\mathcal{R}^{\vee}=(X_{*}(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}}),\ \Phi^{\vee}(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}}),X^{*}(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}}),\ \Phi(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}}),\ \langle\ ,\ \rangle).

Set
\Omega=X_{*}(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}})/\mathbb{Z}\Phi^{\vee}(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}})
. The center
Z({\mathbf{G}}^{\vee})
can be naturally identified with the irreducible characters
\mathsf{Irr}\Omega
, and dually,
\Omega\cong X^{*}(Z({\mathbf{G}}^{\vee}))
. For
\omega\in\Omega
, let
\zeta_{\omega}
denote the corresponding irreducible character of
Z({\mathbf{G}}^{\vee})
.

For details regarding the parametrization of inner twists of
{\mathbf{G}}
, see
[
Vog93
, §2]
,
[
Kot84
]
,
[
Kal16
, §2]
. For our purposes, it suffices to note that there is a natural bijection between equivalence classes of inner twists of
\mathbf{G}
and elements of the Galois cohomology group

H^{1}(\Gamma,\mathbf{G}_{\ad})\cong H^{1}(F,\mathbf{G}_{\ad}(K))\cong\Omega_{\ad}\cong\Irr Z({\mathbf{G}}^{\vee}_{\mathsf{sc}}),

Here
{\mathbf{G}}^{\vee}_{\mathsf{sc}}
denotes the Langlands dual group of
{\mathbf{G}}_{\ad}
and
F
denotes the action of
\mathrm{Frob}
on
{\mathbf{G}}(K)
. The isomorphism above is defined as follows: for a cohomology class
h
in
H^{1}(F,\mathbf{G}_{\ad}(K))
, let
z
be a representative cocycle. Let
u\in{\mathbf{G}}_{\ad}(K)
be the image of
F
under
z
, and let
\omega
denote the image of
u
in
\Omega_{\ad}
. Set
F_{\omega}=\Ad(u)\circ F
. Then the rational structure of
{\mathbf{G}}
corresponding to
h
is given by
F_{\omega}
.
Write
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}
for the connected reductive group defined over
{\mathsf{k}}
such that
{\mathbf{G}}(K)^{F_{\omega}}={\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k})
.

If
H
is a complex reductive group and
x
is an element of
H
or
\mathfrak{h}
, we write
H(x)=C_{H}(x)
for the centralizer of
x
in
H
, and
A_{H}(x)
for the group of connected components of
H(x)
. If
S
is a subset of
H
or
\mathfrak{h}
(or indeed, of
H\cup\mathfrak{h}
), we can similarly define
H(S)
and
A_{H}(S)
. We will sometimes write
A(x)
,
A(S)
when the group
H
is implicit.
The subgroups of
H
of the form
H(x)
where
x
is a semisimple element of
H
are called
pseudo-Levi
subgroups of
H
.

Let
\mathcal{C}({\mathbf{G}}(\mathsf{k}))
be the category of finite-length smooth complex
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathsf{k})
-representations and let
\Pi(\mathbf{G}(\mathsf{k}))\subset\mathcal{C}({\mathbf{G}}(\mathsf{k}))
be the set of irreducible objects. Let
R({\mathbf{G}}(\mathsf{k}))
denote the Grothendieck group of
\mathcal{C}({\mathbf{G}}(\mathsf{k}))
.

2.2.
The Bruhat-Tits Building

In this section we will recall some standard facts about the Bruhat-Tits building.

Fix a
\omega\in\Omega
and let
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}
be the inner twist of
{\mathbf{G}}
corresponding
\omega
as defined in the previous section.
Let
\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}}^{\omega},{\mathsf{k}})
denote the (enlarged) Bruhat-Tits building for
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}({\mathsf{k}})
.
Let
\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}},K)
denote the (enlarged) Bruhat-Tits building for
{\mathbf{G}}(K)
.
For an apartment
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}},K)
and
\Omega\subseteq\mathcal{A}
we write
\mathcal{A}(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
for the smallest affine subspace of
\mathcal{A}
containing
\Omega
.
The inner twist
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}
of
{\mathbf{G}}
gives rise to an action of the Galois group
{\mathrm{Gal}}(K/k)
on
\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}},K)
and we can (and will) identify
\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}}^{\omega},{\mathsf{k}})
with the fixed points of this action.
We use the notation
c\subseteq\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}}^{\omega},{\mathsf{k}})
to indicate that
c
is a face of
\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}}^{\omega},{\mathsf{k}})
.
Given a maximal
{\mathsf{k}}
-split torus
{\mathbf{T}}
of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}
, write
\mathcal{A}({\mathbf{T}},{\mathsf{k}})
for the corresponding apartment in
\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}}^{\omega},{\mathsf{k}})
.
For a face
c\subseteq\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}}^{\omega},{\mathsf{k}})
there is a group
{\mathbf{P}}_{c}^{\dagger}
defined over
\mathfrak{o}
such that
{\mathbf{P}}_{c}^{\dagger}(\mathfrak{o})
identifies with the stabiliser of
c
in
{\mathbf{G}}(k)
. There is an exact sequence

(2.2.1)

1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{U}}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{P}}_{c}^{\dagger}(\mathfrak{o})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{L}}_{c}^{\dagger}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1,

where
{\mathbf{U}}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})
is the pro-unipotent radical of
{\mathbf{P}}_{c}^{\dagger}(\mathfrak{o})
and
{\mathbf{L}}_{c}^{\dagger}
is the reductive quotient of the special fibre of
{\mathbf{P}}_{c}^{\dagger}
.
Let
{\mathbf{L}}_{c}
denote the identity component of
{\mathbf{L}}_{c}^{\dagger}
, and let
{\mathbf{P}}_{c}
be the subgroup of
\bf P_{c}^{\dagger}
defined over
\mathfrak{o}
such that
{\mathbf{P}}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})
is the inverse image of
{\mathbf{L}}_{c}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
in
{\mathbf{P}}_{c}^{\dagger}(\mathfrak{o})
.
We also write
{\mathbf{T}}
for the well defined split torus scheme over
\mathfrak{o}
with generic fibre
{\mathbf{T}}
.
This scheme
{\mathbf{T}}
defined over
\mathfrak{o}
is a subgroup of
{\mathbf{P}}_{c}
and the special fibre of
{\mathbf{T}}
, denoted
\bar{\mathbf{T}}
, is a maximal torus of
{\mathbf{L}}_{c}
.
For
c
viewed as a face of
\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}},K)
, the stabiliser of
c
in
{\mathbf{G}}(K)
identifies with
{\mathbf{P}}_{c}^{\dagger}(\mathfrak{O})
.
It has pro-unipotent radical
{\mathbf{U}}_{c}(\mathfrak{O})
and
{\mathbf{P}}_{c}^{\dagger}(\mathfrak{O})/{\mathbf{U}}_{c}(\mathfrak{O})={\mathbf{L}}_{c}^{\dagger}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q})
.
For
c
a face lying in
\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}}^{\omega},{\mathsf{k}})\subseteq\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}},K)
,
F_{\omega}
stabilises
{\mathbf{P}}_{c}(\mathfrak{O})
and induces a Frobenius on
{\mathbf{L}}_{c}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q})
.
The group
{\mathbf{L}}_{c}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
consists of the fixed points of this Frobenius.
The groups
{\mathbf{P}}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})
obtained in this manner are called (
{\mathsf{k}}
-)
parahoric subgroups
of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}
.
When
c
is a chamber, then we call
{\mathbf{P}}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})
an
Iwahori subgroup
of
\mathbf{G}
.

For the purposes of this paper, it will be convenient to fix a maximal
{\mathsf{k}}
-split torus
{\mathbf{T}}
of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}
and a maximal
K
-split torus
{\mathbf{T}}_{1}
of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}
containing
{\mathbf{T}}
and defined over
{\mathsf{k}}
.
We have that
\mathcal{A}({\mathbf{T}},{\mathsf{k}})=\mathcal{A}({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)^{{\mathrm{Gal}}(K/k)}
.
Write
\Phi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
(resp.
\Psi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
) for the set of roots of
{\mathbf{G}}(K)
(resp. affine roots) with respect to
{\mathbf{T}}_{1}(K)
.
For
\psi\in\Psi({\mathbf{T}},{\mathsf{k}})
write
\dot{\psi}\in\Phi({\mathbf{T}},{\mathsf{k}})
for the gradient of
\psi
, and

W=W({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
for the Weyl group of
{\mathbf{G}}(K)
with respect to
{\mathbf{T}}_{1}(K)
.
We will also fix a
{\mathrm{Gal}}(K/{\mathsf{k}})
-stable chamber
c_{0}
of
\mathcal{A}({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
and a special point
x_{0}\in c_{0}
.
Let
\widetilde{W}=W\ltimes X_{*}(\mathbf{T}_{1},K)
be the (extended) affine Weyl group.
The choice of special point
x_{0}
of
\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}},K)
fixes an inclusion
\Phi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Psi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
and an isomorphism between
\widetilde{W}
and
N_{{\mathbf{G}}(K)}({\mathbf{T}}_{1}(K))/{\mathbf{T}}_{1}(\mathfrak{O}^{\times})
.
Write

(2.2.2)

\widetilde{W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}W,\qquad w\mapsto\dot{w}

for the natural projection map.
For a face
c\subseteq\mathcal{A}({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
let
W_{c}
be the subgroup of
\widetilde{W}
generated by reflections in the hyperplanes through
c
.
The special fibre of
{\mathbf{T}}_{1}
(as a scheme over
\mathfrak{O}
) which we denote by
\overline{{\mathbf{T}}}_{1}
, is a split maximal torus of
{\mathbf{L}}_{c}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q})
.
Write
\Phi_{c}(\bar{\mathbf{T}}_{1},\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q})
for the root system of
{\mathbf{L}}_{c}
with respect to
\bar{\mathbf{T}}_{1}
.
Then
\Phi_{c}(\bar{\mathbf{T}}_{1},\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q})
naturally identifies with the set of
\psi\in\Psi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
that vanish on
c
, and the Weyl group of
\bar{\mathbf{T}}_{1}
in
{\mathbf{L}}_{c}
is isomorphic to
W_{c}
.

Recall that a choice of
x_{0}
fixes an embedding
\Phi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Psi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},L)
.
If we fix a set of simple roots
\Delta\subset\Phi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
, this embedding determines a set of extended simple roots
\tilde{\Delta}\subseteq\Psi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
.
When
\Phi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
is irreducible,
\tilde{\Delta}
is just the set
\Delta\cup\{1-\alpha_{0}\}
where
\alpha_{0}
is the highest root of
\Phi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
with respect to
\Delta
.
When
\Phi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
is reducible, say
\Phi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)=\cup_{i}\Phi_{i}
where each
\Phi_{i}
is irreducible, then
\tilde{\Delta}=\cup_{i}\tilde{\Delta}_{i}
where
\Delta_{i}=\Phi_{i}\cap\Delta
.
Fix
\Delta
so that the chamber cut out by
\tilde{\Delta}
is
c_{0}
.
Let

{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{\Delta}):=\{J\subsetneq\tilde{\Delta}:J\cap\tilde{\Delta}_{i}\subsetneq\tilde{\Delta}_{i},\forall i\}.

Each
J\in{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{\Delta})
cuts out a face of
c_{0}
which we denote by
c(J)
.
In particular
c(\Delta)=x_{0}
.
Note that since
\Omega\simeq\widetilde{W}/W\ltimes\mathbb{Z}\Phi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
(recall
{\mathbf{G}}
is semisimple), and
W\ltimes\mathbb{Z}\Phi({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
acts simply transitively on the chambers of
\mathcal{A}({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
, the action of
\widetilde{W}
on
\mathcal{A}({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
induces an action of
\Omega
on the faces of
c_{0}
and hence on
\tilde{\Delta}
(and
{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{\Delta})
).
For
\omega\in\Omega
let
\sigma_{\omega}
denote the corresponding permutation of
\tilde{\Delta}
.
Let

{\mathbf{P}}^{\omega}(\tilde{\Delta}):=\{J\in{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{\Delta})\mid\sigma_{\omega}(J)=J\}

and let
c_{0}^{\omega}
be the chamber of
\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}}^{\omega},{\mathsf{k}})
lying in
c_{0}
.
The set
{\mathbf{P}}^{\omega}(\tilde{\Delta})
is an indexing set for the faces of
c_{0}^{\omega}
.
For
J\in{\mathbf{P}}^{\omega}(\tilde{\Delta})
write
c^{\omega}(J)
for the face of
c_{0}^{\omega}
corresponding to
J
.
The face
c^{\omega}(J)
lies in
c(J)
.
Moreover for
J,J^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}^{\omega}(\tilde{\Delta})
(resp.
{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{\Delta})
) we have
J\subseteq J^{\prime}
if and only if
\overline{c^{\omega}(J)}\supseteq c^{\omega}(J^{\prime})
(resp.
\overline{c(J)}\supseteq c(J^{\prime})
).
After this section we will write
I
for
\tilde{\Delta}
and
I_{0}
for
\Delta
.

2.3.
Nilpotent orbits

Let
\mathcal{N}
be the functor which takes a field
F
to the set of nilpotent elements in
\mathfrak{g}(F)
, and let
\mathcal{N}_{o}
be the functor which takes
F
to the set of adjoint
{\mathbf{G}}(F)
-orbits on
\mathcal{N}(F)
. When
F
is
{\mathsf{k}}
or
K
, we view
\mathcal{N}_{o}(F)
as a partially ordered set with respect to the closure ordering in the topology induced by the topology on
F
.
When
F
is algebraically closed, we view
\mathcal{N}_{o}(F)
as a partially ordered set with respect to the closure ordering in the Zariski topology.
To simplify the notation, we will write
\mathcal{N}(F^{\prime}/F)
(resp.
\mathcal{N}_{o}(F^{\prime}/F)
) for
\mathcal{N}(F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}F^{\prime})
(resp.
\mathcal{N}_{o}(F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}F^{\prime})
) where
F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}F^{\prime}
is a morphism of fields.
For
(F,F^{\prime})=({\mathsf{k}},K)
(resp.
({\mathsf{k}},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}})
,
(K,{\bar{\mathsf{k}}})
), the map
\mathcal{N}_{o}(F^{\prime}/F)
is strictly increasing (resp. strictly increasing, non-decreasing).
We will simply write
\mathcal{N}
for
\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathcal{N}_{o}
for
\mathcal{N}_{o}(\mathbb{C})
.
In this case we also define
\mathcal{N}_{o,c}
(resp.
\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}}
) to be the set of all pairs
(\mathbb{O},C)
such that
\mathbb{O}\in\mathcal{N}_{o}
and
C
is a conjugacy class in the fundamental group
A(\mathbb{O})
of
\mathbb{O}
(resp. Lusztig’s canonical quotient
\bar{A}(\mathbb{O})
of
A(\mathbb{O})
, see
[
Som01
, Section 5]
). There is a natural map

(2.3.1)

\mathfrak{Q}:\mathcal{N}_{o,c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}},\qquad(\mathbb{O},C)\mapsto(\mathbb{O},\bar{C})

where
\bar{C}
is the image of
C
in
\bar{A}(\mathbb{O})
under the natural homomorphism
A(\mathbb{O})\twoheadrightarrow\bar{A}(\mathbb{O})
. There are also projection maps
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:\mathcal{N}_{o,c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{o}
,
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{o}
. We will typically write
\mathcal{N}^{\vee}
,
\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{o},\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{o,c}
, and
\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{o,\bar{c}}
for the sets
\mathcal{N}
,
\mathcal{N}_{o},\mathcal{N}_{o,c}
, and
\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}}
associated to the Langlands dual group
G^{\vee}
. When we wish to emphasise the group we are working with we include it as a superscript e.g.
\mathcal{N}_{o}^{{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}}
.
Note that since
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}
splits over
K
we have that
\mathcal{N}_{o}^{{\mathbf{G}}}(F)=\mathcal{N}_{o}^{{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}}(F)
for field extensions
F
of
K
. The following well-known result is due to Pommerening, Spaltenstein, Gerstenhaber, and Hesselink.

Lemma 2.3.1
.

Let
F
be algebraically closed of sufficiently large characteristic.
Then there is canonical isomorphism of partially ordered sets
\Lambda^{F}:\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\mathbf{G}}(F)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{o}
.

Proof.

Pommerening establishes a canonical bijection
\Lambda^{F}:\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\mathbf{G}}(F)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{o}
in
[
Pom77
, Corollary 3.5]
and
[
Pom80
, Theorem 1.5]
. In classical types, it is well-known that this bijection is order-preserving (the partial order is given by the dominance order on partitions on both sides, see
[
Ger61
]
and
[
Hes76
]
). In exceptional types, one sees that
\Lambda^{F}
is order-preserving by examining the diagrams in
[
Spa82
, Chapter 4]
.
∎

There are several duality maps relating the sets
\mathcal{N}_{o}
,
\mathcal{N}_{o,c}
,
\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}}
and their dual group counterparts.

(1)

Write

(2.3.2)

d:\mathcal{N}_{o}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\vee},\qquad d:\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\vee}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{o}.

for the duality maps defined by Spaltenstein (
[
Spa82
, Proposition 10.3]
), Lusztig (
[
Lus84b
, §13.3]
), and Barbasch-Vogan (
[
BV85
, Appendix A]
).

(2)

Write

(2.3.3)

d_{S}:\mathcal{N}_{o,c}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{o},\qquad d_{S}:\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{o,c}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{o}

for the duality maps defined by Sommers (
[
Som01
, Section 6]
).

(3)

Write

(2.3.4)

d_{A}:\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{o,\bar{c}},\qquad d_{A}:\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{o,\bar{c}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}}

for the duality maps defined by Achar (
[
Ach03
, Section 1]
).

Write
\leq_{A}
for Achar’s preorder on
\mathcal{N}_{o,c}

[
Ach03
, Introduction]
and
\sim_{A}
for the equivalence relation on
\mathcal{N}_{o,c}
induced by this pre-order, i.e.

(\mathbb{O}_{1},C_{1})\sim_{A}(\mathbb{O}_{2},C_{2})\iff(\mathbb{O}_{1},C_{1})\leq_{A}(\mathbb{O}_{2},C_{2})\text{ and }(\mathbb{O}_{2},C_{2})\leq_{A}(\mathbb{O}_{1},C_{1}).

Write
[(\mathbb{O},C)]
for the equivalence class of
(\mathbb{O},C)\in\mathcal{N}_{o,c}
. The
\sim_{A}
-equivalence classes in
\mathcal{N}_{o,c}
coincide with the fibres of the projection map
\mathfrak{Q}:\mathcal{N}_{o,c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}}

[
Ach03
, Theorem 1]
. So
\leq_{A}
descends to a partial order on
\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}}
, which we simply denote by
\leq_{A}
. The maps
d,d_{S},d_{A}
are all order reversing with respect to the relevant pre/partial orders.

2.3.1.
Structure of
\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\mathbf{G}}(K)

Let
{\mathbf{T}}
be a maximal
{\mathsf{k}}
-split torus of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}
,
{\mathbf{T}}_{1}
be a maximal
K
-split torus of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}
defined over
{\mathsf{k}}
and containing
{\mathbf{T}}
, and let
x_{0}
be a special point in
\mathcal{A}({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
.
In
[
Oka21
, Section 2.1.5]
the third-named author constructs a bijection

\theta_{x_{0},{\mathbf{T}}_{1}}:\mathcal{N}_{o}^{{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}}(K)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{o,c}.

The composition

d^{un}:=d_{S}\circ\theta_{x_{0},{\mathbf{T}}_{1}}:\mathcal{N}_{o}(K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\vee}

is independent of the choice of
x_{0}
and natural in
{\mathbf{T}}_{1}

[
Oka21
, Proposition 2.32]
.

For
\mathbb{O}_{1},\mathbb{O}_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{o}(K)
define
\mathbb{O}_{1}\leq_{A}\mathbb{O}_{2}
by

\mathbb{O}_{1}\leq_{A}\mathbb{O}_{2}\iff\mathcal{N}_{o}(\bar{k}/K)(\mathbb{O}_{1})\leq\mathcal{N}_{o}(\bar{k}/K)(\mathbb{O}_{2}),\text{ and }d^{un}(\mathbb{O}_{1})\geq d^{un}(\mathbb{O}_{2})

and let
\sim_{A}
denote the equivalence classes of this pre-order.

Theorem 2.3.2
.

[
Oka21
, Theorem 2.33]

The composition
\mathfrak{Q}\circ\theta_{x_{0},{\mathbf{T}}_{1}}:\mathcal{N}_{o}(K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}}
descends to a (natural in
{\mathbf{T}}_{1}
) isomorphism of partial orders

\bar{\theta}:\mathcal{N}_{o}(K)/\sim_{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}}

which does not depend on
x_{0}
.

2.3.2.
Lifting nilpotent orbits

Define

(2.3.5)

\mathcal{I}_{o}=\{(c,\mathbb{O})\mid c\subseteq\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}},K),\mathbb{O}\in\mathcal{N}_{o}^{{\mathbf{L}}_{c}}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q})\}.

There is a partial order on
\mathcal{I}_{o}
, defined by

(c_{1},\mathbb{O}_{1})\leq(c_{2},\mathbb{O}_{2})\iff c_{1}=c_{2}\text{ and }\mathbb{O}_{1}\leq\mathbb{O}_{2},

and a strictly increasing surjective map
[
Oka21
, Section 1.1.2]

(2.3.6)

\mathscr{L}:(\mathcal{I}_{o},\leq)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}(\mathcal{N}_{o}(K),\leq).

Recall the groups
G={\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{C}),T={\mathbf{T}}(\mathbb{C})
from section
2.1
. We call a pseudo-Levi subgroup
L
of
G

standard
if it contains
T
, and we write
Z_{L}
for its center. Let
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}({\mathbf{T}}_{1},K)
.

Recall from the end of section
2.2
the definitions of
\Delta,\tilde{\Delta},c_{0},{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{\Delta})
and
c(J)
.
Note that the definitions of
c_{0}
and
c(J)
depend on a choice of
x_{0}
and
\Delta
.
Let
L_{J}
denote the pseudo-Levi subgroup of
G
generated by
T
and the root groups corresponding to
\dot{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in J
.
Then
L_{J}
is the complex connected reductive group with the same absolute root datum as
\mathbf{L}_{c(J)}
.
Thus Lemma
gives rise to an isomorphism

\Lambda_{J}^{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}:\mathcal{N}_{o}^{{\mathbf{L}}_{c(J)}(\mathfrak{o})}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{o}^{L_{J}}(\mathbb{C}).

Define

(2.3.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{x_{0},\tilde{\Delta}}

\displaystyle=\{(J,\mathbb{O})\mid J\in{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{\Delta}),\ \mathbb{O}\in\mathcal{N}_{o}^{{\mathbf{L}}_{c(J)}}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q})\},

(2.3.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\tilde{\Delta}}

\displaystyle=\{(J,\mathbb{O})\mid J\in{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{\Delta}),\ \mathbb{O}\in\mathcal{N}_{o}^{L_{J}}(\mathbb{C})\}.

The map

\iota_{x_{0}}:\mathcal{I}_{x_{0},\tilde{\Delta}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{K}_{\tilde{\Delta}},\quad(J,\mathbb{O})\mapsto(J,\Lambda_{J}^{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(\mathbb{O}))

is a bijection.
Let

(2.3.9)

\mathbb{L}:\mathcal{K}_{\tilde{\Delta}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{c,o}

be the map
[
Oka21
, Corollary 2.19]
.
By
[
CMO21
, Theorem 2.1.7]
the diagram

(2.3.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}_{x_{0},\tilde{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}_{\tilde{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}_{o}(K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}_{o,c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{x_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{x_{0},{\mathbf{T}}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commutes.
Define

\overline{\mathbb{L}}=\mathfrak{Q}\circ\mathbb{L}.

This map can be computed using the algorithms in
[
Ach03
, Section 3.4]
.

2.4.
Wavefront sets

Let
X
be an admissible smooth representation of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}({\mathsf{k}})
and let
\Theta_{X}
be the character of
X
.
Recall that for each nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}\in\mathcal{N}_{o}^{{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}}({\mathsf{k}})
there is an associated distribution
\mu_{\mathbb{O}}
on
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g}^{\omega}({\mathsf{k}}))
called the
nilpotent orbital integral
of
\mathbb{O}
(
[
Ran72
]
).
Write
\hat{\mu}_{\mathbb{O}}
for its Fourier transform.
Generalizing a result of Howe (
[
How74
]
), Harish-Chandra in
[
Har99
]
shows that there are complex numbers
c_{\mathbb{O}}(X)\in\mathbb{C}
such that

(2.4.1)

\Theta_{X}(\mathrm{exp}(\xi))=\sum_{\mathbb{O}}c_{\mathbb{O}}(X)\hat{\mu}_{\mathbb{O}}(\xi)

for
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{\omega}({\mathsf{k}})
a regular element in a small neighborhood of
0
. The formula (
2.4.1
) is called the
local character expansion
of
X
.
The
(
p
-adic) wavefront set
of
X
is

\mathrm{WF}(X):=\max\{\mathbb{O}\mid c_{\mathbb{O}}(X)\neq 0\}\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{o}({\mathsf{k}}).

The
algebraic wavefront set
of
X
is

{}^{\bar{k}}\mathrm{WF}(X):=\max\{\mathcal{N}_{o}({\bar{\mathsf{k}}}/{\mathsf{k}})(\mathbb{O})\mid c_{\mathbb{O}}(X)\neq 0\}\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{o}({\bar{\mathsf{k}}}),

see
[
Wal18
, p. 1108]
(warning: in
[
Wal18
]
, the invariant
{}^{\bar{k}}\mathrm{WF}(X)
is called simply the ‘wavefront set’ of
X
).

In
[
Oka21
, Section 2.2.3]
the third author has introduced a third type of wavefront set for depth-
0
representations, called
the
canonical unramified wavefront set
. This invariant is a natural refinement of
\hphantom{}{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X)
. We will now define
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)
and explain how to compute it.

Recall from Equation
2.3.6
the lifting map
\mathscr{L}
.
For every face
c\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{G})
, the space of invariants
X^{{\mathbf{U}}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})}
is a (finite-dimensional)
{\mathbf{L}}_{c}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-representation. Let
\mathrm{WF}(X^{{\mathbf{U}}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{{\mathbf{L}}_{c}}_{o}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q})
denote the Kawanaka wavefront set
[
Kaw87
]
and let

(2.4.2)

{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}_{c}(X):=\{[\mathscr{L}(c,\mathbb{O})]\mid\mathbb{O}\in\mathrm{WF}(X^{{\mathbf{U}}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})})\}\quad\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{o}(K).

Definition 2.4.1
.

Let
X
be a depth-0 representation of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}({\mathsf{k}})
.
The
canonical unramified wavefront set
of
X
is

(2.4.3)

{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X):=\max\{\hphantom{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}_{c}(X)\mid c\subseteq\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}}^{\omega},{\mathsf{k}})\}\quad\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{o}(K)/\sim_{A}.

Fix
{\mathbf{T}},{\mathbf{T}}_{1},c_{0},x_{0},\Delta
as at the end of section
2.2
.
By
[
Oka21
, Lemma 2.36]
we have that

(2.4.4)

{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)=\max\{\hphantom{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(X)\mid J\in{\mathbf{P}}^{\omega}(\tilde{\Delta})\}.

We will often want to view
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)
and
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}_{c}(X)
as subsets of
\mathcal{N}_{o,\bar{c}}
using the identification
\bar{\theta}_{{\mathbf{T}}_{1}}
from Theorem
2.3.2
.
We will write

{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X,\mathbb{C}):=\bar{\theta}_{{\mathbf{T}}_{1}}(\hphantom{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)),\quad\hphantom{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}_{c}(X,\mathbb{C}):=\bar{\theta}_{{\mathbf{T}}_{1}}(\hphantom{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}_{c}(X)).

We will also want to view
\hphantom{}{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X)
, which is naturally contained in
\mathcal{N}_{o}(\bar{k})
, as a subset of
\mathcal{N}_{o}
. Thus, we will write

\hphantom{}{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X,\mathbb{C}):=\Lambda^{\bar{\mathsf{k}}}(\hphantom{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X)).

By
[
Oka21
, Theorem 2.37]
, if
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)
is a singleton, then
\hphantom{}{}^{{\bar{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X)
is also a singleton and

(2.4.5)

\hphantom{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X,\mathbb{C})=(\hphantom{}^{{\bar{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X,\mathbb{C}),\bar{C}).

for some conjugacy class
\bar{C}
in
\bar{A}(\hphantom{}^{\bar{\mathsf{k}}}\mathrm{WF}(X,\mathbb{C}))
.

3.
Affine Hecke algebras and graded affine Hecke
algebras

In this section we will collect some preliminaries regarding affine Hecke algebras and
graded affine Hecke algebras. We follow
[
Lus89
,
Lus88
,
Lus95a
,
Lus02a
]
,
[
BM93
]
,
[
BC13
]
, and
[
Sol12
]
.

3.1.
Generic affine Hecke algebras

Let
\Psi=(X,X^{\vee},R,R^{\vee})
be a reduced root
datum.
Let
W
be the finite Weyl group associated to
\Psi
. Fix a choice of positive
roots
R^{+}\subset R
, with basis
\Pi
of
simple roots, and let
R^{\vee,+}
,
\Pi^{\vee}
be the corresponding
subsets of
R^{\vee}
. Let

\ell:W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{Z}
be the length function corresponding to
\Pi
. Let
\widetilde{W}=W\ltimes X
be the extended affine Weyl group.

We write
G(\Psi)
(or
just
G
if there is no ambiguity) for the complex connected reductive algebraic group associated to
\Psi
. Then

T:=X^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is a maximal torus in
G
. Let
B\supset T
be the Borel subgroup corresponding to
R^{+}.
We may identify:

X=\Hom(T,\mathbb{C}^{\times}),\qquad X^{\vee}=\Hom(\mathbb{C}^{\times},T).

A parameter set for
\Psi
is a pair of functions
(\lambda,\lambda^{*})
,

\displaystyle\lambda:\Pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

\displaystyle\lambda^{*}:\{{\alpha}\in\Pi:\check{\alpha}\in 2X^{\vee}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

such that
\lambda({\alpha})=\lambda({\alpha}^{\prime})
and

{\lambda}^{*}({\alpha})={\lambda}^{*}({\alpha}^{\prime})
whenever
{\alpha},{\alpha}^{\prime}
are
W-
conjugate.

Definition 3.1.1

(Bernstein presentation,
[
Lus89
, Section 3]
)
.

The affine Hecke algebra

{\mathcal{H}}^{\lambda,\lambda^{*}}(\Psi,z)
, or just
{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi,z)
,
associated to the root datum

\Psi
with parameter set
(\lambda,\lambda^{*})
is the
associative algebra over
\mathbb{C}[z,z^{-1}]
with unit (
z
is an indeterminate), generated by the elements
T_{w}
,
w\in W
, and
\theta_{x}
,

x\in X
subject to the relations:

(3.1.1)

\displaystyle(T_{s_{\alpha}}+1)(T_{s_{\alpha}}-z^{2\lambda({\alpha})})=0,\text{ for all }{\alpha}\in\Pi,

\displaystyle T_{w}T_{w^{\prime}}=T_{ww^{\prime}},\text{ for all }w,w^{\prime}\in W\text{ such that }\ell(ww^{\prime})=\ell(w)+\ell(w^{\prime}),

(3.1.2)

\displaystyle\theta_{x}\theta_{x^{\prime}}=\theta_{x+x^{\prime}},\text{ for all }x,x^{\prime}\in X,

\displaystyle\theta_{x}T_{s_{\alpha}}-T_{s_{\alpha}}\theta_{s_{\alpha}(x)}=(\theta_{x}-\theta_{s_{\alpha}(x)})({\mathcal{G}}({\alpha})-1),\text{ where }x\in X,{\alpha}\in\Pi,\text{ and }

(3.1.3)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}({\alpha})=\begin{cases}\frac{\theta_{\alpha}z^{2\lambda({\alpha})}-1}{\theta_{\alpha}-1},&\text{ if }\check{\alpha}\notin 2X^{\vee},\\
\frac{(\theta_{\alpha}z^{\lambda({\alpha})+\lambda^{*}({\alpha})}-1)(\theta_{\alpha}z^{\lambda({\alpha})-\lambda^{*}({\alpha})}+1)}{\theta_{2{\alpha}}-1},&\text{ if
}\check{\alpha}\in 2X^{\vee}.\end{cases}

Let
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{A}}(\Psi,z)
be the algebra of regular functions on
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times T.
Note that
\mathcal{A}
can be identified with the abelian
\mathbb{C}[z,z^{-1}]-
subalgebra of

{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi,z)
generated by
\{\theta_{x}:x\in X\}
, where for
every
x\in X,

\theta_{x}:T\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is defined by

(3.1.4)

\theta_{x}(y\otimes\zeta)=\zeta^{\langle x,y\rangle},\ y\in X^{\vee},~\zeta\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}.

If we denote by
{\mathcal{H}}_{W}(z)
the
\mathbb{C}[z,z^{-1}]-
subalgebra generated by

\{T_{w}:w\in W\}
, then

(3.1.5)

{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi,z)={\mathcal{H}}_{W}(z)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[z,z^{-1}]}{\mathcal{A}}(\Psi,z),

as a
\mathbb{C}[z,z^{-1}]-
module.

Theorem 3.1.2

(
[
Lus89
, Proposition 3.11]
)
.

The center of

{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi,z)
is
{\mathcal{Z}}={\mathcal{A}}(\Psi,z)^{W},
i.e. the

W-
invariants in
{\mathcal{A}}(\Psi,z).

Let
{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi)\modd
denote the category of
finite dimensional
{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi,z)
-modules. By Schur’s lemma, every
irreducible module
(\pi,V)
has a
central character
, i.e. there
is a homomorphism
\chi:{\mathcal{Z}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that

\pi(z)v=\chi(z)v
for every
v\in V
and
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}.

By Theorem
3.1.2
, there is a one-to-one correspondence between central characters for
{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi,z)
and
W-
conjugacy classes
(z_{0},s)\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times T
. There is a block a decomposition:

(3.1.6)

{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi,z)\modd=\prod_{(z_{0},s)\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times W\backslash T}{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi,z)\modd_{(z_{0},s)},

where
{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi,z)\modd_{(z_{0},s)}
is the
subcategory of modules with central character
(z_{0},s).

Let
\text{Irr}_{(z_{0},s)}{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi,z)
be the set of isomorphism classes of
simple objects in this category. For the remainder of this section,
we will assume that
z_{0}
is a fixed element of
\mathbb{R}_{>1}.

A second presentation of the affine Hecke algebra is due to Iwahori and Matsumoto
[
IM65
]
(and, in fact, predates the Bernstein presentation). Let
W^{a}=W\ltimes\mathbb{Z}R\subseteq\widetilde{W}
be the affine Weyl group and let
S^{a}
denote a set of reflections of
S^{a}
containing in particular all
s_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\Pi
. The length function on
W^{a}
, still denoted by
\ell
, can be extended to a length function on
\widetilde{W}
. Let
L:S^{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be a
\widetilde{W}
-invariant function.

Definition 3.1.3

(Iwahori presentation)
.

The affine Hecke algebra

\mathcal{H}^{L}(\Psi,z)

associated to the root datum

\Psi
with parameter function
L
is the
associative algebra over
\mathbb{C}[z,z^{-1}]
with unit (
z
is an indeterminate) generated by the elements
T_{w}
,
w\in\widetilde{W}
subject to the relations:

(3.1.7)

\displaystyle(T_{s}+1)(T_{s}-z^{L(s)})=0,\text{ for all }s\in S^{a},

\displaystyle T_{w}T_{w^{\prime}}=T_{ww^{\prime}},\text{ for all }w,w^{\prime}\in\widetilde{W}\text{ such that }\ell(ww^{\prime})=\ell(w)+\ell(w^{\prime}).

For the dictionary between the parameter functions
L
and
(\lambda,\lambda^{*})
as well as the relations between
\theta_{x}
and
T_{w}
, we refer to
[
Lus89
, Section 3]
.

If
J\subsetneq S^{a}
, denote by
\widetilde{W}_{J}
the stabiliser in
\widetilde{W}
of
S^{a}-J
. Let
{\mathcal{H}}_{J}(\Psi,z)
the subalgebra of
{\mathcal{H}}^{L}(\Psi,z)
generated by
T_{w}
,
w\in\widetilde{W}_{J}
. This is a finite Hecke algebra with parameters (possibly extended by diagram automorphims).

3.2.
Graded Hecke algebras

Fix a
W
-orbit
{\mathcal{O}}\subset T
. Following
[
Lus89
, Section 4]
and

[
BM93
, Section 3]
, we will define a decreasing chain of ideals

{\mathcal{I}}^{1},{\mathcal{I}}^{2},...
in
{\mathcal{A}}
. Let

(3.2.1)

{\mathcal{I}}:=\{f\text{ regular function on }\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times T:f(1,\sigma)=0,\text{ for all }\sigma\in{\mathcal{O}}\},

and let
{\mathcal{I}}^{k}
be the ideal of functions vanishing on
(1,{\mathcal{O}})
to at least
order
k.
Let
\widetilde{{\mathcal{I}}}^{k}:={{\mathcal{I}}}^{k}{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{I}}^{k}
,
k\geq 1,
be
the chain of ideals in
{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi)
, generated by the
{\mathcal{I}}^{k}
’s.

Definition 3.2.1
.

The graded affine Hecke algebra
\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{O}}(\Psi)
is the
associated graded of the chain of ideals
\dots\supset\widetilde{{\mathcal{I}}}^{k}\supset\dots
in
{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi).

Let
{\mathfrak{t}}=X^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}
be the Lie algebra of
T,
and let

{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}=X\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}
be the dual space. Extend the pairing

\langle\ ,\ \rangle
to
{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}\times{\mathfrak{t}}.
Let
\mathbf{A}

be the algebra of regular functions on
\mathbb{C}\oplus{\mathfrak{t}}.
Note that

\mathbf{A}
can be identified with
\mathbb{C}[r]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}S({\mathfrak{t}}^{*}),
where

S(~)
denotes the symmetric algebra, and
r
is an indeterminate. In
the following
\delta
denotes the delta function.

Theorem 3.2.2

(
[
Lus89
, Proposition 4.4 and §8.7]
,
[
BM93
, Proposition 3.2]
)
.

The graded Hecke algebra

\mathbf{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}}(\Psi)
is a
\mathbb{C}[r]-
algebra generated by

\{t_{w}:w\in W\}
,
S({\mathfrak{t}}^{*}),

and a set of orthogonal idempotents
\{E_{\sigma}:\sigma\in{\mathcal{O}}\}

subject to the relations

(3.2.2)

\displaystyle t_{w}\cdot t_{w^{\prime}}=t_{ww^{\prime}},\ w,w^{\prime}\in W,\quad\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathcal{O}}}E_{\sigma}=1,\quad E_{\sigma^{\prime}}E_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}=\delta_{\sigma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}}E_{\sigma^{\prime}},

\displaystyle E_{\sigma}t_{s_{\alpha}}=t_{s_{\alpha}}E_{s_{\alpha}\sigma},\ E_{\sigma}\omega=\omega E_{\sigma},

\displaystyle\omega\cdot t_{s_{\alpha}}-t_{s_{\alpha}}\cdot s_{\alpha}(\omega)=rg({\alpha})\langle\omega,\check{\alpha}\rangle,\text{ where }{\alpha}\in\Pi,\ \omega\in{\mathfrak{t}}^{*},

\displaystyle g({\alpha})=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathcal{O}}}E_{\sigma}\mu_{\sigma}({\alpha}),\text{ and }\mu_{\sigma}({\alpha})=\left\{\begin{matrix}0,&\text{ if }s_{\alpha}\sigma\neq\sigma,\\
2\lambda({\alpha}),&\text{ if
}s_{\alpha}\sigma=\sigma,\ \ \check{\alpha}\notin 2X^{\vee},\\
\lambda({\alpha})+\lambda^{*}({\alpha})\theta_{-{\alpha}}(\sigma),&\text{ if
}s_{\alpha}\sigma=\sigma,\ \ \check{\alpha}\in 2X^{\vee}.\\
\end{matrix}\right.

Remark 3.2.3
.

An important special case is when

{\mathcal{O}}
is formed of a single (
W-
invariant) element
\sigma.

Then there is only one
idempotent generator
E_{\sigma}=1
, so it is suppressed from the notation.
The algebra
\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{O}}(\Psi)
is generated by
\{t_{w}:w\in W\}

and
S({\mathfrak{t}}^{*})
subject to the commutation relation

(3.2.3)

\displaystyle\omega\cdot t_{s_{\alpha}}-t_{s_{\alpha}}\cdot s_{\alpha}(\omega)=r\mu_{\sigma}({\alpha})\langle\omega,\check{\alpha}\rangle,\quad{\alpha}\in\Pi,\ \omega\in{\mathfrak{t}}^{*},\text{ where }

\displaystyle\mu_{\sigma}({\alpha})=\left\{\begin{matrix}2\lambda({\alpha}),&\text{ if
}\check{\alpha}\notin 2X^{\vee},\\
\lambda({\alpha})+\lambda^{*}({\alpha})\theta_{-{\alpha}}(\sigma),&\text{ if
}\check{\alpha}\in 2X^{\vee}.\\
\end{matrix}\right.

Still assuming that
\sigma
is
W-
invariant, we have

\theta_{{\alpha}}(\sigma)\in\{\pm 1\},
for all
{\alpha}\in\Pi
. If

\sigma
is in the center of group
G(\Psi)
, then
\theta_{{\alpha}}(\sigma)=1,

for all
{\alpha}\in\Pi.

We will use the notation
\mathbf{H}_{\mu_{\sigma}}(r)
for the
graded affine Hecke
algebra in the
particular case
defined by equations (
3.2.3
).
Define

(3.2.4)

W(\sigma):=\{w\in W:w\sigma=\sigma\}.

Then
W\cdot\sigma=\{w_{j}\cdot\sigma:1\leq j\leq n\}
, where

\{w_{1}=1,w_{2},\dots,w_{n}\}
are coset representatives for

W/{W}(\sigma).
Then
\{E_{\tau}\}=\{E_{w_{j}\sigma}:1\leq j\leq n\},
and from Proposition
3.2.2
,

\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{O}}=\mathbb{C}[W]\otimes({\mathcal{E}}\otimes\mathbf{A})
, as a
\mathbb{C}[r]-
vector
space, where
{\mathcal{E}}
is the algebra generated by the

E_{w_{j}\cdot\sigma}
’s with
1\leq j\leq n.

Proposition 3.2.4

(
[
Lus89
, Proposition 4.5]
)
.

The center of
\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{O}}
is
Z=({\mathcal{E}}\otimes\mathbf{A})^{W}.

It follows that the central characters of
\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{O}}
are
parameterized by
{W}(\sigma)-
orbits in
\mathbb{C}\oplus{\mathfrak{t}}.
Define the category

\text{mod}_{(r_{0},x)}\mathbf{H}_{\sigma},
where
r
acts by
r_{0}>0
and

x\in{\mathfrak{t}}.

We describe the structure of
\mathbf{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}}(\Psi)

in more detail. Fix a
\sigma\in{\mathcal{O}}\subset T.
We define a root datum

\Psi_{\sigma}=(X,R_{\sigma},X^{\vee},R_{\sigma}^{\vee})
with positive roots

R_{\sigma}^{+}
, defined as follows:

(3.2.5)

\displaystyle R_{\sigma}=\left\{{\alpha}\in R:\theta_{\alpha}(\sigma)=\left\{\begin{matrix}1,&\text{ if }\check{\alpha}\notin 2X^{\vee},\\
\pm 1,&\text{ if
}\check{\alpha}\in 2X^{\vee}\end{matrix}\right.\right\},

\displaystyle R_{\sigma}^{+}=R_{\sigma}\cap R^{+},\quad R_{\sigma}^{\vee}=\{\check{\alpha}\in R^{\vee}:{\alpha}\in R_{\sigma}\}.

Note that
\Psi_{\sigma}
is the root datum for
(G(\sigma)_{0},T)
,
with positive roots
R_{\sigma}^{+}
with respect to the Borel subgroup
G(\sigma)_{0}\cap B.

Define

(3.2.6)

\Gamma_{\sigma}:=\{w\in{W}(\sigma):w(R_{\sigma}^{+})=R_{\sigma}^{+}\}.

Note that
\Gamma_{\sigma}
acts on the root datum
\Psi_{\sigma}
and preserves the set of positive roots. Thus, it defines an extended graded Hecke algebra

(3.2.7)

\mathbf{H}^{\prime}_{\mu_{\sigma}}(\Psi_{\sigma}):=\mathbf{H}_{\mu_{\sigma}}(\Psi_{\sigma})\rtimes\Gamma_{\sigma}.

Recall the coset representatives
\{w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\}
for

W/{W}(\sigma)
from the paragraph after (
3.2.4
). Set

(3.2.8)

E_{i,j}=t_{w_{i}^{-1}w_{j}}E_{w_{j}\sigma}=E_{w_{i}\sigma}t_{w_{i}^{-1}w_{j}},\text{ for all }1\leq i,j\leq n,

and let
{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
be the matrix algebra with basis
\{E_{i,j}\}
.

Theorem 3.2.5

(
[
Lus89
, Section 8]
,
[
BM93
, Theorem 3.3]
)
.

There is an algebra isomorphism

\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{O}}(\Psi)\cong{\mathcal{M}}_{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbf{H}_{\mu_{\sigma}}^{\prime}(\Psi_{\sigma})={\mathcal{M}}_{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{H}_{\mu_{\sigma}}(\Psi_{\sigma})\rtimes\Gamma_{\sigma}).

Since the only irreducible representation of
{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
is the

n-
dimensional standard representation, one obtains immediately
the equivalences of categories:

(3.2.9)

\text{mod}_{(r_{0},x)}\mathbf{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}}(\Psi)\cong\text{mod}_{(r_{0},x)}\mathbf{H}_{\mu_{\sigma}}^{\prime}(\Psi_{\sigma}).

3.3.
Reduction theorems

In this section, we recall the relation
between
{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi)
and
\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{O}}(\Psi)
.
The torus
T=X^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}^{\times}
admits a polar decomposition

T=T_{c}\times T_{r},
where
T_{c}=X^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}S^{1},
and

T_{r}=X^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}_{>0}.
Consequently, every
s\in T

decomposes uniquely as
s=s_{c}\cdot s_{h},
with
s_{c}\in T_{c}
and

s_{h}\in T_{r}.
We call an element
s_{c}\in T_{c}
elliptic, and an element

s_{h}\in T_{r}
hyperbolic. Similarly,
{\mathfrak{t}}=X^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}

admits a decomposition
{\mathfrak{t}}={\mathfrak{t}}_{i\mathbb{R}}\oplus{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, where
{\mathfrak{t}}_{i\mathbb{R}}=X^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}i\mathbb{R}
and
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathbb{R}}=X^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}.

There are certain completions of the Hecke algebras which appear in the reduction theorems. The
algebras
\mathbb{C}[r],\ \mathscr{S},
and
\mathbb{C}[r]\otimes\mathscr{S}
consist
of polynomial functions on
\mathbb{C},\ {\mathfrak{t}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}:=\mathbb{C}\oplus{\mathfrak{t}}
, respectively. Let

\widehat{\mathbb{C}[r]}
and
\widehat{\mathscr{S}}

be the corresponding algebras of holomorphic functions. Let
\mathscr{K}
and

\widehat{\mathscr{K}}
be the fields of rational and meromorphic functions
on
{\mathcal{M}}.
Finally set

\widehat{\mathbf{A}}:=\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathscr{S}}\widehat{\mathscr{S}}\subset\widehat{\mathscr{K}},

and

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}:=\mathbb{C}[W]\otimes(\widehat{\mathbb{C}[r]}\otimes\widehat{\mathbf{A}}),\quad\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}({\mathscr{K}}):=\mathbb{C}[W]\otimes({\mathcal{E}}\otimes\mathscr{K})\supset\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathcal{O}},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{\mathscr{K}}):=\mathbb{C}[W]\otimes({\mathcal{E}}\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{K}})\supset\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathscr{K}),\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}.

Theorem 3.3.1

(
[
Lus89
, Proposition 5.2]
and
[
BM93
, Theorem 3.5]
)
.

The map
\iota:\mathbb{C}[W]\ltimes({\mathcal{E}}\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{K}})\rightarrow\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{\mathscr{K}})
, defined by

\iota(E_{\sigma})=E_{\sigma},\quad\iota(f)=f,\ f\in\widehat{\mathscr{K}},\quad\iota(t_{\alpha})=(t_{\alpha}+1)(\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathcal{O}}}{g_{\sigma}({\alpha})^{-1}}E_{\sigma})-1,

where

(3.3.1)

g_{\sigma}({\alpha})=1+\mu_{\sigma}({\alpha}){\alpha}^{-1}\in\widehat{\mathscr{K}},

is an algebra isomorphism.

For every character
\chi
of
{\mathcal{Z}}
(the center of
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{W}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
), there is a maximal
ideal
{\mathcal{J}}_{\chi}=\{z\in{\mathcal{Z}}:\chi(z)=0\}
of
{\mathcal{Z}}.
Consider the
quotients

{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}={\mathcal{A}}/{\mathcal{A}}\cdot{\mathcal{J}}_{\chi},\qquad{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi)_{\chi}={\mathcal{H}}(\Psi)/{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi)\cdot{\mathcal{J}}_{\chi}.

Similarly, consider the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}_{\overline{\chi}}
for every character

\overline{\chi}
of
Z
in
\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{O}}
, and define the analogous
quotients.
Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{\overline{\chi}}:=\mathbf{A}/(\mathbf{A}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{\overline{\chi}})=\widehat{\mathbf{A}}/(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\cdot{{\mathcal{I}}}_{\overline{\chi}})=\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\overline{\chi}},\quad\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\chi}}:=\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{O}}/(\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{O}}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{\overline{\chi}})=\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}/(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}_{\overline{\chi}})=\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{\overline{\chi}}.

The map

\tau:\mathbb{C}\oplus{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathbb{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times T,\quad(r_{0},\nu)\mapsto(z_{0},s)=(e^{r_{0}},\sigma\cdot e^{\nu})

is
W(\sigma)-
invariant. It matches the central characters

(3.3.2)

\tau:~\overline{\chi}=W(\sigma)\cdot(r_{0},\nu)\longleftrightarrow\chi=W\cdot(z_{0},s).

Moreover,
\tau
is a bijection onto the central characters of
{\mathcal{H}}
with elliptic part in
{\mathcal{O}}.

Theorem 3.3.2

(
[
BM93
, Proposition 4.1, Theorem 4.3]
)
.

(1)

The map
\phi:{\mathcal{A}}[z,z^{-1}]\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathbb{C}}[r]\otimes\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
defined by

(3.3.3)

\displaystyle\phi(z)=e^{r},\quad\phi(\theta_{x})=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathcal{O}}}\theta_{x}(\sigma)E_{\sigma}e^{x},\qquad x\in X,

is a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra homomorphism which maps
{\mathcal{J}}_{\chi}
to
{\mathcal{I}}_{\overline{\chi}}
and defines by passage to the quotients an isomorphism
{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}\cong\mathbf{A}_{\overline{\chi}}.

(2)

The map
\Phi:{\mathcal{H}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{\mathscr{K}})

defined by

(3.3.4)

\displaystyle\Phi(a)=\phi(a),\quad\Phi(T_{\alpha}+1)=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathcal{O}}}E_{\sigma}(t_{\alpha}+1){\phi({\mathcal{G}}_{\alpha})g_{\sigma}({\alpha})^{-1}}

with
{\mathcal{G}}_{\alpha}
as in Definition
3.1.1
, and
g_{\sigma}({\alpha})
as in
(
3.3.1
), induces an isomorphism
{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi)_{\chi}\cong(\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{O}}(\Psi))_{\overline{\chi}}.

(3)

Assume

\sigma\in T_{c}.
Let
\chi=W\cdot(e^{r_{0}},\sigma\cdot e^{\nu})
,

\overline{\chi}=W(\sigma)\cdot(r_{0},\nu)
be as in
(
3.3.2
), with
(r_{0},\nu)\in\mathbb{C}\oplus{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathbb{R}}.
The
map (
3.3.4
)

\Phi:{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi)_{\chi}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbf{H}_{\mathcal{O}}(\Psi)_{\overline{\chi}}

is
an analytic isomorphism in
(r_{0},\nu)\in\mathbb{C}\oplus{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathbb{R}}
between two families of analytic
\mathbb{C}
-algebras, in the sense of
[
BM93
, Definition 4.2]
.

If
\sigma\in T_{c}
, denote by
r_{\sigma}
the bijection defined by
\Phi
between simple
\mathbf{H}_{\mu_{\sigma}}(\Psi_{\sigma})\rtimes\Gamma_{\sigma}
-modules with real central character and simple
\mathcal{H}(\Psi,z_{0})
-modules with central character having compact part in
W\cdot\sigma
.

3.4.
Tempered modules

Let
V
be a

{\mathcal{H}}(\Psi,z)-
module on which
z
acts by
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{>1}
. For every
t\in T,
define a
{\mathcal{A}}
-generalized eigenspace
of
V
:

(3.4.1)

V_{t}=\{v\in V:\text{ for all }x\in X,\ (x(t)-\theta_{x})^{k}v=0,\text{
for some }k\geq 0\}.

We say that
t
is a weight of
V
if
V_{t}\neq\{0\}.
Let
\Phi(V)

denote the set of weights of
V.
We have
V=\oplus_{t\in\Phi(V)}V_{t}
as
{\mathcal{A}}
-modules.

Definition 3.4.1
.

We say that
V
is tempered if, for all
x\in X^{+}:=\{x\in X:\langle x,\check{\alpha}\rangle\geq 0,\text{ for all }{\alpha}\in R^{+}\}
, and all

t\in\Phi(V),

|x(t)|\leq 1
holds.

Let
\overline{V}
be a

\mathbf{H}_{\mu_{\sigma}}^{\prime}(\Psi_{\sigma})-
module on which
r
acts by

r_{0}>0.
For every
\nu\in{\mathfrak{t}},
define a
\mathbf{A}
-generalized eigenspace
of
\overline{V}
,

(3.4.2)

\overline{V}_{\nu}=\{v\in\overline{V}:\text{for all }\omega\in S({\mathfrak{t}}^{*}),\ (\omega(\nu)-\omega)^{k}v=0,\text{ for some }k\geq 0\}.

We say that
\nu
is a weight of
\overline{V}
if
\overline{V}_{\nu}\neq\{0\}.
Let
\Phi(\overline{V})
denote the set of weights
of
\overline{V}.
We have

\overline{V}=\oplus_{\nu\in\Phi(\overline{V})}V_{\nu},
as
\mathbf{A}
-modules.

Definition 3.4.2
.

We say that
\overline{V}
is tempered if, for all
\omega\in\mathbf{A}^{+}:=\{\omega\in S({\mathfrak{t}}^{*}):\langle\omega,\check{\alpha}\rangle\geq 0,\text{ for all }{\alpha}\in R^{+}\}
, and all
\nu\in\Phi(\overline{V})
,
we have
\langle\omega,\nu\rangle\leq 0.

The two notions of tempered are naturally related, see for example
[
BM93
, Sections 6, 7]
: under the bijection
r_{\sigma}
from
Theorem
3.3.2
, the tempered modules correspond.

3.5.
Deformations of standard modules

We make use of certain continuous deformations of affine Hecke algebras and their Schwarz completions due to Solleveld
[
Sol12
, §4]
, based also on an idea of Opdam
[
Opd04
]
. Let
\mathcal{H}(\Psi,z_{0})
be the specialisation of the generic affine Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(\Psi,z_{0})

at
z=z_{0}>1
. There is a family of additive functors

(3.5.1)

\widetilde{\sigma}_{\epsilon}:\text{mod}\mathcal{H}(\Psi,z_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\text{mod}\mathcal{H}(\Psi,z_{0}^{\epsilon}),\qquad\widetilde{\sigma}_{\epsilon}(\pi,V)=(\pi_{\epsilon},V),\qquad\epsilon\in[-1,1]

such that for each
w\in\widetilde{W}
, the map

[-1,1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\operatorname{End}(V),\ \epsilon\mapsto\pi_{\epsilon}(T_{w})

is analytic in
\epsilon
. The functors
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\epsilon}
are defined by

\pi_{\epsilon}=\pi\circ\rho_{\epsilon},

see
[
Sol12
, Proposition 4.1.2]
, where
\rho_{\epsilon}
is a scaling map (which is an isomorphism of topological algebras when
\epsilon\neq 0
) between certain analytic completions of
\mathcal{H}(\Psi,z_{0}^{\epsilon})
and
\mathcal{H}(\Psi,z_{0})
, such that the maps
\epsilon\mapsto\rho_{\epsilon}(T_{w})
are analytic for all
w\in\widetilde{W}
.
We summarize the properties of
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\epsilon}
.

Theorem 3.5.1

(
[
Sol12
, Corollary 4.2.2 ]
)
.

The functors
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\epsilon}
:

(1)

map modules with central character
s\in W\backslash T
to modules with the same central character;

(2)

are equivalences of categories if
\epsilon\neq 0
;

(3)

map tempered modules to tempered modules when
\epsilon\geq 0
.

We are particularly interested in the functor
\widetilde{\sigma}_{0}
. For finite-dimensional tempered modules, a precise description is available. Let

(3.5.2)

\zeta^{*}:\{\text{simple tempered $\mathcal{H}(\Psi,z_{0})$-modules}\}\longrightarrow\{\text{tempered $\mathbb{C}[X\rtimes W]$-modules}\}

denote the restriction of
\widetilde{\sigma}_{0}
to the set of tempered modules. Fix a compact semisimple element
\sigma\in T_{c}
, and let
r_{\sigma}
denote the corresponding bijection given after Theorem
3.3.2
.

Theorem 3.5.2

(
[
Sol12
, Theorem 2.3.1, Corollary 4.4.3]
)
.

If
\widetilde{\pi}
is any simple tempered
\mathbf{H}_{\mu_{\sigma}}(\Psi_{\sigma})\rtimes\Gamma_{\sigma}
-module with real central character and
\widetilde{\pi}\circ r_{\sigma}
the corresponding simple tempered
\mathcal{H}(\Psi,z_{0})
-module, then

\zeta^{*}(\widetilde{\pi}\circ r_{\sigma})=\operatorname{Ind}_{X\rtimes W(\sigma)}^{X\rtimes W}(\mathbb{C}_{\sigma}\otimes\widetilde{\pi}|_{W(\sigma)}).

Let
J\subsetneq S^{a}
be given. The finite Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{J}(\Psi,z_{0}^{\epsilon})
is semisimple and finite dimensional with the underlying vector space
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{W}_{J}]
. Since the multiplication varies continuously with
\epsilon
, it follows by Tits’ deformation theorem, that the algebras
\mathcal{H}_{J}(\Psi,z_{0}^{\epsilon})
are isomorphic for all
\epsilon
.
Moreover since the maps
\epsilon\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\pi_{\epsilon}(T_{w})
are continuous in
\epsilon
, it follows that the multiplicities of the restrictions of
\pi_{\epsilon}
to
\mathcal{H}_{J}(\Psi,z_{0}^{\epsilon})
are constant with respect to
\epsilon
. We record these facts in the following corollary.

Corollary 3.5.3
.

Let
\tau
be a simple
\mathcal{H}_{J}(\Psi,z_{0})
-module and let
\tau|_{z_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1}
be the corresponding simple
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{W}_{J}]
-module. Then, for every finite-dimensional
\mathcal{H}(\Psi,z_{0})
-module
\pi
,

\Hom_{\mathcal{H}_{J}(\Psi,z_{0})}[\tau,\pi]=\Hom_{\widetilde{W}_{J}}[\tau|_{z_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1},\widetilde{\sigma}_{0}(\pi)].

In particular, for every simple tempered
\pi=\widetilde{\pi}\circ r_{\sigma}
-module as in Theorem
3.5.2
,

(3.5.3)

\Hom_{\mathcal{H}_{J}(\Psi,z_{0})}[\tau,\pi]=\Hom_{\widetilde{W}_{J}}[\tau|_{z_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1},\zeta^{*}(\pi)]=\Hom_{\widetilde{W}_{J}}[\tau|_{z_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1},\operatorname{Ind}_{X\rtimes W(\sigma)}^{X\rtimes W}(\mathbb{C}_{\sigma}\otimes\widetilde{\pi}|_{W(\sigma)})].

3.6.
Parabolic subalgebras

For
P\subseteq\Pi
, define

R_{P}=\Pi\cap\mathbb{Q}P,\quad R_{P}^{\vee}=R^{\vee}\cap\mathbb{Q}P^{\vee},\quad X_{P}=X/X\cap(P^{\vee})^{\perp},\quad X_{P}^{\vee}=X^{\vee}\cap\mathbb{Q}P^{\vee}.

Let
\Psi^{P}=(X,X^{\vee},R_{P},R_{P}^{\vee})
and
\Psi_{P}=(X_{P},X_{P}^{\vee},R_{P},R_{P}^{\vee})
be the corresponding root data. The parameters
\lambda,\lambda^{*}
of
\Psi
give rise by restriction to certain parameters for
\Psi^{P}
,
\Psi_{P}
, see
[
Sol12
, §1.4, (1.3)]
. Denote the resulting affine Hecke algebras by
\mathcal{H}_{P}=\mathcal{H}(\Psi_{P},z_{0})
and
\mathcal{H}^{P}=\mathcal{H}(\Psi^{P},z_{0})
. The algebra
\mathcal{H}^{P}
is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{H}(\Psi,z_{0})
and there is a natural surjective algebra homomorphism

\mathcal{H}^{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{H}_{P},\quad\theta_{x}\mapsto\theta_{x_{P}},\ T_{w}\mapsto T_{w},\ x\in X,\ w\in W,

arising from the natural projection
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X_{P}
,
x\mapsto x_{P}
.

For every
t\in T^{P}:=\Hom[X^{P},\mathbb{C}^{\times}]
, we may define an algebra automorphism

\chi_{t}:\mathcal{H}^{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{H}^{P},\quad\chi_{t}(\theta_{x})=t(x)\theta_{x},\ \chi_{t}(T_{w})=T_{w}.

Every finite-dimensional
\mathcal{H}_{P}
-module
\pi_{P}
can be regarded as an
\mathcal{H}^{P}
-module by pullback and we may define the parabolically induced representations

(3.6.1)

I(P,\pi_{P},t)=\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathcal{H}^{P}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\pi_{P}\circ\chi_{t}),\quad t\in T^{P}.

Since the action varies continuously in
t
, the restriction of
I(P,\pi_{P},t)
to any finite Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{J}(\Psi,z_{0})
is independent of
t
. It is clear from the definitions that if
\pi_{P}
is a simple tempered module for
\mathcal{H}_{P}
, then
I(P,\pi_{P},1)=\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathcal{H}^{P}}^{\mathcal{H}}(\pi_{P})
is a (not necessarily simple) tempered module for
\mathcal{H}
. Hence Corollary
3.5.3
implies at once:

Proposition 3.6.1
.

Let
\pi_{P}
be a simple tempered
\mathcal{H}_{P}
-module,
t\in T^{P}
, and
J\subsetneq S^{a}
. Then for every simple
\mathcal{H}_{J}(\Psi,z_{0})
-module
\tau
,

(3.6.2)

\Hom_{\mathcal{H}_{J}(\Psi,z_{0})}[\tau,I(P,\pi_{P},t)]=\Hom_{\widetilde{W}_{J}}[\tau|_{z_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1},\zeta^{*}(I(P,\pi_{P},1))],

which can be computed using Corollary
3.5.3
.

3.7.
Geometric graded Hecke algebras

We analyze the graded Hecke algebra
\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{H}_{\mu_{\sigma}}(\Psi_{\sigma})
defined by
the relations in (
3.2.3
) for the case
\sigma=1
. We will consider only the Hecke algebras
arising by the constructions of
[
Lus88
,
Lus95a
]
.
Let
G
be a complex connected
reductive group, with a fixed Borel subgroup
B
,
and maximal torus
A\subset B
. The Lie algebras will be denoted by
the corresponding Gothic letters.

Definition 3.7.1
.

A cuspidal triple for
G
is a triple
(L,\mathbb{O},\mathcal{E}),
where
L
is a
Levi subgroup of
G,

\mathbb{O}
is a nilpotent adjoint
L
-orbit on the Lie
algebra
\mathfrak{l}
, and
\mathcal{E}
is an irreducible
G
-equivariant local
system on
\mathbb{O}
which is cuspidal in the sense of
[
Lus84a
, Definition 2.4]
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}(G)
denote the set of
G
-conjugacy
classes of cuspidal triples for
G
. Let us fix
(L,\mathbb{O},\mathcal{E})\in\mathfrak{L}(G)
, such that
A\subset L,

and
P=LU\supset B
is a parabolic subgroup. Let

T
denote the identity component of the center of
L
. Set

W=N_{G}(L)/L
, a (finite) Coxeter group.

Let
\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{H}(L,\mathbb{O},\mathcal{E})
denote the graded affine Hecke algebra attached to
(L,\mathbb{O},\mathcal{E})
in
[
Lus88
, section 2]
. The
explicit classification of cuspidal triples when
G
is simple,
along with the corresponding values for the parameters of the graded Hecke algebra can
be found in the tables of
[
Lus88
, §2.13]
.

Consider the algebraic varieties

(3.7.1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}=\{(x,g)\in\mathfrak{g}\times G:\Ad(g^{-1})x\in\mathbb{O}+{\mathfrak{t}}+\mathfrak{u}\},\quad\dot{\mathfrak{g}}=\{(x,gP)\in\mathfrak{g}\times G/P:(x,g)\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}\},

on which
G\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
acts via
(g_{1},\lambda)
:
x\mapsto\lambda^{-2}\text{Ad}(g_{1})x,

x\in\mathfrak{g},
and
gP\mapsto g_{1}gP,

g\in G.
Let
\mathrm{pr}_{P}:\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\dot{\mathfrak{g}}
denote the obvious
G\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant map.

If
{\mathcal{V}}
is any
G\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-stable subvariety of
\mathfrak{g},
denote by
\dot{{\mathcal{V}}}
the preimage under the first
projection
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:\dot{\mathfrak{g}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{g}
. Let
\mathcal{N}
denote the variety of nilpotent elements in

\mathfrak{g}.
Define

{\mathcal{P}}_{n}=\mathrm{pr}_{2}(\dot{\{n\}})=\{gP:~\Ad(g^{-1})n\in\mathbb{O}+\mathfrak{u}\},

for any
n\in\mathcal{N},
where

\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\dot{\mathfrak{g}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G/P
is the second projection. Define also
{\mathcal{P}}_{n}^{s}=\{gP\in{\mathcal{P}}_{n}:\Ad(g^{-1})s\in\mathfrak{p}\},

for any semisimple
s\in\mathfrak{g}
.

Consider the
G\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant projection
\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{O}}:\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
,
\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{O}}(x,g)=\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{O}}(\Ad(g^{-1})x)
. Let
\dot{\mathcal{E}}
be the

G\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant local system on
\dot{\mathfrak{g}}
defined by
the condition
\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{O}}^{*}(\mathcal{E})=\mathrm{pr}_{P}^{*}(\dot{\mathcal{E}}).

The classification of simple modules
for
\mathbf{H}
is in
[
Lus88
,
Lus95a
,
Lus02a
]
.
Fix a semisimple element
s\in\mathfrak{g}
and
r_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, and let
{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{T}}_{s,r_{0}}
be the smallest torus in

G\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
whose Lie algebra contains
(s,r_{0}).
Let

\mathfrak{g}_{2r_{0}}
be the set of
{\mathcal{T}}-
fixed vectors in
\mathfrak{g},
namely

(3.7.2)

\mathfrak{g}_{2r_{0}}=\{x\in\mathfrak{g}:~[s,x]=2r_{0}x\}.

Let
G(s)\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be the centralizer of
(s,r_{0})
in

G\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}.
The group
G(s)
is connected (in fact, a Levi subgroup of

G
).
Denote

M(n)=C_{G\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}(n)=\{(x,\lambda):\Ad(x)n=\lambda^{2}n\},

and let
M(n)^{\circ}
be its identity component.

If
Q
is an algebraic group,
X
a
Q
-variety, and
{\mathcal{L}}
a
Q
-equivariant local system on
X
, let
H_{\bullet}^{Q}(X,{\mathcal{L}})
denote the equivariant homology groups as in
[
Lus88
, §1]
. In particular, if
Q=\{1\}
, then

(3.7.3)

H_{j}(X,{\mathcal{L}}):=H^{\{1\}}_{j}(X,{\mathcal{L}})=H^{2\dim X-j}_{c}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{*})^{*}.

The construction of standard modules using equivariant homology
H_{\bullet}^{M(n)^{\circ}}
is in §8 in
loc. cit.
, see also
[
Lus95a
, 10.7, 10.12]
). It is shown that for
n\in\mathfrak{g}_{2r_{0}}
, the space

Y_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n)=\mathbb{C}_{(s,r_{0})}\otimes_{H^{\bullet}_{M(n)^{\circ}}}H_{\bullet}^{M(n)^{\circ}}({\mathcal{P}}_{n},\dot{\mathcal{E}})\cong H_{\bullet}^{\{1\}}({\mathcal{P}}_{n}^{s},\dot{\mathcal{E}})

carries an action of
A(s,n)\times\mathbf{H}
. In particular, the groups
H_{\bullet}({\mathcal{P}}_{n},\dot{\mathcal{E}})
carry an
A(n)\times W
-action which is compatible via the identification (
3.7.3
), and with the appropriate normalization, with the action defined in
[
Lus84a
]
.
We normalize the
W
-action so that
H_{0}({\mathcal{P}}_{n},\dot{\mathcal{E}})=\mathsf{triv}
when
n
is the
smallest
nilpotent orbit that occurs in this setting.

Let
\widehat{A(n)}_{\mathcal{E}}
denote the irreducible representations that occur in the action of
A(n)
on
H^{\{1\}}({\mathcal{P}}_{n},\dot{\mathcal{E}})
. We have a natural injection
A(s,n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}A(n)
and let
\widehat{A(s,n)}_{\mathcal{E}}
denote the irreducible
A(s,n)
-representations that occur in the restrictions of
\widehat{A(n)}_{\mathcal{E}}
.
For every
\rho\in\widehat{A(s,n)}
, define the geometric
standard module

(3.7.4)

Y_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n,\rho)=\Hom_{A(s,n)}[\rho,Y_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n)].

By

[
Lus88
, Proposition 8.10]
,
Y_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n,\rho)\neq 0
if and
only if
\rho\in\widehat{A(s,n)}_{\mathcal{E}}.

One can phrase the
classification as follows.

Theorem 3.7.2

(
[
Lus95a
, §8.10, 8.14, 8.17]
,
[
Lus02a
, Theorem 1.15]
)
.

The standard module
Y_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n,\rho),

s\in\mathfrak{g}
semisimple,
n\in\mathfrak{g}_{2r_{0}}
,
\rho\in\widehat{A(s,n)}_{\mathcal{E}}

has a unique irreducible quotient
X_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n,\rho).

This induces a natural one-to-one correspondence

(3.7.5)

\displaystyle\textup{Irr}_{r_{0}}\mathbf{H}(L,\mathbb{O},\mathcal{E})\leftrightarrow\{(s,n,\rho):[s,e]=2r_{0}e,\ s\in\mathfrak{g}\text{ semisimple},~n\in\mathcal{N},~\widehat{A(s,n)}_{\mathcal{E}}\}/G.

We are interested in the
W-
structure of
standard modules.

Lemma 3.7.3

(
[
Lus88
]
,10.13)
.

There is an isomorphism of
W
-representations

(3.7.6)

\displaystyle Y_{\mathbf{H}}{(s,n,\rho)}=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{A(s,n)}[\rho,H_{\bullet}({\mathcal{P}}_{n}^{s},\dot{\mathcal{E}})]=\operatorname{Hom}_{A(s,n)}[\rho,H_{\bullet}({\mathcal{P}}_{n},\dot{\mathcal{E}})].

Denote

(3.7.7)

\mu(n,\tilde{\rho}):=\operatorname{Hom}_{A(n)}[\tilde{\rho},H_{0}({\mathcal{P}}_{n},\dot{\mathcal{E}})],\quad\tilde{\rho}\in\widehat{A(n)}_{\mathcal{E}},

an irreducible
W
-representation. The
correspondence
\widehat{A(n)}_{\mathcal{E}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widehat{W},

(n,\tilde{\rho})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mu(n,\tilde{\rho})
is a bijection.

The results from
[
Lus84a
, §6.5]
,
[
Lus86
, §24.4]
, see also the summary in
[
BC13
, Theorem 3.4.1]
, imply the following
W
-character formula:

(3.7.8)

\operatorname{Hom}_{A(n)}[\tilde{\rho},H_{\bullet}({\mathcal{P}}_{n},\dot{\mathcal{E}})]=\mu(n,\tilde{\rho})+\sum_{(n^{\prime},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime})}m_{(n,\tilde{\rho}),(n^{\prime},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime})}\mu(n^{\prime},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}),

where
m_{(n,\tilde{\rho}),(n^{\prime},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime})}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}\in\widehat{A(n^{\prime})}_{\mathcal{E}}
, and
n^{\prime}
ranges over a set of representatives of
G
-orbits in
\mathcal{N}
such that
n\in\overline{G\cdot n^{\prime}}\setminus{G\cdot n^{\prime}}
. Combining this with Lemma
3.7.3
, we arrive at a character formula for the standard modules.

Proposition 3.7.4
.

Let
(s,n,\rho)
be as in Theorem
3.7.2
. As
W
-representations,

Y_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n,\rho)=\sum_{\tilde{\rho}\in\widehat{A(n)}_{\mathcal{E}}}m(\rho,\tilde{\rho})\mu(n,\tilde{\rho})+\sum_{(n^{\prime},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime})}m_{(n,\tilde{\rho}),(n^{\prime},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime})}\mu(n^{\prime},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}),

where
m(\rho,\tilde{\rho})=\dim\Hom_{A(s,n)}[\rho,\tilde{\rho}|_{A(s,n)}]
,
m_{(n,\tilde{\rho}),(n^{\prime},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime})}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}\in\widehat{A(n^{\prime})}_{\mathcal{E}}
, and
n^{\prime}
ranges over a set of representatives of
G
-orbits in
\mathcal{N}
such that
n\in\overline{G\cdot n^{\prime}}\setminus{G\cdot n^{\prime}}
.

Finally, we record the classification of tempered modules with real central character, see
[
BC13
, §3.5]
for a summary of Lusztig’s results
[
Lus02a
]
. If
n\in\mathcal{N}_{G}
, let
\{\bar{n},h,n\}
be a Lie triple for
n
. Then
A(h,n)=A(n)
, so we may identify
\widehat{A(n)}_{\mathcal{E}}=\widehat{A(h,n)}_{\mathcal{E}}.

Theorem 3.7.5
.

The map

(3.7.9)

\{(n,\rho):e\in\mathcal{N},\rho\in\widehat{A(n)}_{\mathcal{E}}\}/G\longleftrightarrow X_{\mathbf{H}}{(r_{0}h,n,\rho)}=Y_{\mathbf{H}}(r_{0}h,n,\rho),

is a one-to-one correspondence onto the set of isomorphism classes of
tempered irreducible
\mathbf{H}(L,\mathbb{O},\mathcal{E})
- modules with real central
character, on which
r
acts by
r_{0}
.

4.
Lusztig’s arithmetic-geometric correspondence

We assume in this section that
{\mathbf{G}}
is adjoint simple.

4.1.
Representations with unipotent cuspidal support

We briefly recall the classification of irreducible representations with unipotent cuspidal support defined in
[
Lus95
, §1.6,§1.21]
. With the notation as in section
2.2
, let
(\pi,V)
be a
\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k})
-representation. We say
V
has
unipotent cuspidal support
if there exists a parahoric subgroup
\mathbf{P}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})\subset\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k})
and an irreducible unipotent cuspidal representation
(\sigma_{\mathbf{E}},\mathbf{E})
of
\mathbf{L}_{c}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
such that the
\mathbf{E}
-isotypic component of
V|_{P_{c}(\mathfrak{o})}
generates
V
. (By abuse of notation, we denote by
\mathbf{E}
also the pullback of
\mathbf{E}
to
\mathbf{P}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})
). Let
\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k}),\bf E)
denote the corresponding full subcategory of smooth
\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k})
-representations. The assignment

\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{E}}:V\mapsto\Hom_{\mathbf{P}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})}(\mathbf{E},V)

defines an equivalence of categories between
\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k}),\mathbf{E})
and the category of modules for the convolution algebra

\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k}),\mathbf{E})=\{f:\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\End_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{E})\mid f(p_{1}gp_{2})=\sigma_{\mathbf{E}}(p_{1})\circ f(g)\circ\sigma_{\mathbf{E}}(p_{2}),\ g\in\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k}),p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathbf{P}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})\}.

Let
G^{\vee}={\mathbf{G}}^{\vee}(\mathbb{C})
and let
T^{\vee}=X^{*}(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. The polar decomposition of
T^{\vee}
is
T^{\vee}=T^{\vee}_{c}T^{\vee}_{r}
, where
T^{\vee}_{c}=X^{*}(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}S^{1}
and
T^{\vee}_{r}=X^{*}(\mathbf{T},{\bar{\mathsf{k}}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

Write
\Phi(G^{\vee})
for the set of
G^{\vee}
-orbits (under conjugation) of triples
(s,n,\rho)
where

•

s\in G^{\vee}
is semisimple,

•

n\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
such that
\operatorname{Ad}(s)n=qn
,

•

\rho\in\mathrm{Irr}(A_{G^{\vee}}(s,n))
.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
s\in T^{\vee}
. Note that
n\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
is necessarily nilpotent. The group
G^{\vee}(s)
acts with finitely many orbits on the
q
-eigenspace of
\Ad(s)

\mathfrak{g}_{q}^{\vee}=\{x\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\mid\operatorname{Ad}(s)x=qx\}.

In particular, there is a unique open
G^{\vee}(s)
-orbit in
\mathfrak{g}_{q}^{\vee}
.

Fix an
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple
\{n^{-},h^{\vee},n\}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
with
h^{\vee}\in\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}_{\mathbb{R}}
and set

s_{0}:=sq^{-\frac{h^{\vee}}{2}}.

Then
\operatorname{Ad}(s_{0})n=n
.

A parameter
(s,n,\rho)\in\Phi(G^{\vee})
is called
discrete
if
{G^{\vee}}(s,n)
does not contain a nontrivial torus.
A discrete parameter is called
cuspidal
if
(n,\rho)
is a cuspidal pair in
{G^{\vee}}(s)
in the sense of section
3.7
.
Let
\Pi^{\mathsf{Lus}}({\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k}))
denote the set of equivalence classes of irreducible
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k})
-representations with unipotent cuspidal support and let

\Pi^{\mathsf{Lus}}({\mathbf{G}})=\bigsqcup_{\omega\in\Omega_{\mathsf{ad}}}\Pi^{\mathsf{Lus}}({\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k})).

(In this subsection,
{\mathbf{G}}^{1}(\mathsf{k})
is the split form.)
The following theorem is a combination of several results, namely
[
KL87
, Theorems 7.12, 8.2, 8.3]
for Iwahori-spherical representations, and
[
Lus95
, Corollary 6.5]
and
[
Lus02
, Theorem 10.5]
for representations with unipotent cuspidal support. See
[
AMS21
, §2.3]
for a discussion of the compatibility between these classifications.

Theorem 4.1.1

(Deligne-Langlands-Lusztig correspondence)
.

There is a bijection

\Phi(G^{\vee})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Pi^{\mathsf{Lus}}({\mathbf{G}}),\qquad(s,n,\rho)\mapsto X(s,n,\rho),
