with the following properties:

(1)

X(s,n,\rho)
is tempered if and only if
s_{0}\in T_{c}^{\vee}
(in particular,
\overline{G^{\vee}(s)n}=\mathfrak{g}_{q}^{\vee}
),

(2)

X(s,n,\rho)
is square integrable (modulo center) if and only if it is tempered and
(s,n,\rho)
is discrete.

(3)

X(s,n,\rho)
is supercuspidal if and only if
(s,n,\rho)
is a cuspidal parameter.

(4)

X(s,n,\rho)\in\Pi^{\mathsf{Lus}}({\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k}))
if and only if
\rho|_{Z(G^{\vee})}
is a multiple of
\zeta_{\omega}
.

For each parameter
(s,n,\rho)
, there is an associated admissible
standard module

Y(s,n,\rho)
with the following properties, see
[
Lus02
, Section 10]
, which relies on the graded affine Hecke algebra results in
[
Lus02a
, Theorems 1.15, 1.21, 1.22]
):

(1)

X(s,n,\rho)
is the unique simple quotient of
Y(s,n,\rho)
;

(2)

If
\overline{G^{\vee}(s)n}=\mathfrak{g}_{q}^{\vee}
, then
X(s,n,\rho)=Y(s,n,\rho)
; in particular, this is the case when
X(s,n,\rho)
is tempered. More generally:

(3)

There is an identity of characters (
[
Lus95a
, Corollary 10.7]
)

(4.1.1)

Y(s,n,\rho)=X(s,n,\rho)+\sum_{(n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}m((n,\rho),(n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}))X(s,n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}),

where
m((n,\rho),(n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}))\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and the sum is over all
G^{\vee}(s)
-orbits
(n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
, such that
n\in\overline{G^{\vee}(s)n^{\prime}}
.

We denote by
\Pi^{\mathsf{Lus}}_{s}({\mathbf{G}}(\mathsf{k}))
the set of irreducible
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathsf{k})
-representations
X(s,n,\rho)
for a fixed
s\in T^{\vee}
.
We say that
X(s,n,\rho)
has
real infinitesimal character
if
s\in T^{\vee}_{r}.

Remark 4.1.2
.

When
{\mathbf{G}}
is not adjoint, the parameters of the unipotent representations take a similar form
(s,n,\rho)
, see
[
Sol18
]
, except
\rho
is an irreducible representation of the group
A^{1}_{G^{\vee}}(s,n)
, rather than
A_{G^{\vee}}(s,n)
. To define
A^{1}_{G^{\vee}}(s,n)
, let
G^{\vee}_{\mathsf{sc}}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\vee}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\vee}_{\mathsf{ad}}
be the chain of isogenies, and let
Z^{1}_{G^{\vee}}(s)
be the preimage in
G^{\vee}
of the image of
G^{\vee}(s)
in
G^{\vee}_{\mathsf{ad}}.
Then
A^{1}_{G^{\vee}}(s,n)
is the group of components of the centralizer of
n
in
Z^{1}_{G^{\vee}}(s)
.

If
s\in T^{\vee}_{r}
, let
\bar{s}
be the unique element of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}_{r}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
that exponentiates to
s
. Then
G^{\vee}(s)=C_{G^{\vee}}(\bar{s})
, and
Z^{1}_{G^{\vee}}(s)=C_{G^{\vee}_{\mathsf{sc}}}(\bar{s})
, and therefore
A^{1}(s,n)=A_{G^{\vee}_{\mathsf{sc}}}(\bar{s},n)
. In other words, the classification on unipotent representations with real infinitesimal character is independent of isogeny. We will use this implicitly in Section
6
.

4.2.
The arithmetic side

We follow
[
Lus92
]
. Let
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k}),\mathbf{E})
be a Hecke algebra as in Section
4.1
. Let
J\in{\mathbf{P}}^{\omega}(I)
be the subset of the affine simple roots corresponding to
\mathbf{P}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})
, and let
\widetilde{W}_{J}
be the finite subgroup of
\widetilde{W}
generated by the simple reflections in
J
. Write
\mathcal{W}=N_{\widetilde{W}}(\widetilde{W}_{J})/\widetilde{W}_{J}
. As a set,
\mathcal{W}
can be identified with the set of minimal-length
(\widetilde{W}_{J},\widetilde{W}_{J})
-double cosets in
\widetilde{W}
which are contained in
N_{\widetilde{W}}(\widetilde{W}_{J})
. Let
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
(resp.
\overline{\Omega}
) denote the subgroup of
\mathcal{W}
given by the double cosets contained in
W^{a}
(resp.
\Omega
).

The element
\omega\in\Omega
acts on
I
and preserves
J
. Let
\mathcal{W}^{\omega}
,
{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}^{\omega}
,
\overline{\Omega}^{\omega}
denote the fixed points. Let
(I-J)/\omega
denote the orbits of the action of
\omega
on
I-J
. The group
\overline{\Omega}^{\omega}
acts on
(I-J)/\omega
by permutations. Let
\overline{\Omega}^{\omega}_{1}
(resp.
\overline{\Omega}^{\omega}_{2}
) denote the kernel (resp. the image) of the resulting homomorphism
\overline{\Omega}^{\omega}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Perm}(I-J/\omega)
, see
[
Lus92
, §1.20]
.

The affine Coxeter group
{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}^{\omega}
has generators
s_{\underline{k}}
, where
{\underline{k}}
ranges over
(I-J)/\omega
if this set has cardinality at least two. Otherwise
{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}^{\omega}=1
. Let
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(I,J,\omega,\mathbf{E})
be the affine Hecke algebra with parameters
L(\underline{k})
constructed in
[
Lus92
, §1.18]
for the extended affine Weyl group

(4.2.1)

\mathcal{W}(J,\omega,\mathbf{E}):={\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}^{\omega}\rtimes\overline{\Omega}^{\omega}_{2}.

Then

(4.2.2)

\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k}),\mathbf{E})=\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(I,J,\omega,\mathbf{E})\otimes\mathcal{C}[\overline{\Omega}^{\omega}_{1}].

This can be rewritten as
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k}),\mathbf{E})=\bigoplus_{\psi\in\widehat{\overline{\Omega}^{\omega}_{1}}}\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(I,J,\omega,\mathbf{E})
. Each (conjugacy class of) triple
(J,\mathbf{E},\psi)
as above is called an
arithmetic diagram
for
\mathbf{G}^{\omega}
with associated affine Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(I,J,\omega,\mathbf{E})
(independent of
\psi
). Let
\bar{\mathfrak{U}}_{\omega}
denote the set of arithmetic diagrams
(J,\mathbf{E},\psi)
of
\mathbf{G}^{\omega}
and set
\bar{\mathfrak{U}}=\sqcup_{\omega\in\Omega}\bar{\mathfrak{U}}_{\omega}
.

There is a natural one-to-one correspondence (coming from an equivalence of categories)

(4.2.3)

\Pi^{\mathsf{Lus}}(\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k}))\longleftrightarrow\bigsqcup_{(J,\mathbf{E},\psi)\in\bar{\mathfrak{U}}_{\omega}}\mathrm{Irr}~\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(I,J,\omega,\mathbf{E}).

Denote by
\mathsf{Acusp}(V)=(J,\omega,\mathbf{E})
, the supercuspidal support of a simple
\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k})
-representation
V
.

4.3.
The geometric side

Let
I^{\vee}
denote the affine Dynkin diagram of the simply-connected simple group
G^{\vee}
. If
J^{\vee}\subsetneq I^{\vee}
, denote by
G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}}
the corresponding pseudo-Levi subgroup of
G^{\vee}
. Let
\bar{\mathfrak{S}}
denote the set of triples (
geometric diagrams
)
(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{F}^{\vee})
, where
J^{\vee}\subsetneq I^{\vee}
,
\mathcal{C}^{\vee}
is a nilpotent
G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}}
-orbit on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\vee}
is an isomorphism class of
G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}}
-equivariant cuspidal local systems on
\mathcal{C}^{\vee}
.
To such a triple, in
[
Lus92
, §5.12]
, Lusztig associates a generic affine Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(G^{\vee},G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})=\mathcal{H}^{\lambda,\lambda^{*}}(\Psi,z)
, for some root datum
\Psi
and parameters
\lambda,\lambda^{*}
as in
loc.cit.
. Denote by
\mathcal{W}^{\vee}(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})=\bar{W}\ltimes\mathcal{X}
the extended affine Weyl group underlying this affine Hecke algebra. The classification of the simple modules of
\mathcal{H}(G^{\vee},G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
goes via the reduction to graded affine Hecke algebras. More precisely, if
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{c}\cdot\mathcal{T}_{r}=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
, then fixing a semisimple conjugacy class
\bar{W}\cdot t\subset\mathcal{T}
,
t=t_{c}t_{h}
we have

\mathrm{Irr}_{\bar{W}t,z_{0}}\mathcal{H}(G^{\vee},G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})\longleftrightarrow\mathrm{Irr}_{\log t_{h},r_{0}}\mathbf{H}({G^{\vee}}(s_{c}),G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee}),

where
s_{c}
(also denoted by
t_{c}^{\psi}
in
loc.cit.
) is the element of
T^{\vee}_{c}
associated to
t_{c}
in
[
Lus92
, §5.13]
. The group
G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}}
is a Levi subgroup of
Z_{G^{\vee}}(s_{c})=G^{\vee}_{I^{\vee}-S^{\vee}}
, for a nonempty subset
S^{\vee}\subset I^{\vee}-J^{\vee}
. Therefore, the graded affine Hecke algebra in the right hand side is of the type defined in section
3.7
, with
G=G^{\vee}_{I^{\vee}-S^{\vee}}
,
L=G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}}
, and
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{L})=(\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee}).

For every
\zeta\in\mathrm{Irr}Z(G^{\vee})
, let
\bar{\mathfrak{S}}_{\zeta}
denote subset of the triples
(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
such that
Z(G^{\vee})\subset G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}}
acts on each stalk of
\mathcal{E}^{\vee}
by
\zeta
.

For every
z_{0}>0
, denote by
\mathfrak{S}(z_{0})
the set of
G^{\vee}
-conjugacy classes of triples
(s,n,\rho)
, where
s\in T^{\vee}
,
n\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
nilpotent such that
\Ad(s)n=z_{0}^{2}n
, and
\rho\in\Irr A(s,n).
Denote by
\mathfrak{S}(z_{0})_{\zeta}
the subset of
\mathfrak{S}(z_{0})
with the property that
Z(G^{\vee})
acts in
\rho
by the character
\zeta
.

Via the classification of simple modules for graded Hecke algebras recalled in Theorem
3.7.2
, we have (recall
s_{c}
is determined by
t_{c}
):

(4.3.1)

\mathrm{Irr}_{\bar{W}t,z_{0}}\mathcal{H}(G^{\vee},G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})\leftrightarrow\{(s,n,\rho)\in\mathfrak{S}(z_{0})\mid s=s_{c}s_{h},\ s_{h}\in T^{\vee}_{h},\ \rho\in\widehat{A(s,n)}_{\mathcal{E}^{\vee}}\}.

Then, by combining all such correspondences for all geometric diagrams,
[
Lus92
, Theorem 5.21]
] says that there is a natural bijection

(4.3.2)

\mathfrak{S}(z_{0})_{\zeta}\longleftrightarrow\bigsqcup_{(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})\in\bar{\mathfrak{S}}_{\zeta}}\mathrm{Irr}~\mathcal{H}_{z_{0}}(G^{\vee},G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee}).

If
(s,n,\rho)\in\mathfrak{S}(z_{0})
parametrizes a module for
\mathcal{H}_{z_{0}}(G^{\vee},G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
, denote by
\mathsf{Gcusp}(s,n,\rho)=(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
and call it the geometric cuspidal support of
(s,n,\rho)
(or
X(s,n,\rho),Y(s,n,\rho)
).

Remark 4.3.1
.

If
X
has real infinitesimal character and
\mathsf{Gcusp}(X)=(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
, then
J^{\vee}\subseteq I^{\vee}_{0}
.

To complete the correspondence and the proof of Theorem
4.1.1
, Lusztig matched the arithmetic and geometric diagrams. More precisely,
[
Lus92
, Theorem 6.3]
gives an explicit bijection, for each
\omega\in\Omega
,

(4.3.3)

\theta:\bar{\mathfrak{S}}_{\zeta_{\omega}}\longleftrightarrow\bar{\mathfrak{U}}_{\omega},

such that if
\theta((J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee}))=(J,\mathbf{E},\psi)
, then

\mathcal{H}_{\sqrt{q}}(G^{\vee},G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})\cong\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(I,J,\omega,\mathbf{E}).

In particular, the underlying (extended) affine Weyl groups are isomorphic

\mathcal{W}^{\vee}(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})\cong\mathcal{W}(J,\omega,\mathbf{E})

Denote this isomorphism of Hecke algebras also by
\theta
and let
\theta^{*}
the dual module correspondence.
We also write
\theta
for the bijection between
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{U}}
. In this case write
\theta(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})=(J,\bf E,\psi,\omega)
.

4.4.
Multiplicities

Let
(\pi,V)\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k}),\bf E)
be an irreducible or standard representation with unipotent cuspidal support
(\mathbf{P}_{c}(\mathfrak{o}),\mathbf{E})
, and
\mathbf{P}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{o})\supseteq\mathbf{P}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})
another parahoric subgroup. Define the convolution algebra

\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{P}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{o}),\mathbf{E})=\{f\in\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k}),\mathbf{E})\mid\mathsf{supp}f\subseteq\mathbf{P}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{o})\}.

If
\tau
is irreducible representation of
\mathbf{L}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, denote by
\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{E}}(\tau)
the representation of
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{P}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{o}),\bf E)
on
\Hom_{\mathbf{L}_{c}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}(\mathbf{E},\tau|_{\mathbf{L}_{c}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}).
Then we have

(4.4.1)

\Hom_{\mathbf{P}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{o})}(\tau,V)=\Hom_{\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{P}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{o}),\mathbf{E})}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{E}}(\tau),\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{E}}(V)|_{\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{P}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{o}),\mathbf{E})}).

Next, let
J\subseteq J^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}^{\omega}(I)
be the subsets defining
\mathbf{P}_{c}(\mathfrak{o}),\mathbf{P}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{o})
, respectively. Then using (
4.2.2
), we find

(4.4.2)

\Hom_{\mathbf{P}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{o})}(\tau,V)=\Hom_{\mathcal{H}(J^{\prime},J,\omega,\mathbf{E})}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{E}}(\tau),\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{E}}(V)).

Here,
\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{E}}(V)
is a module an affine Hecke algebras
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(I,J,\omega,\mathbf{E})
(for one
\psi
), and
\mathcal{H}(J^{\prime},J,\omega,\mathbf{E})
is the parahoric (finite) Hecke algebra with support in
J^{\prime}
.

Let
(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})=\theta^{-1}(J,\mathbf{E},\psi)
be the corresponding geometric diagram in
\bar{\mathfrak{S}}_{\zeta_{\omega}}.
Denote by
{J^{\prime}}^{\vee}=\theta^{-1}(J^{\prime})
the corresponding subset of
I^{\vee}
such that there is an isomorphism of finite Hecke algebras

\mathcal{H}_{\sqrt{q}}(G^{\vee}_{{J^{\prime}}^{\vee}},G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})\cong\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(J^{\prime},J,\omega,\mathbf{E}).

Let
\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{{J^{\prime}}^{\vee}}(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})\cong\mathcal{W}_{J^{\prime}}(J,\omega,\mathbf{E})
denote the finite parahoric subgroups defined by
{J^{\prime}}^{\vee}\supseteq J^{\vee}
and
J^{\prime}\supseteq J
, respectively.
It follows that

\Hom_{\mathbf{P}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{o})}(\tau,V)=\Hom_{\mathcal{H}_{\sqrt{q}}(G^{\vee}_{{J^{\prime}}^{\vee}},G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})}(\theta^{*}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{E}}(\tau)),\theta^{*}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{E}}(V))),

where

\theta^{*}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{E}}(V))
is an
\mathcal{H}_{\sqrt{q}}(G^{\vee},G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
-module. We are now in position to apply the deformation argument from section
3.5
. From the first part of Corollary
3.5.3
, it follows at once that, when
V
is irreducible,

(4.4.3)

\Hom_{\mathbf{P}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{o})}(\tau,V)=\Hom_{\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{{J^{\prime}}^{\vee}}(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})}(\theta^{*}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{E}}(\tau))_{q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1},\widetilde{\sigma}_{0}(\theta^{*}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{E}}(V))),

where
\widetilde{\sigma}_{0}
is the deformation functor from (
3.5.1
). Here we use the notation
q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1
in place of
z_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1
as in section
3.5
. Recall that
\widetilde{\sigma}_{0}(\theta^{*}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{E}}(V))
is a module for the extended affine Weyl group
\mathcal{W}^{\vee}(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
.

For standard modules, we can explicitate this formula further using Proposition
3.6.1
. Let
Y(s,n,\rho)
be a standard
\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k})
-module with unipotent cuspidal support. Let
(J,\mathbf{E})
be the supercuspidal support of
X(s,n,\rho)
, and
(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})=\mathsf{Gcusp}(s,n,\rho).
Let
G^{\vee}_{I^{\vee}-S^{\vee}}=Z_{G^{\vee}}(s_{c})
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{s_{c},n}
denote the generalized Springer fiber
\mathcal{P}_{n}
defined as in section
3.7
for the pair
(\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
in
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{I^{\vee}-S^{\vee}}.
Let
s_{c}
be the compact part of
s
and
t_{c}
the corresponding element in
\mathcal{T}_{c}
for the geometric Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(G^{\vee}_{{J^{\prime}}^{\vee}},G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
.

Theorem 4.4.1
.

Let
\mathbf{P}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})
be a parahoric subgroup for
\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k})
corresponding to
J
,
J\subset J^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}^{\omega}(I)
, and
\tau
an irreducible
\mathbf{L}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathbf{F}_{q})
-representation. Write
\mathcal{W}^{\vee}:=\mathcal{W}^{\vee}(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
and
\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{{J^{\prime}}^{\vee}}:=\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{{J^{\prime}}^{\vee}}(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
for simplicity, and let
\mathcal{W}^{\vee}(t_{c})
be the centralizer of
t_{c}
in
\mathcal{W}^{\vee}
. Then

(4.4.4)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathbf{P}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{o})}(\tau,Y(s,n,\rho))

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{{J^{\prime}}^{\vee}}}(\theta^{*}(m_{\mathbf{E}}(\tau))_{q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1},\Ind_{\mathcal{W}^{\vee}(t_{c})}^{\mathcal{W}^{\vee}}(\mathbb{C}_{t_{c}}\otimes Y_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n,\rho)))

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{{J^{\prime}}^{\vee}}}(\theta^{*}(m_{\mathbf{E}}(\tau))_{q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1},\Ind_{\mathcal{W}^{\vee}(t_{c})}^{\mathcal{W}^{\vee}}(\mathbb{C}_{t_{c}}\otimes H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{s_{c},n},\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{\vee})^{\rho}),

where
Y_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n,\rho))
is the standard module for the graded affine Hecke algebra
\mathbf{H}({G^{\vee}}(s_{c}),G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
, and
H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{s_{c},n},\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{\vee})^{\rho}
is a (reducible) generalized Springer representation of the finite Weyl group
N_{{G^{\vee}}(s_{c})}(G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}})/G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}}
, as exposited in section
3.7
.

Proof.

The first equality follows immediately from (
4.4.3
), Proposition
3.6.1
and the second part of Corollary
3.5.3
. The second equality is implied directly by Lemma
3.7.3
.
∎

In the case of real infinitesimal character in this paper, this result takes the following simpler form.

Corollary 4.4.2
.

Retain the notation from Theorem
4.4.1
. Suppose
Y(s,n,\rho)
has real infinitesimal character, i.e.
s_{c}=1
. Then

(4.4.5)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathbf{P}_{c^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{o})}(\tau,Y(s,n,\rho))

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{{J^{\prime}}^{\vee}}}(\theta^{*}(m_{\mathbf{E}}(\tau))_{q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1},Y_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n,\rho)))

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{{J^{\prime}}^{\vee}}}(\theta^{*}(m_{\mathbf{E}}(\tau))_{q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1},H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{n},\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{\vee})^{\rho}),

where
Y_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n,\rho))
is the standard module for the graded affine Hecke algebra
\mathbf{H}(G^{\vee},G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
,
\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}}=N_{G^{\vee}}(G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}})/G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}}
, and
H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{n},\dot{\mathcal{E}}^{\vee})
are the homology groups carrying a
\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}}\times A(n)
-representation that occur in the generalized Springer correspondence for
G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}}\subset G^{\vee}
(see
3.7
).

5.
Faithfulness for the generalised Springer correspondence

5.1.
Preliminaries on partitions

In this section, we will recall some of the basic notation for partitions, and some related objects. A
partition of
n
is a non-increasing sequence of positive integers
(\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq...\geq\lambda_{m})
such that
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+...+\lambda_{m}=n
(occasionally, it will be more convenient to denote a partition as a non-decreasing sequence, but we will clearly indicate when this is the case). Let
\mathcal{P}(n)
denote the set of partitions of
n
. We write
\#\lambda:=m
and
|\lambda|:=n
. If
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we write
m_{\lambda}(x)
for the multiplicity of
x
in
\lambda
and
\mathrm{ht}_{\lambda}(x)
for its
height
, i.e.
\mathrm{ht}_{\lambda}(x)=\sum_{y\geq x}m_{\lambda}(y)
. A partition is
very even
if all parts are even, occurring with even multiplicity. A partition is
rather odd
if every even part occurs with even multiplicity and every odd part occurs with multiplicity
\leq 1
. We write
\mathcal{P}_{ve}(n)
(resp.
\mathcal{P}_{ro}(n)
) for the set of very even (resp. rather odd) partitions of
n
.

There is a partial order on integer sequences (and in particular, on
\mathcal{P}(n)
), defined by the formula

(5.1.1)

\lambda\leq\mu\iff\sum_{i\leq j}\lambda_{i}\leq\sum_{i\leq j}\mu_{i}.

Given an arbitrary partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{m})
, we define two new partitions (of
|\lambda|+1
and
|\lambda|-1
)

\lambda^{+}:=(\lambda_{1}+1,\lambda_{2},...,\lambda_{m}),\qquad\lambda^{-}:=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},...,\lambda_{m}-1)

and we write
\lambda^{t}
for the transpose of
\lambda
.

Given
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(n)
and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(m)
, we define
\lambda\cup\mu\in\mathcal{P}(m+n)
by ‘adding multiplicities’

m_{\lambda\cup\mu}(x)=m_{\lambda}(x)+m_{\mu}(x),\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

We will need the following elementary fact.

Lemma 5.1.1
.

Let
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
be partitions such that
\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha_{2}
and
\beta_{1}\leq\beta_{2}
. Then
\alpha_{1}\cup\beta_{1}\leq\alpha_{2}\cup\beta_{2}
.

Proof.

We begin with the following observation. If
\lambda
is an integer sequence and
\lambda^{\prime}
is the partition obtained by permuting the elements of
\lambda
, then
\lambda\leq\lambda^{\prime}
. Indeed,
\lambda^{\prime}
is obtained from
\lambda
through the following algorithm: if
\lambda_{i}>\lambda_{j}
for
i>j
, swap
\lambda_{i}
and
\lambda_{j}
, and swaps of this type are strictly increasing with respect to
\leq
.

Add
0
’s to
\alpha_{i}
,
\beta_{i}
so that
\#\alpha_{1}+\#\beta_{1}=\#\alpha_{2}+\#\beta_{2}
, and choose a function
f:\{1,2,...,\#\alpha_{1}+\#\beta_{1}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{\alpha,\beta\}
such that
\alpha_{1}=((\alpha_{1}\cup\beta_{1})_{i}\mid f(i)=\alpha)
and
\beta_{1}=((\alpha_{1}\cup\beta_{1})_{i}\mid f(i)=\beta)
. Define a sequence
\lambda
by replacing each element of
\alpha_{1}\cup\beta_{2}
with the corresponding element in
\alpha_{2}
or
\beta_{2}
according to
f
, i.e.

\lambda_{i}=\begin{cases}(\alpha_{2})_{\#\{j\leq i\mid f(j)=\alpha\}}&if $f(i)=\alpha$\\
(\beta_{2})_{\#\{j\leq i\mid f(j)=\beta\}}&if $f(i)=\beta$\end{cases}

Since
\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha_{2}
and
\beta_{1}\leq\beta_{2}
, we have
\alpha_{1}\cup\beta_{1}\leq\lambda
. On the other hand,
\alpha_{2}\cup\beta_{2}
is the partition obtained by permuting the entries of
\lambda
. So by the discussion of the previous paragraph,
\lambda\leq\alpha_{2}\cup\beta_{2}
. So
\alpha_{1}\cup\beta_{1}\leq\alpha_{2}\cup\beta_{2}
, as asserted.
∎

If
r\geq\lambda_{1}
, we write
(r,\lambda)=\lambda\cup\{r\}
. We write
\alpha=\lambda\cup_{\geq}\mu
if
\alpha=\lambda\cup\mu
and the smallest part of
\lambda
is at least as large as the largest part of
\mu
. We write
\mu\subseteq\lambda
if
m_{\mu}(x)\leq m_{\lambda}(x)
for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. In this case, there is a unique subpartition partition
\lambda\setminus\mu\subseteq\lambda
such that
\lambda=\mu\cup(\lambda\setminus\mu)
.

A
bipartition
of
n
is a pair of partitions
(\lambda,\mu)
such that
|\lambda|+|\mu|=n
. Write
\mathcal{B}(n)
for the set of bipartitions of
n
.
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
acts by flipping on
\mathcal{B}(n)
, and we write
\mathcal{D}(n)
for the set of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-orbits on
\mathcal{B}(n)
.

5.2.
Preliminaries on nilpotent orbits in classical types

For
X\in\{B,C,D\}
and
n
a positive integer, let
\mathfrak{g}_{X}(n)
denote the simple complex Lie algebra of type
X
and rank
n
. Define the sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{B}(2n+1)

\displaystyle=\{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(2n+1)\mid x\text{ even}\implies m_{\lambda}(x)\text{ even}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{C}(2n)

\displaystyle=\{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(2n)\mid x\text{ odd}\implies m_{\lambda}(x)\text{ odd (or 0)}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{D}(2n)

\displaystyle=\{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(2n)\mid x\text{ even}\implies m_{\lambda}(x)\text{ even}\}

The elements of
\mathcal{P}_{B}(2n+1)
(resp.
\mathcal{P}_{C}(2n)
, resp.
\mathcal{P}_{D}(2n)
) are called
B
-partitions
(resp.
C
-partitions
, resp.
D
-partitions
). There are well-known bijections

\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\mathfrak{g}_{B}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{P}_{B}(2n+1),\qquad\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\mathfrak{g}_{C}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{P}_{C}(2n)

For
X=D
, there is a surjection

\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\mathfrak{g}_{D}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{D}(2n)

which is a bijection over partitions in
\mathcal{P}_{D}(2n)
, except the very even partitions, over which is two-to-one, see
[
CM93
, Section 5.1]
.

For
X\in\{B,C,D\}
, the
X
-collapse of
\lambda
is the unique largest
X
-partition
\lambda_{X}
such that
\lambda_{X}\leq\lambda
. A formula for
\lambda_{X}
is provided in
[
CM93
, Lem 6.3.8]
.

If
\lambda
is an
X
-partition, define the
dual
partition
d_{BV}(\lambda)
of
\lambda
as follows:

•

If
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{B}(2n+1)
, then
d_{BV}(\lambda)=((\lambda^{t})^{-})_{C}\in\mathcal{P}_{C}(2n)
.

•

If
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{C}(2n)
, then
d_{BV}(\lambda)=((\lambda^{t})^{+})_{B}\in\mathcal{P}_{B}(2n+1)
.

•

If
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{D}(2n)
, then
d_{BV}(\lambda)=(\lambda^{t})_{D}\in\mathcal{P}_{D}(2n)
.

5.3.
Preliminaries on symbols

In this section, we will recall some of the basic notation for symbols, following
[
GM20
, Section 4.4]
.

For a sequence
X=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})
and integer
a\in\mathbb{Z}
write
X+a
for the sequence
(x_{1}+a,x_{2}+a,\dots,x_{n}+a)
. We write
z
for the sequence
(0,1,\dots,n-1)
(
n
will always be clear from the context and therefore omitted from the notation).

A
\beta
-
set
is a strictly increasing sequence
x=(x_{1}<x_{2}<...<x_{m})
. The partition
\mathcal{P}(x)
associated to
x
is
(x_{1}-0,x_{2}-1,\dots,x_{m}-(m-1))
. We say that
x
is associated to a partition
\lambda
if
\mathcal{P}(x)=\lambda
. For example, the
\beta
-sets associated to
\lambda=(1,2,4)
are those of the form
(1,3,6)
or
(0,1,2,...,k-1,k+1,k+3,k+6)
for some
k
.

For integers
a,b\geq 0
and
d
, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{d}^{a,b}
denote the set of pairs
(X,Y)
of finite sequences
X=(x_{1},...,x_{r})
and
Y=(y_{1},...,y_{s})
of nonnegative integers, such that

\displaystyle x_{j}

\displaystyle\geq x_{j-1}+a+b\qquad

\displaystyle 2\leq j\leq r,

\displaystyle y_{j}

\displaystyle\geq y_{j-1}+a+b

\displaystyle 2\leq j\leq s,

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle\geq b,

\displaystyle r-s

\displaystyle=d.

The elements of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{d}^{a,b}
are called
symbols
, and are often denoted as follows

(X,Y)=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&&x_{2}&\dots&&x_{r}\\
&y_{1}&&\dots&y_{s}\end{pmatrix}

The
defect
of
S=(X,Y)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{d}^{a,b}
is the integer
d(S)=d=|X|-|Y|
. The
content
is the integer
|S|:=|X|+|Y|
. Define

\displaystyle r(S)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{x\in X\cup Y}x-(a+b)\lfloor\frac{(|S|-1)^{2}}{4}\rfloor-b\lfloor\frac{|S|}{2}\rfloor

\displaystyle m(S)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{x\in X\cup Y}x-(a+b)\left(\binom{|X|}{2}+\binom{|Y|}{2}\right)-b|Y|.

where the sums run over the multiset
X\cup Y
. We call
r(S)
(resp.
m(S)
) the
rank
(resp.
relative rank
) of
S
. It is easy to check that both
r(S)
and
m(S)
are nonnegative integers.
Define

\Delta^{a,b}_{d}:=(a+b)\left\lfloor\frac{d^{2}}{4}\right\rfloor-b\left\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\right\rfloor=\begin{cases}(a+b)\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{2}-b\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)&d\text{ even}\\
(a+b)\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)\left(\frac{d-1}{2}+1\right)-b\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)&d\text{ odd}.\end{cases}

Then for
S\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}^{a,b}_{d}

m(S)=r(S)-\Delta^{a,b}_{d}.

The
shift
of a symbol
S=(X,Y)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{d}^{a,b}
is the symbol

S^{\prime}=(\{0\}\cup(X+a+b),\{b\}\cup(Y+a+b))\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{d}^{a,b}

Note that both defect and rank are invariant under shift. Write
\mathcal{X}_{d}^{a,b}
for the set of equivalence classes under the equivalence relation generated by the shift operation. Write
\mathcal{X}_{d,n}^{a,b}
for the set of classes in
\mathcal{X}_{d}^{a,b}
of rank
n
and write

\mathcal{X}^{a,b}_{\bullet,n}=\bigsqcup_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{X}_{d,n}^{a,b},\quad\mathcal{X}^{a,b}_{even,n}=\bigsqcup_{d\in\mathbb{Z},d\ even}\mathcal{X}_{d,n}^{a,b},\quad\mathcal{X}^{a,b}_{odd,n}=\bigsqcup_{d\in\mathbb{Z},d\ odd}\mathcal{X}_{d,n}^{a,b}.

There is an equivalence relation
\sim
on
\mathcal{X}_{\bullet,n}^{a,b}
defined as follows:
S_{1}\sim S_{2}
if and only if there are representatives
(X_{1},Y_{1})
and
(X_{2},Y_{2})
of
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
such that the multisets
X_{1}\cup Y_{1}
and
X_{2}\cup Y_{2}
coincide.

There is a bijection
\mathcal{B}:\mathcal{X}^{a,b}_{d,n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{B}(n-\Delta_{d}^{a,b})
given by

(5.3.1)

(X,Y)\mapsto(X-(0,(a+b),2(a+b),\dots),Y-(b,b+(a+b),b+2(a+b),\dots)).

For
a=b=0
this specialises to a bijection
\mathcal{B}:\mathcal{X}^{0,0}_{d,n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{B}(n)
.

If
b=0
, then
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
acts on
\mathcal{X}_{d}^{a,0}\cup\mathcal{X}^{a,0}_{-d}
by interchanging the rows. Let
\mathcal{Y}_{d}^{a}
denote the set of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-orbits on
\mathcal{X}_{d}^{a,0}\cup\mathcal{X}^{a,0}_{-d}
. Note that the rank of a symbol is invariant under flipping. We call
S\in\mathcal{Y}_{d}^{a}
degenerate if its
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-orbit is a singleton. We write
\mathcal{Y}_{d,n}^{a}
for the set of orbits of rank
n
and

\mathcal{Y}^{a}_{\bullet,n}=\bigsqcup_{d\geq 0}\mathcal{Y}_{d,n}^{a},\quad\mathcal{Y}^{a}_{even,n}=\bigsqcup_{d\geq 0,d\ even}\mathcal{Y}_{d,n}^{a},\quad\mathcal{Y}^{a}_{odd,n}=\bigsqcup_{d\geq 0,d\ odd}\mathcal{Y}_{d,n}^{a}.

It is clear that
\sim
induces an equivalence relation on
\mathcal{Y}_{\bullet,n}^{a}
.

Given a symbol
S=(X,Y)\in\mathcal{Y}^{a}_{d,n}
define
\underline{S}\in\mathcal{X}^{a,0}_{|d|,n}
as follows: if
d\neq 0

(5.3.2)

\underline{S}:=\begin{cases}(X,Y)&\mbox{if }|X|>|Y|\\
(Y,X)&\mbox{if }|Y|>|X|\end{cases}

and if
d=0

(5.3.3)

\underline{S}:=\begin{cases}(X,Y)&\mbox{if }\sum_{x\in X}x\geq\sum_{y\in Y}y\\
(Y,X)&\mbox{if }\sum_{x\in X}x<\sum_{y\in Y}y\end{cases}

There is a bijection

(5.3.4)

\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{Y}^{a}_{d,n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\begin{cases}\mathcal{D}(n)&d=0\\
\mathcal{B}_{n-\Delta^{a,0}_{d}}&d\neq 0.\end{cases}

given by

(5.3.5)

S\mapsto(X-(0,(a+b),2(a+b),\dots),Y-(0,(a+b),2(a+b),\dots))

where
(X,Y)=\underline{S}
.

For
d\in\mathbb{Z}
there is a bijection
\mathcal{X}_{d,n}^{1,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{X}_{d+1,n}^{0,1}
given by
S=(X,Y)\mapsto S^{!}
where

S^{!}=(\{0\}\cup(X+1),Y+1).

We have that
\mathcal{B}(S)=\mathcal{B}(S^{!})
.

A symbol
S=(X,Y)\in\mathcal{X}^{a,b}_{d,n}
is called
special
if
d\in\{0,1\}
and
x_{1}\leq y_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq...
. Note that this definition is independent of the choice of representative of
S
. Every
\sim
-equivalence class in
\mathcal{X}^{a,b}(n)
contains a unique special element. A symbol
S\in\mathcal{Y}^{a,b}_{n}
is called
special
if the corresponding
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-orbit in
\mathcal{X}^{a,b}_{n}
contains a special symbol. Given a symbol
S
in
\mathcal{X}^{a,b}_{n}
or
\mathcal{Y}^{a}_{n}
, we write
S^{sp}
for the (unique) special symbol in the
\sim
-equivalence class of
S
.

For
n\geq 0
, write
W_{n}
(resp.
W_{n}^{\prime}
) for the Weyl group of type
B_{n}
(resp.
D_{n}
).
Recall that
\mathrm{Irr}(W_{n})\leftrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{n}
and
\mathrm{Irr}(W_{n}^{\prime})\leftrightarrow\mathcal{D}_{n}
except to each degenerate orbit in
\mathcal{D}_{n}
there are two irreducible representations of
W_{n}^{\prime}
.

To make our terminology consistent with that of
[
CMO21
]
we say that
S
is an
a-symbol
if
a+b=1
and an
s-symbol
if
a+b=2
.

We conclude this subsection with two elementary lemmas. A
linear presentation
of a symbol
S=(X,Y)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\bullet}^{a,0}
is a pair
(Z,\epsilon)
consisting of sequences
Z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{|S|}
and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{|S|}
such that
X=(Z_{i}\mid\epsilon_{i}=0)
and
Y=(Z_{i}\mid\epsilon_{i}=1)
. We say that
(Z,\epsilon)
is
canonical
if
Z
is non-decreasing and
\epsilon_{i}=0
whenever
Z_{i}=Z_{i+1}
. Note that if
a>0
, then
S
has a unique canonical linear presentation, which we will denote by
(Z^{*}(S),\epsilon^{*}(S))
or simply
(Z^{*},\epsilon^{*})
when there is no risk for confusion.

Lemma 5.3.1
.

Suppose
S=(X,Y)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\bullet}^{1,0}
. Let
(Z^{*},\epsilon^{*})
be the canonical linear presentation of
S
and let
(Z,\epsilon)
be any linear presentation of
S
. Then

Z^{*}+\epsilon^{*}\geq Z+\epsilon

Proof.

Note that
Z^{*}+\epsilon=Z+\epsilon
as multisets. So by the argument in the proof of Lemma
5.1.1
, it suffices to show that the sequence
Z^{*}+\epsilon^{*}
is non-decreasing. If
i<j
, then either

(1)

Z^{*}_{i}<Z^{*}_{j}
, or

(2)

Z^{*}_{i}=Z^{*}_{j}
,
\epsilon^{*}_{i}=0
, and
\epsilon^{*}_{j}=1
.

In the first case, we have
(Z^{*}+\epsilon^{*})_{i}\leq Z^{*}_{i}+1\leq Z^{*}_{j}\leq(Z^{*}+\epsilon^{*})_{j}
. In the second case,
(Z^{*}+\epsilon^{*})_{i}=Z^{*}_{i}<Z^{*}_{i}+1=(Z^{*}+\epsilon^{*})_{j}
. Hence
Z^{*}+\epsilon^{*}
is non-decreasing.
∎

For a symbol
S=(X,Y)
let
\tilde{S}=(2X,2Y+1)^{sp}
.

Lemma 5.3.2
.

Let
S=(X,Y)
be an a-symbol and let
(Z,\epsilon)
be the canonical linear presentation for
S
. Then
Z^{*}(\tilde{S})=2Z+\epsilon
.

Proof.

Clearly
\tilde{S}
and
2Z+\epsilon
are the same as multisets.
Thus it suffices to prove that
2Z+\epsilon
is non-decreasing.
Let
i<j
. Then either

(1)

Z_{i}<Z_{j}
, or

(2)

Z_{i}=Z_{j}
,
\epsilon_{i}=0
, and
\epsilon_{j}=1
.

In the first case
2Z_{i}+2\leq 2Z_{j}
and so
2Z_{i}+\epsilon_{i}<2Z_{i}+2\leq 2Z_{j}\leq 2Z_{j}+\epsilon_{j}
.
In the second case
2Z_{i}+\epsilon_{i}=2Z_{i}<2Z_{i}+1=2Z_{j}+\epsilon_{j}
. Hence
2Z+\epsilon
is non-decreasing (in fact increasing).
∎

5.4.
Combinatorics for Spin groups

In this section, we will recall, and reinterpret, some of the combinatorics from
[
LS85
, Section 4]
required to formulate the generalized Springer correspondence for
Spin(N)
.

If
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, define
d(m)\in\{0,\pm 1\}
by

d(m)=\begin{cases}\phantom{-}0&if $m$ is even\\
\phantom{-}1&if $m\equiv 1\mod{4}$\\
-1&if $m\equiv 3\mod{4}$\end{cases}

If
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(n)
, define

(5.4.1)

d(\lambda)=\sum_{i}d(\lambda_{i}).

Now suppose
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\leq...\leq\lambda_{m})\in\mathcal{P}_{ro}(N)
. Following
[
LS85
, Section 4.11]
, we will define a bipartition
\rho(\lambda)
. If
m=0
, we put
\rho(\lambda)=((0),(0))
. Suppose
m\neq 0
. Let

(5.4.2)

\mu=\begin{cases}(\lambda_{1}\leq...\leq\lambda_{m-1})&if $\lambda_{m}$ is odd\\
(\lambda_{1}\leq...\leq\lambda_{m-2})&if $\lambda_{m}$ is even\end{cases}

We will now define
\rho(\lambda)=(\alpha,\beta)
in terms of
\rho(\mu)=(\gamma,\delta)
. There are eight cases:

(a)

If
d(\lambda_{m})=0
, set
r=\lfloor\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}+2)\rfloor-d(\mu)
,
d=\lfloor\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{m}\rfloor+d(\mu)
.

(a_{1})

If
d(\mu)<0
, then
\alpha=(\gamma,r)
and
\beta=(\delta,s)
.

(a_{2})

If
d(\mu)\leq 0
, then
\alpha=(\gamma,s)
and
\beta=(\gamma,r)
.

(b)

If
d(\lambda_{m})=1
, set
r=\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-1)-d(\mu)
.

(b_{1})

If
d(\mu)>0
, then
\alpha=(\gamma,r)
and
\beta=\delta
.

(b_{2})

If
d(\mu)=0
, then
\alpha=(\delta,r)
and
\beta=\gamma
.

(b_{3})

If
d(\mu)<0
, then
\alpha=\gamma
and
\beta=(\delta,r)
.

(c)

If
d(\lambda_{m})=-1
, set
r=\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-3)+d(\mu)
.

(c_{1})

If
d(\mu)>1
, then
\alpha=\gamma
and
\beta=(\delta,r)
.

(c_{2})

If
d(\mu)=1
, then
\alpha=(\delta,r)
and
\beta=\gamma
.

(c_{3})

If
d(\mu)<1
, then
\alpha=(\gamma,r)
and
\beta=\delta
.

It is shown in
[
LS85
, Lemma 4.12]
that
\rho(\lambda)\in\mathcal{B}(\frac{1}{4}(N-d(\lambda)(2d(\lambda)-1)))
.

Below, it will be convenient to have a non-recursive formula for
\rho(\lambda)
. This will require some additional notation. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
r(n)\in\{0,1,2,3\}
denote the residue of
n
mod
4
and let
q(n)\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
n=4q(n)+r(n)
. For
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{ro}(n)
and
x\in\{0,1,2,3\}
, define the partition

\lambda^{x}=(q(\lambda_{i})\mid r(\lambda_{i})=x)

For any sequence
\mu=(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{m})
of the same length as
\lambda
define

\mu^{x}=(\mu_{i}\mid r(\lambda_{i})=x)

Also let

D(\lambda)=(\sum_{j<i}d(\lambda_{j}))_{i}

Since every even part of
\lambda
has even multiplicity, every part of
\lambda^{x}
, for
x\in\{0,2\}
, has even multiplicity. So, for
x\in\{0,2\}
, there is a partition
\underline{\lambda}^{x}
such that

\lambda^{x}=\underline{\lambda}^{x}\cup\underline{\lambda}^{x}

Similarly, for
x\in\{0,2\}
, there is a partition
\underline{D}(\lambda)^{x}
such that

D(\lambda)^{x}=\underline{D}(\lambda)^{x}\cup\underline{D}(\lambda)^{x}

Proposition 5.4.1
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{ro}(n)
be rather odd and put
D=D(\lambda)
. Then

\rho(\lambda)=\begin{cases}(\underline{\lambda}^{0}-\underline{D}^{0})\cup(\lambda^{1}-D^{1})\cup(\underline{\lambda}^{2}+1-\underline{D}^{2})\times(\underline{\lambda}^{0}+\underline{D}^{0})\cup(\lambda^{3}+D^{3})\cup(\underline{\lambda}^{2}+\underline{D}^{2})&if $d(\lambda)>0$\\
(\underline{\lambda}^{0}+\underline{D}^{0})\cup(\lambda^{3}+D^{3})\cup(\underline{\lambda}^{2}+\underline{D}^{2})\times(\underline{\lambda}^{0}-\underline{D}^{0})\cup(\lambda^{1}-D^{1})\cup(\underline{\lambda}^{2}+1-\underline{D}^{2})&if $d(\lambda)\leq 0$\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
\rho^{*}(\lambda)
denote the right hand side of the equation in the statement of the proposition. We will show by induction on the number of parts in
\lambda
that
\rho^{*}(\lambda)=\rho(\lambda)
. Fix
\mu
as in (
5.4.2
) and let
(\gamma,\delta)=\rho(\mu)=\rho^{*}(\mu)
. There are eight cases to consider:

(a_{1})

d(\lambda_{m})=0
and
d(\mu)>0
.
Let
r=\lfloor\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}+2)\rfloor-d(\mu)
and
s=\lfloor\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{m}\rfloor+d(\mu)
. Then by the definition of
\rho

\rho(\lambda)=(\gamma,r)\times(\delta,s)

On the other hand
D(\lambda)=(D(\mu),d(\mu),d(\mu))
,
D(\lambda)^{1}=D(\mu)^{1}
,
D(\lambda)^{3}=D(\mu)^{3}
,
\lambda^{1}=\mu^{1}
, and
\lambda^{3}=\mu^{3}
. To compute
\rho^{*}(\lambda)
, there are two subcases to consider. First assume that
\lambda_{m}\equiv 0\mod{4}
, then
\underline{D}(\lambda)^{0}=(\underline{D}(\mu)^{0},d(\mu))
,
\underline{D}(\lambda)^{2}=\underline{D}(\mu)^{2}
,
\underline{\lambda}^{0}=(\underline{\mu}^{0},\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{m})
, and
\underline{\lambda}^{2}=\underline{\mu}^{2}
.
So

\rho^{*}(\lambda)=(\gamma,\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{m}-d(\mu))\times(\delta,\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{m}+d(\mu))=(\gamma,r)\times(\gamma,s)

If instead
\lambda_{m}\equiv 2\mod{4}
, then
\underline{D}(\lambda)^{0}=\underline{D}(\mu)^{0}
,
\underline{D}(\lambda)^{2}=(\underline{D}(\mu),d(\mu))
,
\underline{\lambda}^{0}=\underline{\mu}^{0}
, and
\underline{\lambda}^{2}=(\underline{\mu}^{2},\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-2))
. So

\rho^{*}(\lambda)=(\gamma,\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-2)+1-d(\mu))\times(\delta,\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-2)+d(\mu))=(\gamma,r)\times(\gamma,s)

(a_{2})

d(\lambda_{m})=0
and
d(\mu)\leq 0
. This is identical to case
(a_{1})
except with
\rho(\lambda)
and
\rho^{*}(\lambda)
flipped.

(b_{1})

d(\lambda_{m})=1
and
d(\mu)>0
. Let
r=\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-1)-d(\mu)
. Then by the definition of
\rho

\rho(\lambda)=(\gamma,r)\times\delta.

On the other hand,
D(\lambda)=(D(\mu),d(\mu))
,
\underline{D}(\lambda)^{0}=\underline{D}(\mu)^{0}
,
D(\lambda)^{1}=(D(\mu)^{1},d(\mu))
,
\underline{D}(\lambda)^{2}=\underline{D}(\mu)^{2}
,
D(\lambda)^{3}=D(\mu)^{3}
,
\underline{\lambda}^{0}=\underline{\mu}^{0}
,
\lambda^{1}=(\mu^{1},\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-1))
,
\underline{\lambda}^{2}=\underline{\mu}^{2}
, and
\lambda^{3}=\mu^{3}
. So

\rho^{*}(\lambda)=(\gamma,\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-1)-d(\mu))\times\delta=(\gamma,r)\times\delta

(b_{2})

d(\lambda_{m})=1
and
d(\mu)=0
.Let
r=\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-1)-d(\mu)
. Then by the definition of
\rho

\rho(\lambda)=(\delta,r)\times\gamma.

On the other hand,
D(\lambda)=(D(\mu),d(\mu))
,
\underline{D}(\lambda)^{0}=\underline{D}(\mu)^{0}
,
D(\lambda)^{1}=(D(\mu)^{1},d(\mu))
,
\underline{D}(\lambda)^{2}=\underline{D}(\mu)^{2}
,
D(\lambda)^{3}=D(\mu)^{3}
,
\underline{\lambda}^{0}=\underline{\mu}^{0}
,
\lambda^{1}=(\mu^{1},\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-1))
,
\underline{\lambda}^{2}=\underline{\mu}^{2}
, and
\lambda^{3}=\mu^{3}
. So

\rho^{*}(\lambda)=(\delta,\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-1)-d(\mu))\times\gamma=(\delta,r)\times\gamma

(b_{3})

d(\lambda_{m})=1
and
d(\mu)<0
. This is identical to case
(b_{1})
except with
\rho(\lambda)
and
\rho^{*}(\lambda)
flipped.

(c_{1})

d(\lambda_{m})=-1
and
d(\mu)>1
. Let
r=\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-3)+d(\mu)
. Then by the definition of
\rho

\rho(\lambda)=\gamma\times(\delta,r).

On the other hand,
D(\lambda)=(D(\mu),d(\mu)-1)
,
\underline{D}(\lambda)^{0}=\underline{D}(\mu)^{0}
,
D(\lambda)^{1}=D(\mu)^{1}
,
\underline{D}(\lambda)^{2}=\underline{D}(\mu)^{2}
,
D(\lambda)^{3}=(D(\mu)^{3},d(\mu))
,
\underline{\lambda}^{0}=\underline{\mu}^{0}
,
\lambda^{1}=\mu^{1}
,
\underline{\lambda}^{2}=\underline{\mu}^{2}
, and
\lambda^{3}=(\mu^{3},\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-3))
. So

\rho^{*}(\lambda)=\gamma\times(\delta,\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-3)+d(\mu))=\gamma\times(\delta,r)

(c_{2})

d(\lambda_{m})=1
and
d(\mu)=1
. This is identifical to case
(c_{1})
except with
\rho(\lambda)
and
\rho^{*}(\lambda)
flipped.

(c_{3})

d(\lambda_{m})=-1
and
d(\mu)<1
. Let
r=\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-3)+d(\mu)
. Then by the definition of
\rho

\rho(\lambda)=(\gamma,r)\times\delta.

On the other hand,
D(\lambda)=(D(\mu),d(\mu))
,
\underline{D}(\lambda)^{0}=\underline{D}(\mu)^{0}
,
D(\lambda)^{1}=D(\mu)^{1}
,
\underline{D}(\lambda)^{2}=\underline{D}(\mu)^{2}
,
D(\lambda)^{3}=(D(\mu)^{3},d(\mu)-1)
,
\underline{\lambda}^{0}=\underline{\mu}^{0}
,
\lambda^{1}=\mu^{1}
,
\underline{\lambda}^{2}=\underline{\mu}^{2}
, and
\lambda^{3}=(\mu^{3},\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-3))
. So

\rho^{*}(\lambda)=(\gamma,\frac{1}{4}(\lambda_{m}-3)+d(\mu))\times\delta=(\gamma,r)\times\delta

∎

Proposition
5.4.1
motivates the following much simpler recursive definition for
\rho(\lambda)
which is obvious from Proposition
5.4.1
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\leq...\leq\lambda_{m})\in\mathcal{P}_{ro}(n)
.
If
m=0
set
\tilde{\rho}(\lambda)=(0)\times(0)
.
Otherwise, define
\mu
as in (
5.4.2
). Suppose
\tilde{\rho}(\mu)=\gamma\times\delta
.
We define
\tilde{\rho}(\lambda)=\alpha\times\beta
where
\alpha,\beta
are defined as follows: set
q=q(\lambda_{m}),d=d(\mu)

(1)

if
\lambda_{m}\equiv 0\pmod{4}
then set
\alpha=(\gamma,q-d)
,
\beta=(\delta,q+d)
;

(2)

if
\lambda_{m}\equiv 1\pmod{4}
then set
\alpha=(\gamma,q-d)
,
\beta=\delta
;

(3)

if
\lambda_{m}\equiv 2\pmod{4}
then set
\alpha=(\gamma,q-d+1)
,
\beta=(\delta,q+d)
;

(4)

if
\lambda_{m}\equiv 3\pmod{4}
then set
\alpha=\gamma
,
\beta=(\delta,q+d)
.

Then

\rho(\lambda):=\begin{cases}\alpha\times\beta&\text{if }d(\lambda)>0\\
\beta\times\alpha&\text{if }d(\lambda)\leq 0.\end{cases}

Finally we remark that from the above recursive description of
\tilde{\rho}(\lambda)
one can deduce the following explicit description of
\tilde{\rho}(\lambda)
.

For
r\in\{0,1,2,3\}
and
a\geq 0
, define
\epsilon(r,a)
to be the sequence of length
a
of the form

(5.4.3)

\begin{cases}(0,1,0,1,\dots)&\mbox{if }r=0\\
(0,0,0,0,\dots)&\mbox{if }r=1\\
(1,0,1,0,\dots)&\mbox{if }r=2\\
(1,1,1,1,\dots)&\mbox{if }r=3.\end{cases}

Similarly for
r\in\{0,1,2,3\}
and
a\geq 0
, define
\delta(r,a)
to be the sequence of length
a
of the form

(5.4.4)

\begin{cases}(0,1,0,1,\dots)&\mbox{if }r=0\\
(0,0,0,0,\dots)&\mbox{if }r=1,2,3.\end{cases}

Now for
\lambda\in\mathcal{N}_{ro}
write
\lambda
as
(\lambda_{1}^{a_{1}},\lambda_{2}^{a_{2}},\dots,\lambda_{k}^{a_{k}})
.
Define

\displaystyle q(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(q(\lambda_{1})^{a_{1}},q(\lambda_{2})^{a_{2}},\dots,q(\lambda_{k})^{a_{k}}),

\displaystyle r(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(r(\lambda_{1})^{a_{1}},r(\lambda_{2})^{a_{2}},\dots,r(\lambda_{k})^{a_{k}}),

\displaystyle\epsilon(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(\epsilon(r_{1},a_{1}),\epsilon(r_{2},a_{2}),\dots,\epsilon(r_{k},a_{k})),

\displaystyle\delta(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(\delta(r_{1},a_{1}),\delta(r_{2},a_{2}),\dots,\delta(r_{k},a_{k})).

When
\lambda
is clear from the context, we will write
q_{i}
(resp.
r_{i}
) for
q(\lambda_{i})
(resp.
r(\lambda_{i})
).

Recall from Section
5.3
the sequence
z=(0,1,...)
.

Proposition 5.4.2
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathcal{N}_{ro}
,
q=q(\lambda),\epsilon=\epsilon(\lambda),\delta=\delta(\lambda)
.
Let
\Lambda\in\mathcal{X}^{2,0}_{d(\lambda)}
be the symbol with linear presentation
(q+z-\delta,\epsilon)
.
Then
\tilde{\rho}(\lambda)=\mathcal{B}(\Lambda)
and
(q+z-\delta,\epsilon)
is the canonical linear presentation for
\Lambda
.

Proof.

Let
d_{i}=\sum_{j<i}d(\lambda_{j})
,
A_{i}=\sum_{j<i}a_{j}
, and

x_{i}=\sum_{j<i}[r_{j}=1]+[2\mid\lambda_{j}]a_{j}/2,\quad y_{i}=\sum_{j<i}[r_{j}=3]+[2\mid\lambda_{j}]a_{j}/2.

Clearly
d_{i}=x_{i}-y_{i}
and
x_{i}+y_{i}=A_{i}
.
Write
\tilde{\rho}(\lambda)=(B_{1},B_{2})
.
By the recursive formula for
\tilde{\rho}(\lambda)
we have that
B_{1}
consists of blocks of sequences of the form

(5.4.5)

\begin{cases}q_{i}-d_{i}+(0,0,\dots,0)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=0\\
q_{i}-d_{i}+(0)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=1\\
q_{i}-d_{i}+1+(0,0,\dots,0)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=2\\
\emptyset&\mbox{if }r_{i}=3\end{cases}

and
B_{2}
consists of blocks of sequences of the form

(5.4.6)

\begin{cases}q_{i}+d_{i}+(0,0,\dots,0)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=0\\
\emptyset&\mbox{if }r_{i}=1\\
q_{i}+d_{i}+(0,0,\dots,0)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=2\\
q_{i}+d_{i}+(0)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=3.\end{cases}

Here the sequences of the form
(0,0,\dots,0)
have length
a_{i}/2
.
We wish to compute
\Lambda=(B_{1},B_{2})+2((0,1,2,\dots),(0,1,2,\dots))
.
To compute
B_{1}+2(0,1,2,\dots)
we compute along blocks.
The sequence
(0,1,2,\dots)
can be written as a concatenation of sequences of the form

(5.4.7)

\begin{cases}x_{i}+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=0\\
x_{i}+(0)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=1\\
x_{i}+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=2\\
\emptyset&\mbox{if }r_{i}=3.\end{cases}

These blocks have the same form as the blocks for
B_{1}
so we can add block-wise.
Noting that
2x_{i}-d_{i}=x_{i}+y_{i}=A_{i}
, we get that
B_{1}+2(0,1,2,\dots)
consists of blocks of the form

(5.4.8)

\begin{cases}q_{i}+A_{i}+(0,2,\dots,a_{i}-2)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=0\\
q_{i}+A_{i}+(0)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=1\\
q_{i}+A_{i}+(1,3,\dots,a_{i}-1)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=2\\
\emptyset&\mbox{if }r_{i}=3.\end{cases}

Similarly, using that
d_{i}+2y_{i}=x_{i}+y_{i}=A_{i}
, we get that
B_{2}+2(0,1,2,\dots)
consists of blocks of the form

(5.4.9)

\begin{cases}q_{i}+A_{i}+(0,2,\dots,a_{i}-2)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=0\\
\emptyset&\mbox{if }r_{i}=1\\
q_{i}+A_{i}+(0,2,\dots,a_{i}-2)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=2\\
q_{i}+A_{i}+(0)&\mbox{if }r_{i}=3.\end{cases}

Thus we see that
(q+z-\delta,\epsilon)
is a linear presentation of
\Lambda
.
To see that it is the canonical presentation for
\Lambda
note that
q
is non-decreasing and
z
is strictly increasing.
Thus
q+z
is strictly increasing.
Thus
q+z-\delta
is strictly increasing except for in blocks corresponding to
r_{i}=0
where the block takes the form
(x,x,x+2,x+2,x+4,x+4,\dots)
.
But for such blocks
\epsilon
is of the form
(0,1,0,1,0,1,\dots)
and so
(q+z-\delta,\epsilon)
is indeed the canonical linear presentation for
\Lambda
.
∎

Example 5.4.3
.

Let
\lambda=(2^{4},5,7,12^{6},15)
.
Then

\displaystyle q(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,0,1,1,3,3,3,3,3,3,3)

\displaystyle r(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(2,2,2,2,1,3,0,0,0,0,0,0,3)

\displaystyle\epsilon(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(1,0,1,0,0,1,0,1,0,1,0,1,1)

\displaystyle\delta(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,0,0,0,0,1,0,1,0,1,0)

\displaystyle q(\lambda)+z-\delta(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(0,1,2,3,5,6,9,9,11,11,13,13,15)

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&3&5&9&11&13\\
0&2&6&9&11&13&15\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1&3&3&3\\
0&0&2&3&3&3&3\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\rho(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&2&3&3&3&3\\
1&1&1&3&3&3\end{pmatrix}.

We now give an explicit description of
d_{BV}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{ro}(N)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{ro}(N)
. Write
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}^{a_{1}},\lambda_{2}^{a_{2}},\dots,\lambda_{k}^{a_{k}})
for
\lambda_{1}<\cdots<\lambda_{k}
.
Let

f:\{1,\dots,\tilde{l}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{1,\dots,k\}

be the increasing function which enumerates the odd parts of
\lambda
. For example, if
\lambda=(1,2^{2},5,8^{4},9)
, then
\tilde{l}=3
and
f
is given by
f(1)=1
,
f(2)=3
,
f(3)=5
. Note that
N
and
\tilde{l}
have the same parity. Extend the domain of
f
to a set of even size, as follows. If
N
is odd, write
\tilde{l}
as
\tilde{l}=2l-1
and define
f(2l)=k
.
In this manner we obtain a function

f:\{1,\dots,2l\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{1,\dots,k\}.

Define
\delta^{i}
to be the sequence of length
k
of the form

(5.4.10)

\delta^{i}=\begin{cases}(0,\dots,0,1,-1,0,\dots,0)&\text{ if $i$ is even}\\
(0,\dots,0,-1,1,0,\dots,0)&\text{ if $i$ is odd}.\end{cases}

where the first non-zero entry arises at position
f(i)
.
If
f(i)=k
then we interpret
\delta^{i}
as the sequence

(5.4.11)

\delta^{i}=\begin{cases}(0,\dots,0,1)&\text{ if $i$ is even}\\
(0,\dots,0,-1)&\text{ if $i$ is odd}.\end{cases}

Lemma 5.4.4
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{ro}(N)
.
Let
\delta=\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}}\delta^{i}
,
A_{i}=\sum_{j\geq i}a_{j}
, and
\bar{\lambda}_{i}=\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i-1}
(with the convention
\lambda_{0}=0
).
Then

(5.4.12)

d_{BV}(\lambda)=(A_{i}^{\bar{\lambda}_{i}+\delta_{i}})_{i}.

Proof.

If
a
is even, then
d_{BV}(\lambda\cup(a,a))=d_{BV}(\lambda)+(2^{a})
. So if (
5.4.12
) holds for
\lambda
, it also holds for
\lambda\cup(a,a)
. Thus, we can reduce to the case when
\lambda
consists of only odd parts, i.e.
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},...,\lambda_{k})
with all
\lambda_{i}
odd. In this case,

\lambda^{t}=(k^{\overline{\lambda}_{1}},(k-1)^{\overline{\lambda}_{2}},...,2^{\overline{\lambda}_{k-1}},1^{\overline{\lambda}_{k}}).

By the standard algorithm for the collapse of a partition (cf.
[
CM93
, Lemma 6.3.3]
)

d_{BV}(\lambda)=(k^{\overline{\lambda}_{1}+\delta_{1}},(k-1)^{\overline{\lambda}_{2}+\delta_{2}},...,2^{\overline{\lambda}_{k-1}+\delta_{k-1}},1^{\overline{\lambda}_{k}+\delta_{k}})

where

\delta=\begin{cases}(-1,2,-2,2,...,-2,2)&\text{ if $N$ is even}\\
(-1,2,-2,2,...,2,-2)&\text{ if $N$ is odd}.\end{cases}.

But in both cases,
\delta=\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{l}}\delta^{i}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.4.5
.

There exists a partition
\underline{d_{BV}(\lambda)}
such that
d_{BV}(\lambda)=\underline{d_{BV}(\lambda)}\cup\underline{d_{BV}(\lambda)}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\bar{\lambda}_{i}+\delta_{i}
is even for all
i
.
Suppose
\lambda_{i},\lambda_{i-1}
are both even. Then
\delta_{i}=0
and
\bar{\lambda}_{i}+\delta_{i}=\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i-1}
is even.
If
\lambda_{i-1}
is odd and
\lambda_{i}
is even, or
\lambda_{i-1}
is even and
\lambda_{i}
is odd, then
\delta_{i}\equiv 1\pmod{2}
so
\bar{\lambda}_{i}+\delta_{i}
is even.
Finally, if both
\lambda_{i-1},\lambda_{i}
are odd then
\delta_{i}\equiv 1+1\pmod{2}
and so
\bar{\lambda}_{i}+\delta_{i}\equiv 0\pmod{2}
.
∎

5.5.
Unipotent representations of finite groups of Lie type

In this section, we will collect various facts about unipotent representations of finite classical groups of Lie type (e.g. classification, decomposition into Harish-Chandra series, decomposition into families, computation of Kawanaka wavefront sets). Essentially all of this information appears in
[
Lus84b
]
. However, the presentation below is from
[
GM20
]
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and write
\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
for the set of unipotent representations of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.
The set
\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
decomposes into
Harish-Chandra series

\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))=\coprod_{(\mathbf{L},\bf E)}\mathrm{Unip}_{\mathbf{L},\bf E}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))

where
(\mathbf{L},\bf E)
ranges over
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugacy classes of pairs consisting of a
{\mathbf{G}}
-split Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}
of
\mathbf{G}
and a cuspidal unipotent representation
\bf E
of
\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, and
\mathrm{Unip}_{\mathbf{L},\bf E}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
denotes the subset of
\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
consisting of unipotent representations with cuspidal support
(\mathbf{L},\bf E)
.
Moreover
\mathrm{Unip}_{\mathbf{L},\bf E}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
is parameterised by
\mathrm{Irr}(W_{\mathbf{L}})
where
W_{\mathbf{L}}:=N_{\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}(\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))/\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.
Thus there is a bijection

(5.5.1)

\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\leftrightarrow\coprod_{(\mathbf{L},\bf E)}\mathrm{Irr}(W_{\mathbf{L}}).

where, again, the union runs over all
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugacy classes of pairs
(\mathbf{L},\bf E)
consisting of a
{\mathbf{G}}
-split Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}
of
\mathbf{G}
and a cuspidal unipotent representation
\bf E
of
\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

There is also a decomposition of
\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
into families. It is clear from
[
Lus84b
, Section 13.4]
that
\mathrm{WF}
is constant on families, so for a family
\phi
, the notation
\mathrm{WF}(\phi)
makes sense.
In fact
\mathrm{WF}
induces a bijection between families and special nilpotent orbits of
{\mathbf{G}}
defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.
It follows, and will be useful to note, that a unipotent representation
X
lies in a family
\phi
if and only if
\mathrm{WF}(X)=\mathrm{WF}(\phi)
.

Below, we list all simple finite groups of Lie type which arise in reductive quotients of classical
p
-adic groups, inner to split. For each group, we give

(1)

A parameterization of the set
\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
. See
[
GM20
, Theorem 4.4.13]
.

(2)

A description of the families in
\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
. See
[
GM20
, Proposition 4.4.20]
.

(3)

A formula for the Kawanaka wavefront set of the elements of
\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
, in terms of their parameters.

(4)

A list of all Levi subgroups (up to conjugation) which admit cuspidal representations. In all the cases we consider, there is at most one cuspidal per Levi. See
[
GM20
, Theorem 4.4.28]
.

(5)

A description of the relative Weyl groups corresponding to the Levi subgroups in (4). See
[
GM20
, Proposition 4.4.29]
.

(6)

A description of the matching (
5.5.1
). See
[
GM20
, Proposition 4.4.29]
.

5.5.1.
A_{n-1}(q)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=PGL(n,\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

(1)

\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\leftrightarrow\mathcal{P}(n)

(2)

Every family is a singleton.

(3)

\mathrm{WF}(\lambda)=\lambda^{t}
.

(4)

The only Levi supporting a cuspidal representation is the maximal torus.

(5)

The relative Weyl is
S_{n}
.

(6)

The matching is
\lambda\mapsto\lambda
.

5.5.2.
{}^{2}\!A_{n-1}(q^{2})
.

Let
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=PU(n,\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

(1)

\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\leftrightarrow\mathcal{P}(n)

(2)

Every family is a singleton.

(3)

WF(\lambda)=\lambda^{t}
.

(4)

\mathbf{L}
is of type
A_{r(r+1)/2-1}
for
r\geq 0
such that
r(r+1)/2\leq n
and
2\mid n-r(r+1)/2
.

(5)

W_{\mathbf{L}}=W_{\frac{1}{2}(n-\frac{1}{2}r(r+1))}
, where
W_{k}
denotes the Weyl group of type
B_{k}
.

(6)

Let
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(n)
.
The number of boxes in the young diagram of
\lambda
with odd hook length minus the number of boxes with even hook length is of the form
\frac{1}{2}s(s+1)
for some
s\geq 0
.
The partition
\lambda
belongs to the Harish-Chandra series corresponding to
r=s
.
Now let
Z
be a
\beta
-set for
\lambda
of odd length.
Enumerate the even and odd parts of
Z
as

2x_{1}<2x_{2}<\cdots<2x_{k},2y_{1}+1<2y_{2}+1<\cdots<2y_{l}+1

and let
X=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{k}\},Y=\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{l}\}
.
If
s
is even then
\lambda
corresponds to the bipartition
(\mathcal{P}(X),\mathcal{P}(Y))
, and if
s
is odd it corresponds to
(\mathcal{P}(Y),\mathcal{P}(X))
.

5.5.3.
B_{n}(q)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=PSO(2n+1,\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

(1)

\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\leftrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{odd,n}^{1}
.

(2)

The families are the
\sim
-classes in
\mathcal{Y}_{odd,n}^{1}
.

(3)

\mathrm{WF}(S)=\mathcal{P}(2X+1\cup 2Y)^{t}
, where
(X,Y)=S^{sp}
.

(4)

\mathbf{L}
is of type
B_{r^{2}+r}
for
r\geq 0
such that
r^{2}+r\leq n
.

(5)

W_{\mathbf{L}}=W_{n-r^{2}-r}
.

(6)

Let
S\in\mathcal{Y}_{d,n}^{1}
for
d\geq 1
odd.
Then
S
belongs to the Harish-Chandra series corresponding to
r=(d-1)/2
.
The corresponding bipartition is
\mathcal{D}(S)
.

5.5.4.
C_{n}(q)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=PSp(2n,\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

(1)

\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\leftrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{odd,n}^{1}
.

(2)

The families are the
\sim
-classes in
\mathcal{Y}_{odd,n}^{1}
.

(3)

\mathrm{WF}(S)=\mathcal{P}(2X\cup 2Y+1)^{t}
, where
(X,Y)=S^{sp}
.

(4)

\mathbf{L}
is of type
C_{r^{2}+r}
for
r\geq 0
such that
r^{2}+r\leq n
.

(5)

W_{\mathbf{L}}=W_{n-r^{2}-r}
.

(6)

Let
S\in\mathcal{Y}_{d,n}^{1}
for
d\geq 1
odd.
Then
S
belongs to the Harish-Chandra series corresponding to
r=(d-1)/2
.
The corresponding bipartition is
\mathcal{D}(S)
.

5.5.5.
D_{n}(q)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=PCO^{+}(2n,\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

(1)

\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\leftrightarrow\sqcup_{d\geq 0,4\mid d}\mathcal{Y}_{d,n}^{1}
except to each degenerate symbol in
\mathcal{Y}_{0,n}^{1}
there are two unipotent representations.

(2)

The families are the
\sim
-classes in
\sqcup_{d\geq 0,4\mid d}\mathcal{Y}_{d,n}^{1}
.

(3)

\mathrm{WF}(S)=(\mathcal{P}(2X+1\cup 2Y)^{t})_{D}
, where
(X,Y)=S^{sp}
.

(4)

\mathbf{L}
is of type
D_{r^{2}}
for
r\geq 0
even such that
r^{2}\leq n
.

(5)

W_{\mathbf{L}}=W_{n-r^{2}}
if
r\geq 2
and
W_{n}^{\prime}
if
r=0
.

(6)

Let
S\in\mathcal{Y}_{d,n}^{1}
for
d\in\mathbb{Z},4\mid d
.
Then
S
belongs to the Harish-Chandra series corresponding to
r=d/2
.
The corresponding bipartition is
\mathcal{D}(S)
.
In the case where
S
is degenerate there are two unipotent representations parameterised by
S
, but also two irreducible representations of
W_{n}^{\prime}
parameterised by
\mathcal{D}(S)
and these are matched in some manner (the precise nature of which is not important to us).

5.5.6.
{}^{2}\!D_{n}(q^{2})
.

Let
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=PCO^{-}(2n,\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

(1)

\mathrm{Unip}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\leftrightarrow\sqcup_{d\geq 0,4\mid d-2}\mathcal{Y}_{d,n}^{1}.

(2)

The families are the
\sim
-classes in
\sqcup_{d\geq 0,4\mid d-2}\mathcal{Y}_{d,n}^{1}
.

(3)

\mathrm{WF}(S)=(\mathcal{P}(2X+1\cup 2Y)^{t})_{D}
, where
(X,Y)=S^{sp}
.

(4)

\mathbf{L}
is of type
{}^{2}D_{r^{2}}
for
r\geq 1
odd such that
r^{2}\leq n
. Here
{}^{2}D_{1}
is regarded as the empty graph.

(5)

W_{\mathbf{L}}=W_{n-r^{2}}
.

(6)

Let
S\in\mathcal{Y}_{d,n}^{1}
for
d\geq 0,4\mid d-2
. Then
S
belongs to the Harish-Chandra series corresponding to
r=d/2
.
The corresponding bipartition is
\mathcal{D}(S)
.

5.6.
The generalised Springer correspondence

In this section we will recall the statement of the generalised Springer correspondence and give a description of this correspondence in classical types. The main ideas are from
[
Lus84
]
and
[
LS85
]
.

Let
G
be a complex reductive group,
T
a maximal torus of
G
,
B
a Borel subgroup of
G
containing
T
, and
I_{0}
the set of simple roots determined by
T
and
B
.
For a subset
J\subseteq I_{0}
write
G_{J}
for the Levi subgroup of
G
determined by
T
and
J
.
For a Levi subgroup
L
of
G
write
W_{L}=N_{G}(L)/L
.
Let
\mathcal{N}_{o,l}
be the set of pairs
(\mathbb{O},\mathscr{L})
consisting of a nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}\in\mathcal{N}_{o}
and a
G
-equivariant irreducible local system
\mathscr{L}
on
\mathbb{O}
.
The generalised Springer correspondence is a bijection

(5.6.1)

\mathcal{N}_{o,l}\leftrightarrow\coprod_{(L,\mathcal{C},\mathscr{E})\in\mathfrak{C}(G)}\mathrm{Irr}(W_{L}),\quad(\mathbb{O},\rho)\mapsto E(\mathbb{O},\rho)

where
\mathfrak{C}(G)
is the set of
G
-conjugacy classes of triples
(L,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
where
L
is a Levi subgroup of
G
,
\mathcal{C}
is a nilpotent orbit of the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{l}
of
L
, and
\mathcal{E}
is a
L
-equivariant cuspidal local system on
\mathcal{C}
.
Let
\bar{\mathfrak{C}}(G)
be the set of all triples
(J,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
where
J\subseteq I_{0}
,
\mathcal{C}
is a nilpotent orbit of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{J}
of
G_{J}
, and
\mathcal{E}
is a
G_{J}
-equivariant cuspidal lcoal system on
\mathcal{C}
.
There is the obvious map
\bar{\mathfrak{C}}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{C}(G)
, given by
(J,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})\mapsto(G_{J},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
.
It is clear from form of the Levis that admit cuspidal local systems that this map is a bijection.
For
x=(J,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})\in\bar{\mathfrak{C}}(G)
we will write
L_{x}
for
G_{J}
and
W_{x}
for
W_{L_{x}}
.
We write
(\mathcal{N}_{o,l})_{x}
for the pairs lying in the block parameterised by
x\in\bar{\mathfrak{C}}(G)
and
(\mathcal{N}_{o})_{x}
for
p_{1}((\mathcal{N}_{o,l})_{x})
.
So if
x=(J,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
then

(\mathcal{N}_{o,l})_{x}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Irr}(W_{x}).

Note that for a Levi subgroup
L\subset G
and a character
\chi
of
Z(L)
, there is at most one pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
such that
(L,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})\in\mathfrak{C}(G)
and
Z(L)
acts via (a multiple of)
\chi
on
\mathcal{E}
.

Below, we describe this bijection for all simple simply connected groups of classical type. For each group, we give

(1)

A parameterization of
\mathcal{N}_{o,l}
.

(2)

A description of the projection
p_{1}:\mathcal{N}_{o,l}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{o}
in terms of their parameter.

(3)

A list of all pairs
(L,\chi)
consisting of a Levi subgroup
L\subset G
and a character
\chi
of
Z(L)
such that there is a triple
(L,\mathbb{O},\mathcal{E})\in\mathcal{C}
and
Z(L)
acts via (a multiple of)
\chi
on
\mathcal{E}
. In most cases,
\chi
is uniquely determined by
L
; in such cases we omit it.

(4)

A description of the relative Weyl groups corresponding to the Levi subgroups in (3).

(5)

A description of the matching (
5.6.1
).

The references are:
[
LS85
, Section 5.1]
for
SL(N)
,
[
Lus84
, Section 10.6]
for
SO(N)
,
[
Lus84
, Section 10.4]
and
Sp(2n)
, and
[
LS85
, Section 0.10, Section 4]
for
Spin(N)
.

For
G=Spin(N)
, we present (1)-(5) in a slightly different manner.
There are natural inclusions

\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{SO(N)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{Spin(N)},\quad\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(SO(N))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(Spin(N)).

With respect to these inclusions there are decompositions

\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{Spin(N)}=\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{SO(N)}\sqcup\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{*}

where
\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{*}
is the set of pairs
(\mathbb{O},\mathcal{L})\in\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{Spin(n)}
consisting of a nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}\in\mathcal{N}_{o}
and a
Spin(N)
-equivariant local system
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathbb{O}

which is not

SO(N)
-equivariant
, and

\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(Spin(N))=\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(SO(N))\sqcup\overline{\mathfrak{C}}^{*}(Spin(N))

where
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}^{*}
is the set of triples
(J,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(Spin(N))
where
\mathcal{E}

is not equivariant
for the associated Levi subgroup of
SO(N)
. The matching (
5.6.1
) takes
\mathcal{N}^{SO(N)}_{o,l}
to
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(SO(N))
and
\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{*}
to
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}^{*}(Spin(N))
. In (
5.6.2
) and (
5.6.3
), we describe the correspondence for
\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{SO(N)}
, and in (
5.6.5
) and (
5.6.6
), we describe the correspondence for
\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{*}
.

5.6.1.
SL(N)

(1)

\mathcal{N}_{o,l}
is parameterized by pairs
(\lambda,\rho)
, where
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{k})\in\mathcal{P}(N)
and
\rho
is a character of
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
, where
d=\mathrm{gcd}(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{k})
.

(2)

p_{1}(\lambda,\rho)=\lambda
.

(3)

L
is of type
(N/r)A_{r-1}
for
r\mid N
, and
\chi
is a character (trivial on connected center) of order
r
.

(4)

W_{L}=S_{N/r}
.

(5)

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{k})
,
d=\mathrm{gcd}(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{k})
, and let
\rho
be a character of
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
of degree
m
. Then
(\lambda,\rho)
belongs to a block with to
r=m
. The specific
\chi
it corresponds to depends on
\rho
, but is not important for our purposes. The corresponding
S_{N/r}
-representation is given by the partition
(\lambda_{1}/r,...,\lambda_{k}/r)
of
N/r
.

5.6.2.
SO(N)
,
N
odd

Let
n=\lfloor N/2\rfloor
.

(1)

\mathcal{N}_{o,l}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{2}_{odd,n}
.

(2)

Let
S\in\mathcal{Y}^{2}_{odd,n}
.
Then
p_{1}(S)=\mathcal{P}(2(X-z)+1\cup 2(Y-z))
where
(X,Y)=S^{sp}
.

(3)

L
is of type
B_{(r^{2}-1)/2}
for
r\geq 0
odd such that
r^{2}\leq N
.

(4)

W_{L}=W_{\frac{1}{2}(N-r^{2})}
.

(5)

Let
S\in\mathcal{Y}^{2}_{d,n}
for
d\geq 0
odd.
Then
S
belongs to the block corresponding to
r=d
.
The corresponding bipartition is
\mathcal{D}(S)
.

5.6.3.
SO(N)
,
N
even

Let
n=\lfloor N/2\rfloor
.

(1)

\mathcal{N}_{o,l}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{2}_{even,n}
.

(2)

Let
S\in\mathcal{Y}^{2}_{even,n}
.
Then
p_{1}(S)=\mathcal{P}(2(X-z)+1\cup 2(Y-z))
where
(X,Y)=S^{sp}
.

(3)

L
is of type
D_{r^{2}/2}
for
r\geq 0
even such that
r^{2}\leq N
.

(4)

W_{L}=W_{\frac{1}{2}(N-r^{2})}
if
r>0
and
W_{n}^{\prime}
if
r=0
.

(5)

Let
S\in\mathcal{Y}^{2}_{d,n}
for
d\geq 0
even.
Then
S
belongs to the block corresponding to
r=d
.
The corresponding bipartition is
\mathcal{D}(S)
.
The label of
\mathcal{D}(S)
when
S
is degenerate is not important for our purposes.

5.6.4.
Sp(2n)

(1)

\mathcal{N}_{o,l}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{X}^{1,1}_{odd,n}
.

(2)

Let
S\in\mathcal{X}^{1,1}_{odd,n}
.
Then
p_{1}(S)=\mathcal{P}(2(X-z)\cup 2(Y-z)+1)
where
(X,Y)=S^{sp}
.

(3)

L
is of type
C_{\frac{1}{2}r(r+1)}
for
r\geq 0
such that
\frac{1}{2}r(r+1)\leq n
.

(4)

W_{L}=W_{n-\frac{1}{2}r(r+1)}
.

(5)

Let
S\in\mathcal{X}^{1,1}_{d,n}
for
d
odd.
Then
S
belongs to the block corresponding to
r=d-1
if
d\geq 0
and
r=-d
if
d<0
.
The corresponding bipartition is
\mathcal{B}(S)
if
d\geq 0
and the flip of
\mathcal{B}(S)
otherwise.

5.6.5.
Spin(N)
,
N
odd

(1)

\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{*}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{ro}(N)
.

(2)

p_{1}(\lambda)=\lambda
.

(3)

L
is of type
B_{(d-1)(2d+1)/2}+\frac{1}{4}(N-d(2d-1))A_{1}
for
d\in\mathbb{Z}
,
d\equiv N\mod{4}
and
d(2d-1)\leq N
.

(4)

W_{L}=W_{(N-d(2d-1))/4}
.

(5)

Let
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{ro}(N)
. Then
\lambda
belongs to the block corresponding to
d=d(\lambda)
, cf. (
5.4.1
), and the corresponding bipartition is
\rho(\lambda)
, cf. Proposition
5.4.1
.

5.6.6.
Spin(N)
,
N
even

(1)

\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{*}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{ro}(N)\sqcup\mathcal{P}_{ro}(N)
.

(2)

p_{1}(\lambda)=\lambda
.

(3)

L
is of type
D_{d(2d-1)/2}+\frac{1}{4}(N-d(2d-1))A_{1}
for
d\in\mathbb{Z}
,
d\equiv N\mod{4}
and
d(2d-1)\leq N
.

(4)

W_{L}=W_{(N-d(2d-1))/4}
.

(5)

Let
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{ro}(N)
. Then
\lambda
belongs to the block corresponding to
d=d(\lambda)
, cf. (
5.4.1
), and the corresponding bipartition is
\rho(\lambda)
, cf. Proposition
5.4.1
.

5.7.
The Arithmetic-Geometric correspondence

Recall from Section
4
the definitions of
{\mathbf{G}},G^{\vee},\overline{\mathfrak{S}},\overline{\mathfrak{U}},\theta
etc.
Recall from the previous section the definition of the set
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(G^{\vee})
and note that there is a natural inclusion
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(G^{\vee})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\mathfrak{S}}
.
In this section, we will give an explicit description of Lusztig’s arithmetic-geometric correspondence (cf. Section
4
) for the geometric diagrams
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(G^{\vee})
for the classical groups.

Below we record

(1)

A list of all pairs
(L^{\vee},\chi)
consisting of a Levi subgroup
L^{\vee}\subset G^{\vee}
and a character
\chi
of
Z(L^{\vee})
such that there is a triple
(L^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(G^{\vee})
and
Z(L^{\vee})
acts on the stalks of
\mathcal{E}^{\vee}
by (a multiple of)
\chi
. In most cases,
\chi
is uniquely determined by
L^{\vee}
; in such cases we omit it.

(2)

The type of the affine Weyl group
\mathcal{W}^{\vee}(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
.

(3)

A description of
J
and
\omega
in
\theta(x)=(J,\bf E,\psi,\omega)
for each
x\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(G^{\vee})
.
In all the cases we consider there is a unique choice of
\bf E
and so we omit it.

This information is taken from
[
Lus95
, Section 7]
.

For
G^{\vee}=\mathrm{Spin}(N)
we present this information in a slightly different manner. As discussed in Section
5.6
, there is a decomposition

\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(Spin(N))=\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(SO(N))\sqcup\overline{\mathfrak{C}}^{*}(Spin(N)).

In (
5.7.2
) and (
5.7.3
), we describe the restriction of
\theta
to
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(SO(N))
, and in (
5.7.5
) and (
5.7.6
), we describe the restriction of
\theta
to
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}^{*}(Spin(N))
.

5.7.1.
G^{\vee}=SL(N)

(1)

L^{\vee}
is of type
(N/r)A_{r-1}
for
r\mid N
,
\chi
is of order
r
.

(2)

\tilde{A}_{N/r-1}

(3)

J
is
\emptyset
and
\omega
is of order
r
.

5.7.2.
G^{\vee}=SO(N)
,
N
odd

(1)

L^{\vee}
is of type
B_{\frac{1}{2}(r^{2}-1)}
for
r\geq 0
odd and
\frac{1}{2}(r^{2}-1)\leq n
.

(2)

\tilde{C}_{\frac{1}{2}(N-r^{2})}

(3)

J
is
C_{a^{2}+a}\times C_{a^{2}+a}
where
a:=(r-1)/2
.
\omega=1
.

5.7.3.
G^{\vee}=SO(N)
,
N
even

(1)

L^{\vee}
is of type
D_{r^{2}/2}
for
r\geq 0
even and
r^{2}/2\leq n
.

(2)

\tilde{C}_{\frac{1}{2}(N-r^{2})}
if
r>0
,
\tilde{D}_{n}
if
r=0
.

(3)

J
is
D_{a^{2}}\times D_{a^{2}}
where
a:=r/2
.
\omega=1
if
a
is even and
\omega
of type I if
a
is odd.

5.7.4.
G^{\vee}=Sp(2n)

(1)

L^{\vee}
is of type
C_{\frac{1}{2}r(r+1)}
for
r\geq 0
and
\frac{1}{2}r(r+1)\leq n
.

(2)

\tilde{C}_{n-\frac{1}{2}r(r+1)}
if
r>0
,
\tilde{B}_{n}
if
r=0
..

(3)

J
is
D_{a^{2}}\times B_{a^{2}+a}
if r is even and
D_{a^{2}}\times B_{a^{2}-a}
if
r
is odd where
a:=\lfloor(r+1)/2\rfloor
.
\omega=1
if
a
is even and
\omega\neq 1
if
a
is odd.

5.7.5.
G^{\vee}=Spin(N)
,
N
odd

(1)

L^{\vee}
is of type
B_{(d-1)(2d+1)/2}+\frac{1}{4}(N-d(2d-1))A_{1}
for
d\equiv N\mod{4}
and
d(2d-1)\leq N
.

(2)

\tilde{C}_{(N-d(2d-1))/4}

(3)

J
is
C_{(d^{2}-1)/4}\times A_{d(d-1)/2-1}\times C_{(d^{2}-1)/4}
,
\omega\neq 1
.

5.7.6.
G^{\vee}=Spin(N)
,
N
even

(1)

L^{\vee}
is of type
D_{d(2d-1)/2}+\frac{1}{4}(N-d(2d-1))A_{1}
for
d\equiv N\mod{4}
and
d(2d-1)\leq N
. There are two possible
\chi
.

(2)

\tilde{C}_{(N-d(2d-1))/4}

(3)

J
is
D_{d^{2}/4}\times A_{d(d-1)/2-1}\times D_{d^{2}/4}
,
\omega
of type II.

5.8.
Faithful nilpotent orbits

Recall the notation
{\mathbf{G}},G^{\vee},\overline{\mathfrak{S}},\overline{\mathfrak{U}},\theta
from Section
4
,
\mathcal{N}_{o,l},(\mathcal{N}_{o,l})_{x},W_{x},\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
from Section
4.3
,
d_{A},\mathcal{K}_{I},\overline{\mathbb{L}}
from Section
2.3
, and
\mathrm{Unip},\mathrm{Unip}_{{\mathbf{L}},\bf E},\mathrm{WF}(\phi)
from Section
5.5
.

•

If
x=(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})\in\overline{\mathfrak{S}}
, and
\theta(x)=(K,\bf E,\psi,\omega)
, write
\mathcal{W}_{x}
for the affine Weyl group
\mathcal{W}(K,\omega,\bf E)
and
S_{x}
for the Coxeter generators for
\mathcal{W}_{x}
.
Recall from Section
4.2
that
S_{x}\leftrightarrow(I-K)/\omega
. Write
W_{x}
for the finite part of
\mathcal{W}_{x}
(when
x\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(G^{\vee})
this agrees with the definition of
W_{x}
given in Section
5.6
).
Write
\pi:\mathcal{W}_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}W_{x}
for the projection map and
\mathcal{I}:(I-K)/\omega\xrightarrow{\sim}S_{x}
for the bijection.

•

Let
\mathcal{J}_{x}=\{J\mid K\subseteq J\subsetneq I,\ \omega J=J\}
.

•

Write
I_{0,K}
for the element of
\mathcal{J}_{x}
with
\mathcal{I}(I_{0,K}-K)
equal to the Coxeter generators of
W_{x}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{F}_{x}=\{(J,\phi)\mid J\in\mathcal{J}_{x},\ \phi\text{ a family of }\mathrm{Unip}({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\}
.

•

For
J\in\mathcal{J}_{x}
write
\mathcal{W}_{x,J}
for
\langle y\mid y\in\mathcal{I}((J-K)/\omega)\rangle
and
W_{x,J}
for
\pi(\mathcal{W}_{x,J})
.

•

Let
J\in\mathcal{J}_{x}
.
Then
{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(K)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is a
{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-split Levi subgroup of
{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
affording the cuspidal representation
\bf E
, and
\mathcal{H}({\mathbf{P}}_{c^{\omega}(J)(\mathfrak{o})},\bf E)
(c.f. Section
4.4
) has underlying coxeter system
\mathcal{W}_{x,J}\cong W_{x,J}
.
Thus
\mathrm{Irr}(W_{x,J})
(resp.
\mathrm{Irr}(\mathcal{W}_{x,J})
) parameterizes the irreducible representations in the Harish-Chandra series of
{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
determined by the cuspidal data
({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(K)},\bf E)
.
Let
\mathcal{U}_{x,J}:\mathrm{Irr}(W_{x,J})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Unip}_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(K)},\bf E}({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
(resp.
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{x,J}:\mathrm{Irr}(\mathcal{W}_{x,J})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Unip}_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(K)},\bf E}({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
) denote this parameterisation.
We also write
\mathcal{U}_{x,J}
for the composition

\mathrm{Irr}(W_{x,J})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Unip}_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(K)},\bf E}({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Unip}({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})).

Definition 5.8.1
.

Let
x=(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(G^{\vee})
,
\theta(x)=(K,\bf E,\psi,\omega)
and
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\in(\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\vee})_{x}
. We say that
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is
x
-faithful
if there exists a pair
(J,\phi)\in\mathcal{F}_{x}
such that

(i)

\overline{\mathbb{L}}(J,\mathrm{WF}(\phi,\mathbb{C}))=d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},1)
,

and for every irreducible representation
\rho
of
A(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
such that
(\mathbb{O},\rho)\in(\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{\vee})_{x}
,

(ii)

there is a representation
F\in\mathrm{Irr}(W_{x,J})
such that
\mathscr{U}_{x,J}(F)\in\phi
and

\Hom_{W_{x,J}}(F,E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},\rho)|_{W_{x,J}})\neq 0;

(iii)

for every
J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{x}
and
F^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(W_{x,J^{\prime}})
, if

\Hom_{W_{x,J^{\prime}}}(F^{\prime},E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},\rho)|_{W_{x,J^{\prime}}})\neq 0

then

(5.8.1)

\overline{\mathbb{L}}(J^{\prime},\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime}}(F^{\prime}),\mathbb{C}))\leq d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},1).

Note that Equation
5.8.1
is equivalent to the condition

d_{S}(J^{\prime},\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime}}(F^{\prime}),\mathbb{C}))\geq\mathbb{O}^{\vee}.

Lemma 5.8.2
.

It suffices to check (iii) in Definition
5.8.1
for
J^{\prime}
maximal in
\mathcal{J}_{x}
with respect to inclusion.

Proof.

Suppose condition (iii) of Definition
5.8.1
holds for all
J^{\prime}
maximal.
Let
J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{x}
and
F^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(W_{x,J^{\prime}})
be such that

\Hom_{W_{x,J^{\prime}}}(F^{\prime},E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},\rho)|_{W_{x,J^{\prime}}})\neq 0.

Let
J^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{x}
be maximal among elements of
\mathcal{J}_{x}
containing
J^{\prime}
.
Then there exists an
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(W_{x,J^{\prime\prime}})
such that

\Hom_{W_{x,J^{\prime\prime}}}(F^{\prime\prime},E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},\rho)|_{W_{x,J^{\prime\prime}}})\neq 0

and

\Hom_{W_{x,J^{\prime}}}(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime}|_{W_{x,J^{\prime}}})\neq 0.

By assumption

d_{S}(J^{\prime\prime},\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime\prime}}(F^{\prime\prime}),\mathbb{C}))\geq\mathbb{O}^{\vee}.

Thus it suffices to prove

d_{S}(J^{\prime},\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime}}(F^{\prime}),\mathbb{C}))\geq d_{S}(J^{\prime\prime},\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime\prime}}(F^{\prime\prime}),\mathbb{C})).

By
[
Oka21
, Proposition 2.31]
and the remarks in the proof we have

d_{S}(J^{\prime},\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime}}(F^{\prime}),\mathbb{C}))=d_{S}(J^{\prime\prime},\mathrm{Sat}_{L_{J^{\prime}}}^{L_{J^{\prime\prime}}}\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime}}(F^{\prime}),\mathbb{C})).

Thus it suffices to prove that for
F^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(W_{x,J^{\prime}}),F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(W_{x,J^{\prime\prime}})

(5.8.2)

\Hom_{W_{x,J^{\prime}}}(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime}|_{W_{x,J^{\prime}}})\neq 0\implies\mathrm{Sat}_{L_{J^{\prime}}}^{L_{J^{\prime\prime}}}\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime}}(F^{\prime}),\mathbb{C})\leq\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime\prime}}(F^{\prime\prime})).

By
[
HL83
, Theorem 5.9]
,

\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime\prime}}\left(\mathrm{Ind}_{W_{x,J^{\prime}}}^{W_{x,J^{\prime\prime}}}F^{\prime}\right)=\mathrm{Ind}_{L_{J^{\prime}}}^{L_{J^{\prime\prime}}}\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime}}(F^{\prime})

where
\mathrm{Ind}
applied to unipotent characters means Harish-Chandra induction.
Thus by Frobenius reciprocity we have that

(5.8.3)

\Hom_{W_{x,J^{\prime}}}(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime}|_{W_{x,J^{\prime}}})\neq 0\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime\prime}}(F^{\prime\prime})\subseteq\mathrm{Ind}_{L_{J^{\prime}}}^{L_{J^{\prime\prime}}}\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime}}(F^{\prime}).

By
[
TT20
, Lemma 10.3]
(and noting that we may replace the Deligne-Lusztig induction in the lemma by Harish-Chandra induction since
{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J^{\prime})}
is a
{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J^{\prime\prime})}
-split Levi of
{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J^{\prime\prime})}
by
[
TT20
, Section 2.1]
) we have that

(5.8.4)

\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime\prime}}(F^{\prime\prime})\subseteq\mathrm{Ind}_{L_{J^{\prime}}}^{L_{J^{\prime\prime}}}\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime}}(F^{\prime})\implies\mathrm{Sat}_{L_{J^{\prime}}}^{L_{J^{\prime\prime}}}\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime}}(F^{\prime}),\mathbb{C})\leq\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J^{\prime\prime}}(F^{\prime\prime})).

Stringing together Equation
5.8.3
and Equation
5.8.4
gives Equation
5.8.2
as required.
∎

Theorem 5.8.3
.

Suppose
\mathbf{G}
is a simple group of adjoint type and let
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\in\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\vee}
. Then for every
x\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(G^{\vee})
such that
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\in(\mathcal{N}_{o})_{x}
,
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is
x
-faithful.

Proof.

We consider separately the cases of classical and exceptional groups.
If
\mathbf{G}
is classical, the statement is proved in Section
5.9
. If
\mathbf{G}
is exceptional, it is proved in Section
5.10
.
∎

5.9.
Proof of faithfulness in classical types

5.9.1.
G^{\vee}=SL(N)

Let
n=N-1
.
Let
x\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(G^{\vee})
correspond to
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(\mathbb{Z}_{N})
of order
r\mid N
.
Then
\theta(x)=(\emptyset,1,\psi,\chi)
and
\mathcal{W}_{x}
is of type
\tilde{A}_{k-1}
where
k=N/r
.
A nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\in\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\vee}\leftrightarrow\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{e})
lies in
(\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\vee})_{x}
iff
r\mid\lambda_{i}
for all
i
.

We wish to show that
\lambda
is
x
-faithful.
Let
J=rA_{k-1}
and
\mu=(\lambda_{1}/r\leq\lambda_{2}/r\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{e}/r)
. Then
{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\chi}(J)}
is isomorphic to
A_{k-1}(q)
and so the families are parameterised by
\mathcal{P}(k)
.
Let
\phi
be the family parameterised by the partition
\mu
.
Then
\bar{\mathbb{L}}(J,\mathrm{WF}(\phi,\mathbb{C}))=\mu^{t}\cup\mu^{t}\cup\cdots\cup\mu^{t}
where there are
r
terms in this expression.
But
\mu^{t}\cup\mu^{t}\cup\cdots\cup\mu^{t}=(\mu+\mu+\cdots+\mu)^{t}=\lambda^{t}=d(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},1)
.
Thus condition (i) holds.
Condition (ii) holds because
W_{x,J}=W_{x}
and
E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},\rho)=\mu
.
Finally condition (iii) holds by using Lemma
5.8.2
to reduce to the case where
J\in\mathcal{J}_{x}
is maximal and noting that for such
J
,
W_{x,J}=W_{x}
.
Condition (iii) then holds as a consequence of condition (ii) (the only possible
F^{\prime}
that can arise is
F^{\prime}=F
).

5.9.2.
SO(N)
,
N
odd

Let
n=\lfloor N/2\rfloor
,
G^{\vee}=Spin(N)
.
Fix
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\in\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{o}
. We show that
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is
x
-faithful for all
x\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(SO(N))
such that
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\in(\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\vee})_{x}
by exhibiting a single pair
(J,\phi)
that works for all such blocks.
Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{e})
be the orthogonal partition of
N
corresponding to
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
.
Define
\mu(\lambda)\subseteq\lambda^{t}
as follows

m_{\mu(\lambda)}(x)=\begin{cases}2&\mbox{if }x\text{ is even and }m_{\lambda^{t}}(x)\text{ is}\\
&\mbox{even and nonzero}\\
1&\mbox{if }x\text{ is even and }m_{\lambda^{t}}(x)\text{ is}\\
&\mbox{odd}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}

and define
