\nu(\lambda)=d_{BV}(\lambda)\setminus\mu(\lambda)
.
By
[
CMO21
, Lemma 4.8.4]

\mu(\lambda)
and
\nu(\lambda)
are both special symplectic partitions.
We set
J
to be the set
C_{m}\times C_{n-m}
where
m=|\mu|/2
, and
\phi
to be the family characterised by
\mathrm{WF}(\phi,\mathbb{C})=(\mu(\lambda),\nu(\lambda))
.
Since
J=C_{m}\times C_{n-m}
, the family
\phi
decomposes as a product
\phi_{1}\times\phi_{2}
where
\phi_{1}
is a family of
C_{m}
,
\phi_{2}
is a family of
C_{n-m}
and

\mathrm{WF}(\phi_{1})=\mu(\lambda),\quad\mathrm{WF}(\phi_{2})=\nu(\lambda).

It follows from
[
CMO21
, Lemma 4.8.6]
that condition (i) holds for this choice of
(J,\phi)
.
For condition (ii), let
x\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(SO(N))
and suppose
(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},\rho)\in(\mathcal{N}_{o,l})_{x}
.
As described in Section
5.6.2
(3) let
r
be the odd integer corresponding to
x
.
Then
\theta(x)=(C_{a^{2}+a}\times C_{a^{2}+a},\bf E,\psi,1)
,
a=(r-1)/2
,
\bf E
is a uniquely determined cuspidal unipotent representation, and
\mathcal{W}_{x}
is of type
\tilde{C}_{\frac{1}{2}(r^{2}-1)}
.
By Section
5.5.4
,
\mathrm{Unip}_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(K)},\bf E}({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\leftrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{1}_{r,m}\times\mathcal{Y}^{1}_{r,n-m}
.
Note that
K\subseteq J
(and hence
J\in\mathcal{J}_{x}
) if and only if
\mathcal{Y}^{1}_{r,m}\times\mathcal{Y}^{1}_{r,n-m}
is non-empty.
Under the bijection in Section
5.6.2
(1),
(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},\rho)
corresponds to a
S\in\mathcal{Y}^{2}_{r,n}
with
p_{1}(S)=\lambda
.
By
[
CMO21
, Lemma 4.4.2]
,
S
has the following description in terms of
S^{sp}=(X,Y)
.
Let
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{c}),Y=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{c})
be the refinements of
X
and
Y
(see paragraph preceeding
[
CMO21
, Example 4.4.1]
).
Then
S=(A,B)
where
A=(A_{1},\dots,A_{c}),B=(B_{1},\dots,B_{c})
and
\{A_{i},B_{i}\}=\{X_{i},Y_{i}\}
for all
i
.
Let
S^{1,sp}=(X^{1},Y^{1})\in\mathcal{Y}_{1,m}^{1},S^{2,sp}=(X^{2},Y^{2})\in\mathcal{Y}_{1,n-m}^{1}
be the special symbols in the families
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
respectively.
Taking sufficiently large representatives in the relevant shift equivalence classes we have by condition (i) and
[
CMO21
, Proposition 4.7.1]
that
S^{sp}=S^{1,sp}+S^{2,sp}
.
Let
S^{i,sp}=(X^{i},Y^{i})=((X_{1}^{i},\dots,X_{c}^{i}),(Y_{1}^{i},\dots,Y_{c}^{i}))
be the decomposition into subsequences along the same indices as the decomposition of
X,Y
.
Define
S^{i}=((A_{1}^{i},\dots,A_{c}^{i}),(B_{1}^{i},\dots,B_{c}^{i}))
where

(5.9.1)

(A_{j}^{i},B_{j}^{i})=\begin{cases}(X_{j}^{i},Y_{j}^{i})&\mbox{ if }(A_{j},B_{j})=(X_{j},Y_{j})\\
(Y_{j}^{i},X_{j}^{i})&\mbox{ if }(A_{j},B_{j})=(Y_{j},X_{j}).\end{cases}

By construction
S^{i,sp}\sim S^{i}
and
S=S^{1}+S^{2}
.
Moreover, by
[
CMO21
, Lemma 4.5.2]
and
[
CMO21
, Lemma 4.9.4]
we have that
S^{1}\in\mathcal{Y}^{1}_{r,m},S^{2}\in\mathcal{Y}^{1}_{r,n-m}
.
Thus
\mathrm{Unip}_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(K)},\bf E}({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\neq\emptyset
, so
J\in\mathcal{J}_{x}
, and

(5.9.2)

\mathcal{D}(S)=\mathcal{D}(S^{1})+\mathcal{D}(S^{2}).

Note that
\mathcal{D}(S)
parameterises an irreducible representation of
W_{x}
and
(\mathcal{D}(S^{1}),\mathcal{D}(S^{2}))
parameterises an irreducible representation of
W_{x,J}
.
By the Littlewood-Richardson rule (c.f. the proof of
[
CMO21
, Proposition 4.7.2]
) Equation
5.9.2
implies that

\Hom_{W_{x,J}}(F,E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},\rho)|_{W_{x,J}})\neq 0.

Finally for condition (iii), let
S\in\mathcal{Y}^{2}_{r,n}
of defect
r\geq 1
correspond to
(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},\rho)
as above and suppose
J\in\mathcal{J}_{x}
is maximal and
F\in\mathrm{Irr}(W_{x,J^{\prime}})
satisfies

\Hom_{W_{x,J}}(F,E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},\rho)|_{W_{x,J}})\neq 0.

Since
J
is maximal it is of the form
C_{m}\times C_{n-m}
and
W_{x,J}
is of the form
W_{k}\times W_{l-k}
.
Let the pair of bipartitions
(B^{1},B^{2})
correspond to
F
.
Let
(S^{1},S^{2})
be the corresponding symbols of defect
r
.
By the Littlewood-Richardson rule (cf.
[
CMO21
, Proposition 4.7.2]
) we have that
S^{1}+S^{2}\geq S
.
We wish to show that

d_{S}(J,\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J}(F),\mathbb{C}))\geq\mathbb{O}^{\vee}.

By
[
AA07
, Lemma 3.2]
it suffices to show that the inequality holds with respect to the dominance order for the respective s-symbols viewed as non-decreasing sequences i.e. partitions.
The s-symbol for
E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},1)
is just
S^{sp}
.
Let us compute the s-symbol for the left hand side.
Let
\lambda_{1}=\mathcal{P}(2S_{1}^{1,sp}\cup 2S_{2}^{1,sp}+1)
and
\lambda_{2}=\mathcal{P}(2S_{2}^{2,sp}\cup 2S_{2}^{2,sp}+1)
(these are both special symplectic partitions).
Then

\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J}(F))=\lambda_{1}^{t}\times\lambda_{2}^{t}.

Thus by
[
Oka21
, Proposition 2.31]

d_{S}(J,\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J}(F),\mathbb{C}))=j_{W_{J}}^{W^{\vee}}(E(\lambda_{1},1)\otimes E(\lambda_{2},1)).

where
j
is
j
-induction, see
[
Lus84b
, Chapter 4]
. By
[
Lus09
, 4.5 (a)]
,
W_{J}
is the parahoric of
W
(the Weyl group of
{\mathbf{G}}
) determined by
J
, and the s-symbol for the
j
-induced representation is

(5.9.3)

S^{1,sp}+S^{2,sp}.

Since
S^{1}+S^{2}\geq S
, by Lemma
5.1.1
we have that
S^{1,sp}+S^{2,sp}\geq S
.
This is the required inequality.

5.9.3.
SO(N)
,
N
even

Suppose
\lambda
is not one of the edge cases listed in
[
CMO21
, Proposition 4.8.2]
.
Then the proof is identical to the
N
odd case except for a few minor technical differences which we note.

•

The partition
\mu(\lambda)
is defined as

m_{\mu(\lambda)}(x)=\begin{cases}2&\mbox{if }x\text{ is odd and }m_{\lambda^{t}}(x)\text{ is}\\
&\mbox{even and nonzero}\\
1&\mbox{if }x\text{ is odd and }m_{\lambda^{t}}(x)\text{ is}\\
&\mbox{odd}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

•

J=D_{m}\times D_{n-m}
.

•

r
is even.
\omega=1
if
4\mid r
, and
\omega
is type I if
4\mid r-2
.
K=D_{a^{2}}\times D_{a^{2}}
where
a=r/2
.
Despite these differences, we still have
\mathrm{Unip}_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(K)},\bf E}({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\leftrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{1}_{r,m}\times\mathcal{Y}^{1}_{r,n-m}
.

•

The rest of the argument follows through identically, except for the case
r=0
(see Equation
5.3.4
for why this case is different) which is treated in
[
CMO21
, Lemma 4.9.5]
.

Now suppose
\lambda
is one of the edge cases listed in
[
CMO21
, Porposition 4.8.2]
.
Recall that for these
\lambda
,
\mu(\lambda)=(1^{2})
which is a partition of
2
.
The issue with these edge cases can be traced down to the fact that
m=1
, but
D_{1}\times D_{n-1}
is not a subgraph of
\tilde{D}_{n}
if we interpret
D_{1}
as
A_{1}
.
If we interpret
D_{1}
as the empty graph however, then as long as we interpret things correctly, the same argument as the one given for
\lambda
not an edge case works.
We briefly give the details.

Suppose
\lambda
is one of said edge cases.
Then
\lambda
is special,
r
takes values in
\{0,2\}
,
\mu(\lambda)=(1^{2})
and
\nu(\lambda)=d_{BV}(\lambda)\setminus\mu(\lambda)
.
For both
r=0,2
we have that
K=\emptyset
(since
[
Lus95
]
uses the convention
D_{1}=\emptyset
).
Thus we can take
J
to be
D_{n-1}
.
Take
\phi
to be the family characterised by
\mathrm{WF}(\phi,\mathbb{C})=\nu(\lambda)
.
Since
d_{BV}(\lambda)=\nu(\lambda)\cup\mu(\lambda)=\nu(\lambda)\cup(1^{2})
, we have that
\nu(\lambda)
saturates to
d_{BV}(\lambda)
in
D_{n}
and so since
\lambda
is special we get that

\bar{\mathbb{L}}(D_{n-1},\nu(\lambda))=\bar{\mathbb{L}}(D_{n},d_{BV}(\lambda))=d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},1).

This establishes property (i).
For property (ii) we can construct as usual the a-symbols
S_{1},S_{2}
so that Equation
5.9.2
holds.
Supposing
r\neq 0
(the
r=0
case has already been treated in
[
CMO21
, Proposition 4.8.2]
), then
W_{x}=W_{l}
where
l=\frac{1}{2}(N-r^{2})
, and
W_{x,J}=W_{l-1}
.
Since
W_{l-1}\leq W_{1}\times W_{l-1}\leq W_{l}
and
\mathcal{D}(S^{2})\subseteq\mathcal{D}(S^{1})\otimes\mathcal{D}(S^{2})|_{W_{l-1}}
, we get from Equation
5.9.2
that

\Hom_{W_{x,J}}(F,E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},\rho)|_{W_{x,J}})\neq 0

as required.
The proof for condition (iii) requires no modifications.

5.9.4.
Sp(2n)

Suppose
\lambda
is not one of the edge cases listed in
[
CMO21
, Proposition 4.8.2]
.
The proof for this case is similar to the
SO(N)
,
N
odd case with a few (more substantial) technical differences which we note.

•

\lambda
is a symplectic partition of
2n
. The partition
\mu(\lambda)
is defined as

m_{\mu(\lambda)}(x)=\begin{cases}2&\mbox{if }x\text{ is odd and }m_{\lambda^{t}}(x)\text{ is}\\
&\mbox{even and nonzero}\\
1&\mbox{if }x\text{ is odd and }m_{\lambda^{t}}(x)\text{ is}\\
&\mbox{odd}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

•

J=D_{m}\times B_{n-m}
.

•

r
has no parity restriction.
K=D_{a^{2}}\times B_{a^{2}+a}
if
r
is even and
K=D_{a^{2}}\times B_{a^{2}-a}
if
r
is odd where
a=\lfloor(r+1)/2\rfloor
.

\omega=1
if
a
is even, and
\omega=-1
if
a
is odd.

•

S\in\mathcal{X}^{1,1}_{d,n}
where
d=r+1
if
r
is even and
d=-r
if
r
is odd.

•

We have
\mathrm{Unip}_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(K)},\bf E}({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\leftrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{1}_{d-1,m}\times\mathcal{Y}^{1}_{d,n-m}
.

•

S^{1,sp}
lies in
\mathcal{Y}^{1}_{0,m}
.
The additive identity is
S^{sp}=(S^{1,sp})^{!}+S^{2,sp}
.

•

(S^{1})^{!}
is constructed from
(S^{1,sp})^{!}
. We have
S^{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{d-1,m}^{1,0},(S^{1})^{!}\in\mathcal{X}_{d,m}^{0,1},S^{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{d,n-m}^{1,0}
and
S=(S^{1})^{!}+S^{2}
. The
\bullet^{!}
map reflects similarity classes and so
S^{1}\sim S^{1,sp}
.

•

The rest of the argument follows through identically.

If
\lambda
is one of the edge cases listed in
[
CMO21
, Proposition 4.8.2]
then if we interpret
D_{1}
as the empty graph, the same argument as in Section
5.9.3
follows through.

5.9.5.
Spin(N)
,
N
odd

Let
n=\lfloor N/2\rfloor
,
G^{\vee}=Spin(N)
.
Fix
(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},\rho)\in\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{*}
and let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}^{a_{1}},\lambda_{2}^{a_{2}},\dots,\lambda_{k}^{a_{k}})\in\mathcal{N}_{ro}
for
\lambda_{1}<\cdots<\lambda_{k}
and
a_{i}\geq 1,i=1,\dots,k
be the corresponding partition under the bijection in Section
5.6.5
(1).
Define
\mu(\lambda)\subseteq\lambda^{t}
as follows

m_{\mu(\lambda)}(x)=\begin{cases}2&\mbox{if }x\text{ is even and }4\mid m_{\lambda^{t}}(x)\\
&\mbox{and }m_{\lambda^{t}}(x)\neq 0\\
1&\mbox{if }x\text{ is even and }m_{\lambda^{t}}(x)\text{ is}\\
&\mbox{odd}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Recall from Corollary
5.4.5
that we can write
d_{BV}(\lambda)=\underline{d_{BV}(\lambda)}\cup\underline{d_{BV}(\lambda)}
.

Lemma 5.9.1
.

\mu(\lambda)\subseteq\underline{d_{BV}(\lambda)}
.

Proof.

Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
5.4.4
, we can reduce to the case when
\lambda
consists of only odd parts, i.e.
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<...<\lambda_{k})
. Recall that

\lambda^{t}=(k^{\overline{\lambda}_{1}},(k-1)^{\overline{\lambda}_{2}},...,1^{\overline{\lambda}_{k}})

and

d_{BV}(\lambda)=(k^{\overline{\lambda}_{1}+\delta_{1}},(k-1)^{\overline{\lambda}_{2}+\delta_{2}},...,1^{\overline{\lambda}_{k}}+\delta_{k})

where
\delta=(-1,2,-2,...,2,-2)
. If
x
is even, then
m_{\lambda^{t}}(x)\geq 2
. So
m_{d_{BV}(\lambda)}(x)=m_{\lambda^{t}}(x)+2\geq 4
. Hence,
m_{\underline{d_{BV}(\lambda)}}(x)=\frac{1}{2}m_{d_{BV}(\lambda)}(x)\geq 2
.
∎

Define
\nu(\lambda)=\underline{d_{BV}(\lambda)}\ \setminus\mu(\lambda)
.

Let
x\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}^{*}(Spin(N))
be such that
(\mathbb{O},\rho)\in(\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{*})_{x}
.

x
is uniquely determined and corresponds to the block parameterised by
d=d(\lambda)
in the description of blocks given in
5.6.5
(3).
We have
\theta(x)=(C_{(d^{2}-1)/4}\times A_{d(d-1)/2-1}\times C_{(d^{2}-1)/4},\bf E,\psi,\omega)
where
\omega=-1
, and the affine Weyl group is of type
\tilde{C}
.
Note that since
N
is odd and
d(\lambda)\equiv N\pmod{2}
we have that
d
is also odd.
We set
J
to be the
\omega
-stable set
C_{m}\times A_{n-2m-1}\times C_{m}
where
m=|\mu|/2
.
The reductive quotient has type
C_{m}(q)\times\hphantom{}^{2}A_{n-2m-1}(q^{2})
.
By Section
5.5
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Irr}(W_{m-(d^{2}-1)/4})\times\mathrm{Irr}(W_{\frac{1}{2}(n-2m-\frac{1}{2}r(r+1))})

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Unip}_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(K)},\bf E}({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))

where
r=d-1
if
d>0
and
r=-d
if
d\leq 0
.
It will be useful to note that

\mathrm{Unip}_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(K)},\bf E}({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\subseteq\mathrm{Unip}(C_{m}(q))\times\mathrm{Unip}(\hphantom{}^{2}A_{n-2m-1}).

and that
\mathcal{U}_{x,J}
is a product of maps

\mathrm{Irr}(W_{m-(d^{2}-1)/4})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Unip}(C_{m}(q)),\quad\mathrm{Irr}(W_{\frac{1}{2}(n-2m-\frac{1}{2}r(r+1))})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Unip}(\hphantom{}^{2}A_{n-2m-1}).

We call these maps
\mathcal{U}_{x,J,1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{x,J,2}
respectively.

We set
\phi
to be the family characterised by
\mathrm{WF}(\phi,\mathbb{C})=(\mu(\lambda),\nu(\lambda))
.
Note the family
\phi
decomposes as a product
\phi_{1}\times\phi_{2}
where
\phi_{1}
is a family of
C_{m}(q)
,
\phi_{2}
is a family of
{}^{2}A_{n-2m-1}(q^{2})
and

\mathrm{WF}(\phi_{1})=\mu(\lambda),\quad\mathrm{WF}(\phi_{2})=\nu(\lambda).

The following Lemma establishes condition (i) for the pair
(J,\phi)
.

Lemma 5.9.2
.

\bar{\mathbb{L}}(J,\mathrm{WF}(\phi,\mathbb{C}))=d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},1)
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbb{L}}(J,\mathrm{WF}(\phi,\mathbb{C}))

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbb{L}}(C_{m}\times A_{n-2m-1}\times C_{m},(\mu(\lambda),\nu(\lambda),\mu(\lambda)))

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbb{L}}(C_{m}\times C_{n-m},(\mu(\lambda),\nu(\lambda)\cup\nu(\lambda)\cup\mu(\lambda)))

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbb{L}}(C_{m}\times C_{n-m},(\mu(\lambda),d_{BV}(\lambda)\setminus\mu(\lambda))

\displaystyle=d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},1)

where the last equality follows from essentially the same argument given in
[
CMO21
, Lemma 4.8.6]
.
∎

Recall the definition of
\rho
from Section
5.4
and let
B=(B_{1},B_{2})=\rho(\lambda)
.
Note that there is a case distinction involving a flip depending on if
d(\lambda)>0
or
\leq 0
.
Let us assume for now that
d(\lambda)>0
.
We will discuss the case
d(\lambda)\leq 0
separately at the end.

To verify condition (ii) it suffices to find
B^{1}\in\mathcal{B}(m-(d^{2}-1)/4),B^{2}\in\mathcal{B}(\frac{1}{2}(n-2m-\frac{1}{2}r(r+1)))
such that

B=B^{1}+B^{2}

(c.f. the proof of
[
CMO21
, Proposition 4.7.2]
) and
\mathcal{U}_{x,J,1}(B^{1})\in\phi_{1},\mathcal{U}_{x,J,2}(B^{2})\in\phi_{2}
.
Note that since the families in
{}^{2}A_{n-2m-1}
are all singletons, the condition
\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J,2}(B^{2}))=\nu(\lambda)
in fact completely determines
B^{2}
provided such a
B^{2}
exists.
For this reason it is covenient to determine
B^{2}
first.

Recall the definitions of
f,\delta,\bar{\lambda}_{i},A_{i}
from the end of Section
5.4
.
Let
(a,b]
denote the set
\{a+1,\dots,b\}
when
a<b
and
\emptyset
otherwise. Let
(a,b)
denote the set
\{a+1,\dots,b-1\}
if
a+1<b
and
\emptyset
otherwise.
Let
[P]
denote the function that takes value
1
if
P
is true and
0
if
P
is false.
Define
\xi_{i}=[4\mid\bar{\lambda}_{i}],\zeta_{i}=[2\nmid\bar{\lambda}_{i}]
and
\mathcal{S}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{l}(f(2i-1),f(2i)]
.
Since
a_{i}=1
whenever
\lambda_{i}
is odd, we have that
A_{i}
is even if and only if
i\in\mathcal{S}
.
Therefore, we can write
\mu
as
(A_{i}^{\zeta_{i}+2\xi_{i}})_{i\in\mathcal{S}}
(this is a decreasing sequence in
i
).
It follows that

\nu=(A_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\lambda}_{i}+\delta_{i})-[i\in\mathcal{S}](\zeta_{i}+2\xi_{i})}).

A direct computation then shows that

\nu^{t}=(\eta_{1}^{a_{1}},\eta_{2}^{a_{2}},\dots,\eta_{k}^{a_{k}})

where
\eta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\eta_{k}
,

\eta_{i}=\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{i}+[i\in\mathcal{O}_{0}]-[i\in\mathcal{O}_{1}])-E_{i},\quad E_{i}=\sum_{j\leq i,j\in\mathcal{S}}\zeta_{i}+2\xi_{i}

and

\mathcal{O}_{i}=\{f(j)\mid 1\leq j\leq 2l,\ j\equiv i\pmod{2}\}.

By Section
5.5.2
(2), the partition
\nu^{t}
parameterises
\mathcal{U}_{x,J,2}(B^{2})
as en element of
\mathrm{Unip}(\hphantom{}^{2}A_{n-2m-1}(q^{2}))
.
Let us now compute
B_{2}=(B_{1}^{2},B_{2}^{2})
using the algorithm in Section
5.5.2
(6).
Let
z_{i}=\lfloor\lambda_{i}/2\rfloor
so
z_{i}
is even if
r_{i}=0,1
and is odd if
r_{i}=2,3
.
In terms of
z_{i}
we have

\eta_{i}=z_{i}+[i\in\mathcal{O}_{0}]-E_{i}.

The
\beta
-set
Z
for
\nu^{t}
of length
\#\lambda
consists of blocks of sequences of the form

z_{i}+[i\in\mathcal{O}_{0}]-E_{i}+C_{i}+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}-1)

where
C_{i}=\sum_{j<i}a_{j}
.
Note that

\displaystyle z_{i}+[i\in\mathcal{O}_{0}]-E_{i}+C_{i}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle[r_{i}=2,3]+[i\in\mathcal{S},\lambda_{i}\text{ is odd}]+[i\in\mathcal{S},\lambda_{i}\text{ is even}]+[i\in\mathcal{S}]\pmod{2}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle[r_{i}=2,3]\pmod{2}.

Thus the
\beta
-set for
B_{1}^{2}
, which we denote
S_{1}^{2}
, consists of blocks of sequences of the form

(5.9.4)

\begin{cases}q_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{i}+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=0$}\\
q_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{i}+(0)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=1$}\\
q_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{i}+1+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=2$}\\
\emptyset&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=3$}\end{cases}

and the
\beta
-set for
B_{2}^{2}
, which we denote
S_{2}^{2}
, consists of blocks of sequences of the form

(5.9.5)

\begin{cases}q_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{i}+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=0$}\\
\emptyset&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=1$}\\
q_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{i}+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=2$}\\
q_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{i}+(0)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=3$.}\end{cases}

where
\Delta_{i}=[i\in\mathcal{O}_{0}]+C_{i}-E_{i}
and
1\leq i\leq k
.
The partitions
B_{1}^{2},B_{2}^{2}
can then be obtained by applying
\mathcal{P}
to the
\beta
-sets.
Note that
|S_{1}^{2}|-|S_{2}^{2}|=d
.

Let us now produce a bipartition
B_{1}
satisfying
\mathcal{U}_{x,J,1}(B_{1})\in\phi_{1}
which, we will see, satisfies
B=B^{1}+B^{2}
.
First suppose such a
B^{1}
exists.
Let
S=\mathcal{D}^{-1}(B^{1})\in\mathcal{Y}_{d,m}^{1}
.
Using Section
5.5.4
(3), we can compute
S^{sp}
from the condition
\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J,1}(B^{1}))=\mu(\lambda)
.
A direct computation shows that

\mu^{t}=(E_{1}^{a_{1}},E_{2}^{a_{2}},\dots,E_{k}^{a_{k}}).

Taking a
\beta
-set of length
\#\lambda
one computes that
S^{sp}=(X,Y)
where
X
consists of blocks of sequences of the form

(5.9.6)

\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{ if $i\in\mathcal{O}_{0}$}\\
(C_{i}+E_{i})/2+(0)&\mbox{ if $i\in\mathcal{O}_{1}$}\\
(C_{i}+E_{i})/2+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)&\mbox{ if $\lambda_{i}$ is even}\end{cases}

and
Y
consists of blocks of sequences of the form

(5.9.7)

\begin{cases}(C_{i}+E_{i}-1)/2+(0)&\mbox{ if $i\in\mathcal{O}_{0}$}\\
\emptyset&\mbox{ if $i\in\mathcal{O}_{1}$}\\
(C_{i}+E_{i})/2+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)&\mbox{ if $\lambda_{i}$ is even.}\end{cases}

Thus we know that if such a
S
exists, it must have entries coming from the above special symbol.
We now define such an
S
.
Let
S=(S_{1}^{1},S_{2}^{1})
where
S_{1}^{1}
consists of blocks of sequences of the form

(5.9.8)

\begin{cases}(C_{i}+E_{i}-[i\in\mathcal{O}_{0}])/2+(0)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=1$}\\
\emptyset&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=3$}\\
(C_{i}+E_{i})/2+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=0,2$}\end{cases}

and
S_{2}^{1}
consists of blocks of sequences of the form

(5.9.9)

\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=1$}\\
(C_{i}+E_{i}-[i\in\mathcal{O}_{0}])/2+(0)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=3$}\\
(C_{i}+E_{i})/2+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=0,2$.}\end{cases}

Note as a sanity check that
C_{i}+E_{i}\equiv[i\in\mathcal{S}]+[i\in\mathcal{S},\lambda_{i}\text{ even}]\equiv[i\in\mathcal{O}_{0}]\pmod{2}
since
\mathcal{O}_{0}=\{i\in\mathcal{S}\mid\text{$\lambda_{i}$ is odd}\}
and so all the entries are integers.
It is clear that
S
has the same entries as
S^{sp}
and has defect
d
.
To show that
S
lies in
\mathcal{Y}_{d,m}^{1}
it suffices to check that
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
are both strictly increasing.
Clearly within each block the sequence is strictly increasing so we only need to compare endpoints of blocks.
We prove this for
S_{1}^{1}
. The proof for
S_{2}^{1}
is analogous.
Let
o_{i}=\lfloor(C_{i}+E_{i})/2\rfloor=(C_{i}+E_{i}-[i\in\mathcal{O}_{0}])/2
,
e_{i}=(C_{i}+E_{i})/2
and
e_{i}^{\prime}=(C_{i+1}+E_{i})/2-1
.
Here
e_{i}
and
e_{i}^{\prime}
are the entries at the start and at the end of the
i
th block when
\lambda_{i}
is even.
Let
i
and
j
index consecutive blocks of
S_{1}^{1}
(so
i<j
and
r_{l}=3
for all
i<l<j
).
There are a number of cases to consider:

•

r_{i}=1,r_{j}=1

\implies

o_{i}<o_{j}
,

•

r_{i}=1,r_{j}\in\{0,2\}

\implies

o_{i}<e_{j}
,

•

r_{i}\in\{0,2\},r_{j}=1

\implies

e_{i}^{\prime}<o_{j}
,

•

r_{i}\in\{0,2\},r_{j}\in\{0,2\}

\implies

e_{i}^{\prime}<e_{j}
.

We prove each case as follows:

•

Note that
C_{j}=C_{i}+(j-i)
and so
o_{i}<o_{j}
certainly holds if
j-i\geq 2
.
Thus it remains to verify the case when
j=i+1
.
Suppose
j=i+1
. Then
C_{j}=C_{i}+1
.
Moreover, since
r_{j-1}=r_{i}=r_{j}
we have
\xi_{j}=1
and so
E_{j}=E_{i}+2[j\in\mathcal{S}]
.
Thus

o_{j}=o_{i}+(1+2[j\in\mathcal{S}]+[j-1\in\mathcal{O}_{0}]-[j\in\mathcal{O}_{0}])/2=o_{i}+1>o_{i}

as required.

•

By a similar reduction to the one performed in the previous case we may assume that
j=i+1
.
Then
C_{j}=C_{i}+1
and

E_{j}=E_{i}+[j\in\mathcal{S}]=E_{i}+1-[j-1\in\mathcal{O}_{0}].

Thus

e_{j}=(C_{j}+E_{j})/2=(C_{i}+E_{i}-[i\in\mathcal{O}_{0}])/2+1>o_{i}

as required.

•

Suppose
j-i\geq 2
. Then
C_{j}=C_{i+1}+(j-i-1)\geq C_{i+1}+1
and
E_{j}\geq E_{i}
so

o_{j}=(C_{j}+E_{j}-[j\in\mathcal{O}_{0}])/2\geq(C_{i+1}+E_{i}+1-[j\in\mathcal{O}_{0}])/2>e_{i}^{\prime}.

If
j=i+1
then
E_{j}=E_{i}+[j\in\mathcal{O}_{0}]
so
o_{j}=e_{i}^{\prime}+1>e_{i}^{\prime}
as required.

•

Since
j>i
we have
C_{j}\geq C_{i+1}
and
E_{j}\geq E_{i}
and so

e_{i}^{\prime}\leq(C_{j}+E_{j})/2-1<e_{j}.

It thus now remains to check that
\mathcal{P}(S_{y}^{1})+\mathcal{P}(S_{y}^{2})=B_{y}
for
y=1,2
.
For this we prove the equivalent statement that

(5.9.10)

S_{y}^{1}+S_{y}^{2}=B_{y}+2\cdot(0,1,2,\dots).

Let
\lambda^{i}
denote the partition
(\lambda_{1}^{a_{1}},\lambda_{2}^{a_{2}},\dots,\lambda_{i-1}^{a_{i-1}})
.
We note that by Proposition
5.4.1
,
B_{1}
consists of blocks of the form

(5.9.11)

\begin{cases}q_{i}-d(\lambda^{i})+(0,0,\dots,0)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=0$}\\
q_{i}-d(\lambda^{i})+(0)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=1$}\\
q_{i}-d(\lambda^{i})+1+(0,0,\dots,0)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=2$}\\
\emptyset&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=3$}\end{cases}

where in the
r_{i}=0,2
cases the blocks have length
a_{i}/2
, and
B_{2}
consists of blocks of sequences of the form

(5.9.12)

\begin{cases}q_{i}+d(\lambda^{i})+(0,0,\dots,0)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=0$}\\
\emptyset&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=1$}\\
q_{i}+d(\lambda^{i})+(0,0,\dots,0)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=2$}\\
q_{i}+d(\lambda^{i})+(0)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=3$}\end{cases}

where in the
r_{i}=0,2
cases the blocks have length
a_{i}/2
.
We prove Equation
5.9.10
for
y=1
. The case
y=2
is analogous.
Note that the block structures of
S_{i}^{1},S_{i}^{2}
, and
B_{i}
all coincide (by which we mean they have the same number of blocks and the blocks have the same size) and so we prove the equality on an arbitrary block.
For this it will be necessary to decompose the sequence
(0,1,\dots)
into blocks of the same size.
We have for
y=1
, the sequence
(0,1,\dots)
decomposes into the blocks

(5.9.13)

\begin{cases}F_{i}+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=0$}\\
F_{i}+(0)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=1$}\\
F_{i}+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=2$}\\
\emptyset&\mbox{ if $r_{i}=3$}\end{cases}

where
F_{i}=\sum_{j<i}([2\mid\lambda_{j}]a_{j}/2+[r(\lambda_{j})=1])
.
Unpacking Equation
5.9.10
for a block with
r_{i}=0,2
we see that we must show that

\displaystyle q_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{i}+[r_{i}=2]+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)+(C_{i}+E_{i})/2+(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q_{i}-d(\lambda^{i})+[r_{i}=2]+2F_{i}+2(0,1,\dots,a_{i}/2-1)

After cancelling equal terms we get that we must show

(5.9.14)

\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{i}+(C_{i}+E_{i})/2=2F_{i}-d(\lambda^{i}).

Now the left hand side simplifies to
C_{i}
, whereas the right hand side simplifies to

\displaystyle\sum_{j<i}\left([2\mid a_{j}]a_{j}+2[r(\lambda_{j})=1]\right)-\sum_{j<i}\left([r(\lambda_{j})=1]-[r(\lambda_{j})=3]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j<i}\left([2\mid a_{j}]a_{j}+[r(\lambda_{j})=1]+[r(\lambda_{j})=3]\right)=C_{i}.

Thus we have the equality that we desire. For blocks with
r_{i}=1
we must show that

\displaystyle q_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{i}+(0)+(C_{i}+E_{i}-[i\in\mathcal{O}_{0}])/2+(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q_{i}-d(\lambda^{i})+2F_{i}+2(0).

Cancelling equal terms yields exactly Equation
5.9.14
which we have just established.
Finally, the
r_{i}=3
case is vacuous.
This concludes our proof that condition (ii) holds when
d(\lambda)>0
.
The case
d(\lambda)\leq 0
is analogous after making the following modifications

•

In terms of
B_{1},B_{2}
as defined above,
B=(B_{2},B_{1})
;

•

In terms of
B_{1}^{2},B_{2}^{2}
as defined above,
B_{2}=(B_{2}^{2},B_{1}^{2})
;

•

In terms of
S_{1}^{1},S_{2}^{1}
as defined above, we define
S=(S_{2}^{1},S_{1}^{1})
.

Since all the terms are just flipped the proof follows through identically.

Finally we show that condition (iii) holds.
We first need a preparatory lemma.
For
r\in\{0,1,2,3\}
and
a\geq 0
, define
\gamma(r,a)
to be the sequence of length
a
of the form

(5.9.15)

\begin{cases}(0,1,0,1,\dots)&\mbox{if }r=0,2\\
(0,0,0,0,\dots)&\mbox{if }r=1,3.\end{cases}

Define

\displaystyle\gamma(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(\gamma(r_{1},a_{1}),\gamma(r_{2},a_{2}),\dots,\gamma(r_{k},a_{k})).

Lemma 5.9.3
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathcal{N}_{ro}
.
Let
\Lambda\in\mathcal{Y}^{2}_{\bullet,n}
be the symbol with
\mathcal{D}(\Lambda)=E(\lambda,1)
.
Then

Z^{*}(\Lambda)=2q(\lambda)+z+\epsilon(\lambda)-2\delta(\lambda).

Proof.

Write
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}^{a_{1}},\dots,\lambda_{k}^{a_{k}})
with
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<\dots<\lambda_{k}
.
Let
1\leq n_{1}<\cdots<n_{l}\leq k
denote the indicies for which
\lambda_{i}
is odd. Let
n_{0}=0
,
\lambda_{0}=0
, and
a_{0}=0
.
We can break
\lambda
up into subsequences of the form
(\lambda_{n_{i}}^{a_{n_{i}}},\dots,\lambda_{n_{i+1}-1}^{a_{n_{i+1}-1}})
.
Note that for
i>0
,
a_{n_{i}}=1
.
Let
A_{i}=\sum_{j<i}a_{j}
.
Then
\lambda+z
is block-wise of the form

(5.9.16)

A_{n_{i}}+(\lambda_{n_{i}}^{a_{n_{i}}},\dots,\lambda_{n_{i+1}-1}^{a_{n_{i+1}-1}})+(0,1,\dots).

Since
A_{n_{i}}\equiv i-1\pmod{2}
for
i>0
and
A_{n_{0}}=0
, considering this sequence in Equation
5.9.16
modulo
2
we see that it is of the form
i+(0,0,1,0,1,\dots)\pmod{2}
for
i>0
and
(0,1,0,1,\dots)\pmod{2}
for
i=0
.
Let
Thus the a-symbol consists of blocks of the form

\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{n_{i}}/2,&\dots,&\lambda_{n_{i+1}-1}/2,&\dots\\
\lambda_{n_{i}}/2,&\dots,&\lambda_{n_{i+1}-1}/2,&\dots\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0,&1,&\dots\\
0,&1,&\dots\end{pmatrix}

for
i=0
,

A_{n_{i}}/2+\begin{pmatrix}\lfloor\lambda_{n_{i}}/2\rfloor,&\lambda_{n_{i}+1}/2,&\dots,&\lambda_{n_{i+1}-1}/2,&\dots\\
&\lambda_{n_{i}+1}/2,&\dots,&\lambda_{n_{i+1}-1}/2,&\dots\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0,&0,&1,&\dots\\
&1,&2,&\dots\end{pmatrix}

for
i>0
and odd, and

(A_{n_{i}}-1)/2+\begin{pmatrix}&\lambda_{n_{i}+1}/2,&\dots,&\lambda_{n_{i+1}-1}/2&\dots\\
\lfloor\lambda_{n_{i}}/2\rfloor,&\lambda_{n_{i}+1}/2,&\dots,&\lambda_{n_{i+1}-1}/2,&\dots\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}&1,&2,&\dots\\
1,&1,&2,&\dots\end{pmatrix}

for
i>0
and even.
The s-symbol thus consists of blocks of the form

\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{n_{i}}/2,&\dots,&\lambda_{n_{i+1}-1}/2,&\dots\\
\lambda_{n_{i}}/2,&\dots,&\lambda_{n_{i+1}-1}/2,&\dots\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0,&2,&\dots\\
0,&2,&\dots\end{pmatrix}

for
i=0
,

A_{n_{i}}+\begin{pmatrix}\lfloor\lambda_{n_{i}}/2\rfloor,&\lambda_{n_{i}+1}/2,&\dots,&\lambda_{n_{i+1}-1}/2,&\dots\\
&\lambda_{n_{i}+1}/2,&\dots,&\lambda_{n_{i+1}-1}/2,&\dots\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0,&1,&3,&5,&\dots\\
&1,&3,&5,&\dots\end{pmatrix}

for
i>0
and odd, and

A_{n_{i}}+\begin{pmatrix}&\lambda_{n_{i}+1}/2,&\dots,&\lambda_{n_{i+1}-1}/2&\dots\\
\lfloor\lambda_{n_{i}}/2\rfloor,&\lambda_{n_{i}+1}/2,&\dots,&\lambda_{n_{i+1}-1}/2,&\dots\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}&1,&3,&5,&\dots\\
0,&1,&3,&5,&\dots\end{pmatrix}

for
i>0
and even.
Thus as a non-decreasing sequence, the s-symbol is equal to

\lfloor\lambda/2\rfloor+z-\gamma(\lambda).

But
\lfloor\lambda/2\rfloor=2\lfloor\lambda/4\rfloor+[r=2,3]
.
Since
[r=2,3]-\gamma(\lambda)=\epsilon(\lambda)-2\delta(\lambda)
we get that

\lfloor\lambda/2\rfloor+z-\gamma(\lambda)=2q(\lambda)+z+\epsilon(\lambda)-2\delta(\lambda).

∎

We handle the case
d(\lambda)>0
first.
Suppose
J\in\mathcal{J}_{x}
is maximal and
F\in\mathrm{Irr}(W_{x,J})
is such that

(5.9.17)

\Hom_{W_{x,J}}(F,E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},\rho)|_{W_{x,J}})\neq 0.

Since
J
is maximal
W_{x,J}=W_{i}\times W_{l-i}
for
l=(N-d(2d-1))/4
and some
0\leq i\leq l
.
Let
(B^{1},B^{2})
be the pair of bipartitions parameterising
F
(so
B^{1}
is a bipartition of
i
, and
B^{2}
is a bipartition of
l-i
).
We wish to show that

d_{S}(J,\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J}(F),\mathbb{C}))\geq\mathbb{O}^{\vee}.

By
[
AA07
, Lemma 3.2]
it suffices to show that the inequality holds for the respective s-symbols viewed as non-decreasing sequences.
By Lemma
5.9.3
the multiset of entries of the s-symbol for
E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},1)
viewed as a non-decreasing sequence is

2q(\lambda)+z+\epsilon(\lambda)-2\delta(\lambda).

Let us compute the s-symbol attached to
d_{S}(J,\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J}(F),\mathbb{C}))
.
Let
S^{i}\in\mathcal{X}_{d(\lambda)}^{1,0}
be the symbol associated to
B^{i}
.
Write
S^{i}=(S_{1}^{i},S_{2}^{i})
.
Let
\lambda_{1}=\mathcal{P}(2S_{1}^{1,sp}\cup 2S_{2}^{1,sp}+1)
(a special symplectic partition) and
\lambda_{2}=\mathcal{P}(2S_{1}^{2}\cup 2S_{2}^{2}+1)
.
Then

\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J,1})(B^{1})=\lambda_{1}^{t},\quad\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J,2}(B^{2}))=\lambda_{2}^{t}.

Thus by
[
Oka21
, Proposition 2.31]

d_{S}(J,\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J}(F),\mathbb{C}))=j_{W_{J}}^{W^{\vee}}(E(\lambda_{1},1)\otimes E(\lambda_{2},1)\otimes E(\lambda_{1},1))

where
W_{J}
is the parahoric of
W
(the Weyl group of
{\mathbf{G}}
) determined by
J
.
By
[
Lus09
, 4.5 (a)]
,
Z^{*}
of the s-symbol for the
j
-induced representation is

(5.9.18)

2S^{1,sp}+\widetilde{S^{2}}-z.

Thus we must show that

(5.9.19)

2S^{1,sp}+\widetilde{S^{2}}-z\geq 2q(\lambda)+z+\epsilon(\lambda)-2\delta(\lambda).

For this we first observe that by the Littlewood-Richardson rule (c.f.
[
CMO21
, Proposition 4.7.2]
), Equation
5.9.17
implies that
B^{1}+B^{2}\geq B
where
B=\rho(\lambda)
.
Let
q=q(\lambda),\epsilon=\epsilon(\lambda),\delta=\delta(\lambda)
and
\Lambda
be the symbol with linear presentation
(q+z-\delta,\epsilon)
.
By Proposition
5.4.2
,
\Lambda=\rho(B)+2(z,z)
.
Thus

B^{1}+B^{2}\geq B\Rightarrow S^{1}+S^{2}\geq\Lambda.

Since
\epsilon
has exactly
d(\lambda)
many
1
’s, there is a unique sequence
S^{i,r}
such that
(S^{i,r},\epsilon)
is a linear presentation for
S^{i}
.
Write
\Lambda^{r}
for
q+z-\delta
.
We thus get that

S^{1,r}+S^{2,r}-\Lambda^{r}\geq 0.

By Lemma
5.3.1
we have that

S^{2,sp}+\epsilon^{*}(S^{2})\geq S^{2,r}+\epsilon.

In particular

S^{2,sp}+\epsilon^{*}(S^{2})-S^{2,r}\geq\epsilon.

Finally we have by Lemma
5.3.2
that

2S^{1,sp}+\widetilde{S^{2}}-z=2(S^{1,sp}+S^{2,sp})+\epsilon^{*}(S^{2})-z

Therefore

\displaystyle 2S^{1,sp}+\widetilde{S^{2}}-z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(S^{1,sp}+S^{2,sp})+\epsilon^{*}(S^{2})-z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(S^{1,sp}+S^{2,sp}-S^{1,r}-S^{2,r}+S^{1,r}+S^{2,r}-\Lambda^{r}+\Lambda^{r})+\epsilon^{*}(S^{2})-z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(S^{1,sp}-S^{1,r})+(S^{2,sp}-S^{2,r})+(S^{2,sp}+\epsilon^{*}(S^{2})-S^{2,r})+2(S^{1,r}+S^{2,r}-\Lambda)+2q+z-2\delta

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2\cdot 0+0+\epsilon+2\cdot 0+2q+z-2\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q+z+\epsilon-2\delta.

where we use Lemma
5.1.1
to deduce that
S^{i,sp}-S^{i,r}\geq 0
.
This proves Equation
5.9.19
as required.
For the case
d(\lambda)\leq 0
we note that
\Lambda
and
S^{2}
get replaced by their flips,
\Lambda^{f}
and
(S^{2})^{f}
.
The linear presentation for
\Lambda^{f}
is just
(q+z-\delta,1-\epsilon)
.
Also
\widetilde{(S^{2})^{f}}=2S^{2,sp}+1-\epsilon^{*}(S^{2})
.
In particular

S^{2,sp}+1-\epsilon^{*}(S^{2})-S^{2,r}\geq 1-\epsilon

where
S^{2,r}
is now the linear presentation with respect to to
1-\epsilon
, and so the rest of the argument now follows through identically.

5.9.6.
Spin(N)
,
N
even

When
N
is even,
\mathcal{N}_{o,l}^{*}
consists of two copies of
\mathcal{P}_{ro}(N)
instead of one.
However the associated Hecke algebras are the same for both copies and so we treat both cases the same.
Otherwise the proof is essentially the same as the
N
odd case and all the relevant lemmas have been proved in sufficient generality for the even case.
We do not repeat the proof, but mention the main technical differences.

•

The partition
\mu(\lambda)
is defined as

m_{\mu(\lambda)}(x)=\begin{cases}2&\mbox{if }x\text{ is odd and }4\mid m_{\lambda^{t}}(x)\\
&\mbox{and }m_{\lambda^{t}}(x)\neq 0\\
1&\mbox{if }x\text{ is odd and }m_{\lambda^{t}}(x)\text{ is}\\
&\mbox{odd}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

•

J=D_{m}\times A_{n-2m-1}\times D_{m}
.

•

d
is even.
\omega
is type II.
K=D_{d^{2}}\times A_{d(d-1)/2-1}\times D_{d^{2}}
.

5.10.
Proof of faithfulness in exceptional types

Let
G^{\vee}
be a complex exceptional group and
x\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(G^{\vee})
.
Let
\theta(x)=(K,\bf E,\psi,\omega)
.
The block
x
takes one of the following three forms:

(1)

L^{\vee}=T
and
\mathcal{E}^{\vee}
is the trivial local system;

(2)

L^{\vee}=G^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\vee}
is a cuspidal local system;

(3)

L^{\vee}\neq G^{\vee},T^{\vee}
.

The first case is covered in
[
CMO21
, Section 4.10, Lemma 3.0.2]
, and the second case in
[
CMO22
]
.
We now cover the third case.
There are very few possibilities for this case: we must have
G^{\vee}
is either of type
E_{6}
or
E_{7}
.
If
G^{\vee}=E_{6}
there are two possibilities for
x
.
In both cases however the relative Weyl group is of type
G_{2}
and
\mathcal{J}_{x}
is the same set for both.
We can treat these two cases simultaneously.
If
G^{\vee}=E_{7}
there is only one choice for
x
and the relative Weyl group is of type
F_{4}
.
We use GAP to exhibit explict pairs
(J,\phi)\in\mathcal{F}_{x}
that realise faithfulness.
We summarise the results in the next paragraph.

We treat the cases
E_{6},E_{7}
simultaneously.
Suppose
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\in(\mathcal{N}_{o}^{\vee})_{x}
.
If
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
appears in Table
1
or Table
2
take the indicated
(J,\phi)
(recall that
\mathrm{WF}(\phi,\mathbb{C})
completely characterises
\phi
).
If
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
does not appear then
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
has the following properly:
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
is special and
d(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
intersects
\mathbb{L}_{c^{\omega}(I_{0,K})}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}})
in a single
F_{\omega}
-stable special orbit which we denote
\mathbb{O}_{0}
.
We take
(J,\phi)
to be such that
J=I_{0,K}
and
\mathrm{WF}(\phi,\mathbb{C})=\mathbb{O}_{0}
.

The reductive quotient
\mathbf{L}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}})
is of type
A_{1}\times D_{4}\times A_{1}
and comes with a Frobenius
F_{\omega}
that identifies the two
A_{1}
factors and folds the
D_{4}
factor.
Thus the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
points
\mathbf{L}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
has type
A_{1}\times\hphantom{}^{2}D_{4}
.
When we say
\mathrm{WF}(\phi,\mathbb{C})=(2)\times(3^{2}1^{2})
we are listing the geometric orbit in
A_{1}\times D_{4}
.
The actual geometric orbit in
\mathbf{L}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}})
is
(2)\times(3^{2}1^{2})\times(2)
.

Remark 5.10.2
.

We remark that there appears to be a mistake in table for the generalised Springer correspondence for
E_{7}
in
[
Spa85
]
(and hence in GAP).
It is listed that

E(E_{7}(a_{4}),\epsilon^{\prime\prime})=\chi_{8,3},\quad E(D_{5}+A_{1},-1)=\chi_{2,3}.

However, it should be

E(E_{7}(a_{4}),\epsilon^{\prime\prime})=\chi_{2,3},\quad E(D_{5}+A_{1},-1)=\chi_{8,3}.

This was verified in
[
Ciu08
, Table 5]
, where the
W(F_{4})
-structure of the simple tempered modules with real infinitesimal character (and as a byproduct the generalized Springer correspondence) was computed for the graded affine Hecke algebra attached to the cuspidal local system on the principal nilpotent orbit in the Levi subgroup
(3A_{1})^{\prime}\subset E_{7}
.

6.
Main results

Our main result is a general formula for the (canonical unramified) wavefront set of an irreducible unipotent representation with real infinitesimal character.

For this we first need an analogue of
[
Oka21
, Theorem 3.3]
.
Let
X
be a unipotent representation of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}({\mathsf{k}})
and let
x=\mathsf{Gcusp}(X)=(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
and
\theta(x)=\mathsf{Acusp}(X)=(K,\mathbf{E},\psi,\omega)
(c.f. Section
4.2
and Section
4.3
]).
Recall from Section
4.1
that we can associate to
X
a module
m_{\bf E}(X)
of the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}({\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}({\mathsf{k}}),\bf E)
.
Our first order of business is to describe the structure of
X^{{\mathbf{U}}_{c}(\mathfrak{o})}
in terms of restrictions of
m_{\bf E}(X)
to subalgebras of
\mathcal{H}
.

Recall from Section
2.4
the definition of the local wavefront sets
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}_{c}(X)
for
c\in\mathcal{B}({\mathbf{G}}^{\omega},{\mathsf{k}})
.

Lemma 6.0.1
.

We have

{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)=\max_{J}\hphantom{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(X)

where
J
ranges over
\omega
-stable subsets of
I
containing
K
.

Proof.

By
[
Ree00
, Lemma 5.1]
, if
X^{{\mathbf{U}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathfrak{o})}\neq 0
then there exists a
\sigma\in\Omega
such that
\sigma(J)\supseteq K
.
But since
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}_{c^{\omega}(J)}
is constant on
\Omega
-orbits the result follows from the analogue of
[
Oka21
, Theorem 1.18]
for canonical unramified wavefront sets (this is essentially the content of
[
Oka21
, Lemma 2.36]
).
∎

Recall from Section
5.8
the definition of
\mathcal{J}_{x},\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{x,J}
,
\mathcal{W}_{x},W_{x},\mathcal{W}_{x,J}
and
W_{x,J}
.
Recall from Section
4.4
the definition of the
\mathcal{W}_{x}
representation
\widetilde{\sigma}_{0}(\theta^{*}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{E}}(V))
.
Write
E
for this representation.

Lemma 6.0.2
.

We have

{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)=\max\{\overline{\mathbb{L}}(J,\mathrm{WF}(\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{x,J}(F),\mathbb{C}))\mid J\in\mathcal{J}_{x},F\in\mathrm{Irr}(\mathcal{W}_{x,J}),\Hom_{\mathcal{W}^{\vee}_{J}}(F,E)\neq 0\}.

Proof.

Let
J\in\mathcal{J}_{x}
.
By
[
MP94
]
the representation
X^{{\mathbf{U}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathfrak{o})}
of
{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathfrak{o})
is a direct sum of representations in
\mathrm{Unip}_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(K)},\bf E}({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
.
Moreover
(\theta^{*}\circ m_{\bf E})_{q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1}
furnishes a bijection between
\mathrm{Unip}_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(K)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),\bf E}({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
and
\mathrm{Irr}(\mathcal{W}_{J})
with inverse
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{x,J}
.
The result then follows from Equation
4.4.3
and Lemma
6.0.1
.
∎

Theorem 6.0.3
.

Let
X=X(s,n,\rho)
be an irreducible unipotent
\mathbf{G}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k})
-representation with
s\in T^{\vee}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Write
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X}=G^{\vee}n\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
. Then
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(\mathsf{AZ}(X))
,
\hphantom{}{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(\mathsf{AZ}(X))
are singletons, and

{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(\mathsf{AZ}(X))

\displaystyle=d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X},1)

\hphantom{ }{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(\mathsf{AZ}(X))

\displaystyle=d(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X}).

Proof.

Arguing as in the proof of
[
CMO22
, Theorem 3.0.2]
, we can reduce to the case when
\mathbf{G}
is simple and adjoint.

By Equation (
2.4.5
) and the identity

(6.0.1)

\mathrm{pr}_{1}(d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X},1))=d(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X})

the formula for
\hphantom{}{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(\mathsf{AZ}(X))
follows from the formula for
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(\mathsf{AZ}(X))
. So it suffices to show that
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(\mathsf{AZ}(X))=d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X},1)
.

By inverting the character identities (
4.1.1
), we see that there is an equality in
R(\mathbf{G}^{\omega}({\mathsf{k}}))

X=Y(s,n,\rho)+\sum_{n\in\partial(G^{\vee}n^{\prime})}m_{s,n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}}Y(s,n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})

for standard modules
Y(s,n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
and
m_{s,n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
x=\mathsf{Gcusp}(X(s,n,\rho))=(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
and
\theta(x)=\mathsf{Acusp}(X(s,n,\rho))=(K,\mathbf{E},\psi,\omega)
.
By Remark
4.3.1

x
lies in
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(G^{\vee})
.

If
J\in\mathcal{J}_{x}
and
\tau\in\mathrm{Unip}_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(K)},\bf E}({\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
, let
\tau|_{q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1}
be the corresponding irreducible
\mathcal{W}_{x,J}
-representation (this was denoted
\theta^{*}(m_{\mathbf{E}}(\tau))_{q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1}
in section
4.4
). By Corollary
4.4.2
,

(6.0.2)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathbf{P}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathfrak{o})}(\tau,Y(s,n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\Hom_{W_{x,J}}(\tau_{q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1},Y_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})|_{W_{x}}),

where
Y_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n,\rho)
is the standard module for the graded affine Hecke algebra
\mathbf{H}(G^{\vee},G^{\vee}_{J^{\vee}},\mathcal{C}^{\vee},\mathcal{F}^{\vee})
. To simplify the notation, let us denote by
\Res_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}(X)
the character of the parahoric restriction of an admissible representation
V
. Then we can rewrite (
6.0.2
) in the following form:

(6.0.3)

\Res_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}(Y(s,n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}))=\Res^{W_{x}}_{W_{x,J}}(Y_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})),

by which we understand that the multiplicity of
\tau
in the left hand side equals the multiplicity of
\tau_{q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}1}
in the right hand side.

By Proposition
3.7.4
, we have the following identity of
W_{x}
-characters:

Y_{\mathbf{H}}(s,n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})|_{W_{x}}=\sum_{\tilde{\rho}\in\widehat{A(n^{\prime})}_{{\mathcal{L}}}}m(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\rho})\mu(n^{\prime},\tilde{\rho})+\sum_{(n^{\prime\prime},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime\prime})}m_{(n^{\prime},\tilde{\rho}),(n^{\prime\prime},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime})}\mu(n^{\prime\prime},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}),

where

m(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\rho})=\dim\Hom_{A(s,n^{\prime})}[\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\rho}|_{A(s,n^{\prime})}],

m_{(n^{\prime},\tilde{\rho}),(n^{\prime\prime},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime})}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}\in\widehat{A(n^{\prime\prime})}_{{\mathcal{L}}}
, and
n^{\prime\prime}
ranges over a set of representatives of
G^{\vee}
-orbits in
\mathcal{N}^{\vee}
such that
n^{\prime}\in\partial{G^{\vee}n^{\prime\prime}}
.

This means that

(6.0.4)

\Res_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}(X)=\sum_{\tilde{\rho}\in\widehat{A(n)}_{{\mathcal{L}}}}m(\rho,\tilde{\rho})\mu(n,\tilde{\rho})|_{W_{x,J}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu^{\prime}\in\Irr(W_{x})\\
n\in\partial\mathbb{O}^{\vee}(\mu^{\prime},\mathbb{C})\end{subarray}}m_{\mu^{\prime}}\mu^{\prime}|_{W_{x,J}},

for some integers
m_{\mu^{\prime}}
. Consequently (see Remark
6.0.4
),

(6.0.5)

\Res_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}(\mathsf{AZ}(X))=\sum_{\tilde{\rho}\in\widehat{A(n)}_{{\mathcal{L}}}}m(\rho,\tilde{\rho})~(\mu(n,\tilde{\rho})\otimes\mathrm{sgn})|_{W_{x,J}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu^{\prime}\in\Irr(W_{x})\\
n\in\partial\mathbb{O}^{\vee}(\mu^{\prime},\mathbb{C})\end{subarray}}m_{\mu^{\prime}}~(\mu^{\prime}\otimes\mathrm{sgn})|_{W_{x,J}},

Notice that our normalization is such that
\mu(n,\tilde{\rho})\otimes\mathrm{sgn}
is precisely the irreducible generalized Springer representation attached to the pair
(n,\tilde{\rho})
i.e.
E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{n},\tilde{\rho})
.
Thus

(6.0.6)

\Res_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}(\mathsf{AZ}(X))=\sum_{\tilde{\rho}\in\widehat{A(n)}_{{\mathcal{L}}}}m(\rho,\tilde{\rho})E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X},\tilde{\rho})|_{W_{x,J}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{O}^{\vee}<\mathbb{O}^{\prime\vee},\\
(\mathbb{O}^{\prime\vee},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime})\in(\mathcal{N}_{o,l})_{x}\end{subarray}}m_{\mathbb{O}^{\prime\vee},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}}E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{1},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime})|_{W_{x,J}}.

Let
F
be an irreducible constituent of
\Res_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}(\mathsf{AZ}(X))
.
Then
\Hom_{W_{x,J}}(F,E(\mathbb{O}^{\prime\vee},\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}))\neq 0
for some
\mathbb{O}^{\prime\vee}\geq\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X}
.
By condition (iii) of faithfulness, we have

\overline{\mathbb{L}}(J,\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J}(F),\mathbb{C}))\leq d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime\vee},1)\leq d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X},1).

Thus by Lemma
6.0.2

{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)\leq d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X},1)
.
To establish equality note that there exists at least one
\tilde{\rho}
such that
m(\rho,\tilde{\rho})\neq 0
.
By conditions (i) and (ii) of faithfulness there exists a
J
and
F\in\mathrm{Irr}(W_{x,J})
such that
\Hom_{W_{x,J}}(F,E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X},\tilde{\rho}))\neq 0
and

\overline{\mathbb{L}}(J,\mathrm{WF}(\mathcal{U}_{x,J}(F),\mathbb{C}))=d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X},1).

But

\Hom_{W_{x,J}}(F,E(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X},\tilde{\rho}))\neq 0\implies\Hom_{W_{x,J}}(F,\Res_{{\mathbf{L}}_{c^{\omega}(J)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}(\mathsf{AZ}(X)))\neq 0

since
m(\rho,\tilde{\rho})\neq 0
and so by Lemma
6.0.2
we get
{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(\mathsf{AZ}(X))=d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X},1)
as desired.

∎

Remark 6.0.4
.

In the above proof, we have used implicitly (see (
6.0.5
)) that the restrictions of
\mathsf{AZ}(X)
are obtained from those of
X
by tensoring with
\mathrm{sgn}
. In the Iwahori-spherical case, we presented the details in
[
CMO21
, Section 2.7]
. In the general case, the argument is identical using Kato’s duality for affine Hecke algebras with unequal parameters
[
Kat93
]
(see also
[
Cha16
, Section 6]
for graded Hecke algebras), and the known compatibility between the parabolic induction (respectively, Jacquet restriction) functors for
{\mathbf{G}}^{\omega}(\mathsf{k})
-representations in a Bernstein component and the parabolic induction (respectively, restriction) functors for the modules of the corresponding Hecke algebras (see
[
BK98
]
).

Now let
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
be a nilpotent orbit. Choose an
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple
(e^{\vee},f^{\vee},h^{\vee})
with
e^{\vee}\in\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
.

Corollary 6.0.5
.

Let
X=X(\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee},n,\rho)
be an irreducible unipotent
\mathbf{G}^{\omega}({\mathsf{k}})
-representation. Then

(6.0.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},1)&\leq_{A}\hphantom{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)\\
d(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})&\leq\hphantom{}^{\bar{{\mathsf{k}}}}\mathrm{WF}(X)\end{split}

Proof.

The second inequality follows from the first by applying
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
to both sides. So it suffices to show that
d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},1)\leq_{A}\hphantom{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)
.

Let
X^{\prime}=\mathsf{AZ}(X)
. Note that
X^{\prime}=X(\frac{1}{2}h^{\vee},n^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
, for some
n^{\prime}
and
\rho^{\prime}
. By
[
CMO21
, Lemma 3.0.9]
, we have
\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X^{\prime}}=G^{\vee}n^{\prime}\leq\mathbb{O}^{\vee}
. In particular,
(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X^{\prime}},1)\leq_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},1)
and so
d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee},1)\leq_{A}d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X^{\prime}},1)
since
d_{A}
is order-reversing. But by Theorem
6.0.3
,
d_{A}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}_{X^{\prime}},1)=\hphantom{}^{K}\mathrm{WF}(X)
. This completes the proof.
∎
